# Changelog

Each milestone as it was built, newest first, with what the tests measured on the development Mac. Releases are marked where they were cut.

## Unreleased

**Milestone 325: a low voice's pitch**
- The pitch finder behind Dialogue Isolation, Wind, Rustle and Bleed and Rebalance (`DialogueIsolation::pitchOf`) read the character voice-over's lowest passages as nothing: at 75 to 85 Hz its fundamental lies under the correlation's 100 Hz band and its strong first formant makes the frame repeat at a third of its period nearly as well as at the period, so the shortest repeat was taken as the pitch, found no harmonics of its own, and the frame read as unvoiced. Now when the shortest repeat fails the harmonic test the longer repeats up to four times it are tried in turn, each asked for more (five harmonics standing over the gaps, one of them among the first three), so a chance repeat in noise gains nothing from the second look. The header's description, which still described the comb method replaced in milestone 319, now describes the finder as it is. Measured on the real recordings of milestone 319: the voice-over's loud frames voiced at the middle sensitivity went from 71 to 84 of 183 (39 % to 46 %), its quiet frames 13 as before, and Dialogue Isolation's cut on its loud blocks from 4.35 to 4.03 dB (worst 11.76 dB as before); the handheld room's numbers are identical; the shortwave has 865 of 1,163 loud frames voiced (864 before) and its isolation takes 2.20 dB off the loud blocks (2.21). A prototype of the finder checked against synthetic frames at 48 and 96 kHz (white, pink and red noise, lone tones at 150 and 440 Hz, a 525 Hz tone in noise, hum at 50 and 60 Hz with six harmonics, clicks in noise, buzzes at 80 to 300 Hz) reads them as before. Of the voice-over's 183 loud frames, 50 still read as nothing periodic (closed and nasal sounds with two harmonics over the floor, which the three-harmonic rule keeps out along with hum) and 46 repeat at 0.3 to 0.6 (a rough voice whose periods are unlike each other, which is what the sensitivity is for). Tried in the prototype and dropped: a period-to-period repeat measure (it raised the quiet frames' false voicing from 7 to 21 %), wider analysis frames (85 ms read 25 % of the loud frames as voiced) and shorter ones (noise read as voice), and trying every longer repeat under the old three-harmonic rule (a 525 Hz tone in noise then read as voiced over half the time). Test: an 80 Hz buzz with its fundamental at a fifth and its third harmonic six times over reads 80 Hz with voicing over 0.8 at 48 and 96 kHz, which the prototype of the finder before read as nothing periodic. Not listened to.

**Milestone 324: Apple silicon only**
- From here on SpectraBlade is built for Apple silicon alone. Releases up to 0.14.0 were universal, but their Intel half was never launched and no Intel Mac was ever tried, so rather than ship code nobody has run there is no Intel build: `Scripts/package.sh` builds arm64 only and stops if the app holds any other architecture, and the README (its badge, the download line and the install notes) says so. The plug-in scan always looks for Apple silicon code now (the check for which kind of Mac the app runs on is gone, since it runs on no other), and its message for a plug-in built only for Intel Macs no longer suggests opening SpectraBlade using Rosetta, which an Apple silicon-only app cannot be. Measured: the package script's build holds arm64 only (`lipo -archs`), and the suite's Intel-only test bundle is still reported as having no code for Apple silicon. Not tried by hand: the scan window's message.

## Released

**Release 0.14.0: previews on every effect, our own stretch, and the mastering and repair suite**
- `SpectraBlade-0.14.0.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 287 to 323 below on top of 0.13.0. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1403 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.14.0, holds both architectures, and carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` with LAME's and Signalsmith's licences. The image's app was not launched; the same code, signed for development, was launched for each milestone. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried. Nothing in this release has been listened to; the parts of each milestone not tried by hand are listed with it below.

**Milestone 323: Level Speech's fastest change**
- Level Speech has a new setting, the fastest change (1 to 30 dB a second): each step's gain is held under a rise at that rate from the gains before and after it (a forward pass, then a backward one, before the ceiling and the smoothing), so a swing between passages takes its time and the gain never goes above what the passages ask, only arrives later. At 30, the largest setting and the default, the gains are exactly what they were (bit for bit in the tests): on the four real recordings of milestone 320 the gains moved at most 5.0 (the voice-over), 10.0 (the shortwave) and 12.6 dB a second (the handheld), all at pauses and changes of speaker, which is where a fader rider moves fast too, so no limit is imposed until one is heard to be needed; the setting is there to lower if the levelling pumps. Sequences and the batch recorded before carry no "speed" value and run as they did. Measured on the test's passages (-12, -30 and -21 LUFS, 4 s each, a second of room noise between): at 6 dB a second the gains rise at most 0.3 dB a step, never over the unlimited gains, and the quiet passage's last half second is 1.2 LU from the target (its 18 dB swing takes 3 s, and the gain starts down early for the louder passage after it); at 30 the gains are the unlimited ones bit for bit. Not listened to.

**Milestone 322: Reduce Reverb measures the room first**
- Reduce Reverb now measures the recording's own reverb time before it works, and a time set longer than that is taken as the measured one. The model predicts the tail as the sound 80 ms before, decayed as the set time says; a time set longer than the room's expects a slower fall, so anything that stops faster, a dry voice's every syllable included, read as a tail and was cut (milestone 320 found the dry voice-over's loud blocks down 1.17 dB at 1.5 s, the worst 7.18). The measurement (Dereverb::measure): in four bands from 250 Hz to 4 kHz, every frame that stood 20 dB over the band's floor (its quietest tenth) 80 ms earlier is compared with that earlier frame, and the median fall over those 80 ms is read as the time 60 dB would take. The dialog says so, and the result's note names the time used when it differs from the setting; the setting itself, and a sequence's or batch's "time" value, are unchanged.
- Measured: the test's synthetic rooms of 0.5, 1 and 2 s (velvet noise, as loud as the sound) measure 0.51, 1.01 and 1.74 s, the dry bursts under 0.15 (0.02 in the numpy prototype; taken as the 0.1 s minimum: with 1.5 s set they move under 0.1 dB); with 2 s set on the 1 s room the tail is lowered 9.23 dB against 9.13 at 1 s (1.68 at 0.5 s); the dry studio voice-over measures 0.34 s, the handheld room 0.58, the shortwave 0.57. On the voice-over at a 1.5 s setting the loud blocks now move -0.06 dB (was -1.17), the worst -0.25 (was -7.18), none over 1 dB (was 12 of 40); the handheld's tail after speech still falls from 2.73 dB under the speech to 2.92 (3.81 uncapped at 1.5 s, 2.86 at 0.5) with its loud blocks moving -0.07 dB (was -0.37, the worst -0.29 against -1.58); the shortwave's static between words falls 4.42 dB (4.05 uncapped). Nothing listened to.

**Milestone 321: The band split in linear phase**
- The Multiband Compressor, Multiband Stereo Width, Exciter, Transient Shaper and De-esser can now split the sound in linear phase. The minimum-phase split (the Linkwitz-Riley crossovers, no delay) stays the default; the new switch (Band split: Linear phase, in each dialog, the Multiband Compressor's panel, and the live effects as a Linear Phase parameter) splits with symmetric impulses instead: the lows from a low-pass impulse at the low crossover and the highs from a high-pass one at the high crossover, each with the crossovers' own level curve (the digital Linkwitz-Riley's, so the bands keep the same levels either way), built by the linear-phase EQ's engine (Multiband::Split, on Equalizer::LinearPhase); the middle is what is left of the sound delayed to match. So the three bands add back to the sound exactly as it was, its peaks held. The cost is the linear-phase EQ's delay, 8,703 frames (181 ms at 48 kHz), which the live effects report (the Exciter's 40 frames on top) and the offline effects take off, so the result lands where the audio was; a channel the width passes (a mono sound, the channels past the first pair) is delayed to stay in step, bit for bit.
- Measured on full-scale square waves (100 Hz on one channel, 700 Hz on the other): through the minimum-phase split with every band left alone the peak rises 5.36 dB; through the linear-phase split the sound comes back within 1.2e-7 of itself (compressor at 1:1, width at 100 %; the de-esser taking nothing bit for bit). A burst of hits peaking at 0.954 comes out of the idle Transient Shaper at 1.078 in minimum phase and at 0.954 in linear phase (within 6e-8). The width's bands in linear phase: the 60 Hz side at -31.4 dB with the low band at 0 % (-31.4 in minimum phase too: the crossover's skirt), the 500 Hz side moved 0.04 dB, the 8 kHz side +6.01 dB at 200 %, the mid moved under 1e-8 dB. Each live effect in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames gives the offline samples bit for bit, late by the frames it reports, and reports 0 again once the switch is off; state, presets and sequence steps keep the switch (a step without it, as every recorded sequence so far, is minimum phase).
- On the real recordings of milestone 320 (the 1962 record transfer, limited to 0.01 dBTP; the clipped handheld room recording, 2.29 dBTP; the studio voice-over, 0.02 dBTP), with every band left alone: the minimum-phase split puts out 5.93 / 10.58 / 1.28 dBTP from the Transient Shaper, 6.46 / 9.22 from the width (the two stereo ones), 6.01 / 10.00 / 1.06 from the compressor at 1:1 and 1.59 / 4.93 / 0.17 from the de-esser taking nothing; the linear-phase split gives each back at its own true peak (0.01 / 2.29 / 0.02 dBTP), the samples equal to the original to -149 dB or better (the de-esser's to -188). The Exciter at 0 dB of drive still passes each band through its oversampler and curve, so it is not idle: 5.71 / 11.74 / 1.05 dBTP in minimum phase, 0.45 / 6.69 / -0.09 in linear phase. With the Transient Shaper's attacks at +6 dB in every band, the record's true peak reaches 8.74 dBTP in minimum phase and 5.76 in linear phase, its crest factor 8.53 dB against 6.02 (from 5.64): most of the minimum-phase rise was the phase turn, not the attacks; the handheld 14.41 against 9.79 dBTP, the voice-over 6.76 against 5.83. The de-esser at a -40 dB threshold takes the same either way (the voice-over's loud blocks -0.097 dB in both, the worst block -1.48 and -1.47), with true peaks of 0.17 and 0.03 dBTP.
- Not listened to. Not tried: the live effects in the rack with the switch turned while playing (the latency changes, as the Maximizer's does with its character).

**Milestone 320: Mastering tools tried on real recordings**
- The mastering jobs, Reduce Reverb and Level Speech were run on the same four recordings as milestone 319 (the studio voice-over at 96 kHz, the 1962 record transfer, the handheld recording of a room full of people, the shortwave numbers station), compared the same way (outputs lined up with their originals, 50 ms block levels, the loudest 30 % against the quietest 30 %), with each output's true peak, its level change per third of an octave and its loudness measured as well. Two things were wrong and are fixed, one is documented rather than fixed, and the rest held up or is noted below. Nothing has been listened to.
- The Exciter changed a sound at 0 dB of drive: its curves bent at full scale, so a hot master was already into them with the drive at nothing (the record transfer's loud blocks came down 1.41 dB, 476 of 541 by over 1 dB, and the high band driven 12 dB took 2 dB off everything above 8 kHz). The curves now sit 12 dB past full scale (Exciter::headroomDb): at 0 dB of drive a full-scale peak is 0.18 dB down and the record's loud blocks move 0.12 dB (none by over 1 dB), the handheld's 0.06, the shortwave's 0.02, the voice-over's 0.14. The factory presets' drives are 12 dB higher, so they sound as they did. Driven 24 dB (12 past the headroom) on the record, the driven band comes down (the highs 1 to 2.3 dB above 2.5 kHz with Tube, the lows 3 to 4 dB under 100 Hz with Tape) and no rise above it is measurable on these recordings: the harmonics the curves add (25 dB under the fundamental at that drive) lie under what the recordings already have there. The voice-over's room tone between words (-59 dBFS, three quarters of it under 60 Hz) still moves by over 1 dB in 19 of 39 quiet blocks at 0 dB of drive, 15 of them from the 5 Hz offset filter after the curve alone.
- Match Loudness stopped 0.49 LU short on the voice-over (-16.49 LUFS for -16, with 10.84 dB of gain into the limiter): each pass added the whole shortfall, but the limiter took more off with every dB added, and four passes were not enough. The gain now rises by the shortfall divided by what the last raise gained (never taken under a quarter), up to eight passes: the voice-over reaches -15.996 LUFS with 13.20 dB of gain; the record (-16.00), the handheld (-16.05) and the shortwave (-16.0004) were already within the 0.05 LU.
- The band split behind the Multiband Compressor, Multiband Stereo Width, Exciter, Transient Shaper and De-esser keeps every frequency's level but turns the phase, and a phase turn moves peaks: on the record transfer, limited to 0.01 dBTP, the Transient Shaper with every setting at zero puts out 5.93 dBTP, the Exciter at 0 dB 5.71, the width at 100 % 6.46; on the clipped handheld recording (2.29 dBTP in, flat-topped) 10.58 and 11.74; the De-esser's one split at 6 kHz 0.57 dBTP on the record and 3.81 on the handheld; the voice-over 0.02 to 1.28; the shortwave -2.94 to -2.78. Not changed (a linear-phase split would hold the peaks and add latency); the split's description and the five dialogs now say so, and that a ceiling is set by a limiter after these effects, not before.
- Reduce Reverb, tried for the first time on real material: on the handheld room recording at 12 dB the level after speech stops (the three blocks after a loud block followed by quiet ones, relative to the loud one) falls from 2.73 dB under to 2.86 with a 0.5 s reverb time and to 3.81 with 1.5 s, the loud blocks moving 0.05 and 0.37 dB (the worst 1.58); on the dry voice-over set to 1.5 s the loud blocks come down 1.17 dB, the worst 7.18, 12 of 40 by over 1 dB, so a reverb time longer than the room's costs direct speech; on the shortwave it lowers the static between words by 4.05 dB at 1.5 s.
- Level Speech, also tried for the first time: the voice-over goes from -22.20 to -20.80 LUFS for a target of -20 with its loudness range from 5.89 to 2.95 LU and gains from -1.0 to +4.1 dB that move at most 5.0 dB in a second; the shortwave voice's range from 9.43 to 2.05 LU with gains from -8.5 to +12.0 dB, moving up to 10.0 dB in a second as the signal fades; the handheld's from 7.41 to 0.49 LU with gains from -11.9 to +6.6 dB, moving up to 12.6 dB in a second (4.1 at the 95th percentile); the record, being music, is simply brought down 12 dB. Whether the faster movements are heard as pumping is not known.
- Breath Control (left open in milestone 319) read the shortwave's static between words as 108 breaths and lowered the quiet blocks 2.85 dB on average, 143 of 540 by over 1 dB: the floor it measured against was the least 10 ms block within three seconds, which on a noisy floor is one of the floor's own dips (4 to 10 dB under its typical block), so most of the static stood the 6 dB over it. The floor is now what the quietest twentieth of those blocks reach (digital silence not counted), a run has to stand 6 dB over its surroundings (the non-speech blocks within half a second either side that aren't candidates themselves), and a breath is never lowered further than to its surroundings, so no hole is carved in the noise round it. Measured: 41 found on the shortwave, the quiet blocks moved 0.09 dB on average and 8 by over 1 dB; the voice-over and the handheld have 0 before and after; the tests' two breaths are found and lowered as before.
- Held up: the parametric EQ's minimum-phase and linear-phase runs agree within 0.04 dB per block on all four recordings, and a side band leaves the mono sum bit for bit (minimum phase) or within 1e-6 dB (linear); the Dynamic EQ's band at 3 kHz (over, -6 dB) takes 2.18 dB off that band in the record's loud blocks and 0.66 in its quiet ones (the voice-over 4.92 and 0.88), 0.03 dB off 300 to 600 Hz; the Graphic EQ's tilt reads -6.2 dB at 50 Hz to +5.3 at 16 kHz on the record, with a 2.4 dB dip at 12.8 kHz at 44.1 kHz where the top band sits near Nyquist (none at 96 kHz); the Compressor at 4:1 brings the record's range from 3.51 to 2.02 LU; the Limiter and the Maximizer's Transparent and Balanced characters hold -1.00 dBTP on all four with 6 dB in, the Loud character with 50 % emphasis reaches -0.90 to -0.99; the width at 0 / 100 / 200 % moves the record's side 13.4 dB down in the lows and 5.2 up in the highs with the mono sum within 0.12 dB per block; the Transient Shaper's attack at +6 dB takes the record's crest from 5.64 to 8.53 dB (its sustain at -6 moves dense music 0.09 dB, the voice-over 0.99); Dither leaves no sample off the 16-bit grid, adds -96.3 dBFS of noise plain (TPDF plus quantisation), -88.6 light with 29 dB less of it under 2 kHz and 10 dB more above 10 kHz than plain, -83.3 strong (41 dB less and 16 dB more); Codec Preview's MP3 at 128 kbps and AAC at 96 round trips line up at lag 0 on all four within 0.1 dB per block, the MP3 taking 1.8 dB off the record's 16 kHz third and the AAC 3.9; Match EQ towards pink brings the record's profile from 4.63 dB off to 1.83 and the voice-over's from 5.66 to 1.49, lifting the record's 16 kHz by the full 12 dB allowed (there is only hiss there); the Tonal Balance panel's targets put the record 52 % within Orchestral and 45 % within Speech, the voice-over 53 % within Speech, the handheld under 30 % and the shortwave under 10 % within any; Hum Removal finds nothing to remove (the recordings have no hum: the 60 Hz bins sit level with their neighbours) and takes 0.5 to 1.2 dB off those bins and 0.02 dB off 300 Hz to 3 kHz; De-clip rebuilds 92 runs on the handheld (36 longer than 5 ms left), 6 on the record, 1 on the voice-over; Centre Channel takes 14.5 dB off the record (its side is 34 dB under its mid: nothing but centre) and leaves it within 0.1 dB when keeping the centre; Phase Correction reads the record's channels 0.000006 frames apart at a correlation of 0.9995. The De-esser only had something to act on at a threshold of -40 dB on the voice-over (2 loud blocks changed by over 1 dB, 0.6 dB off 4 kHz to 4.7 off 8 kHz); the other three recordings never reach it above 6 kHz.
- Not resolved from milestone 319: the handheld room recording (Adaptive Noise Reduction and Dialogue Isolation lower everything, since nothing there is ever quiet or on one pitch) is left as it is, and both dialogs now say what they need; the character voice-over's pitch (a median of 189 Hz, 86 to 381 Hz between its 10th and 90th percentiles) is within range but weakly periodic (its plain autocorrelation peaks at a median of 0.45), so 71 of its 183 loud frames read as voiced at a sensitivity of 50 and 100 at 100, which is what the sensitivity is for.

**Milestone 319: Repair tools tried on real recordings**
- The repair jobs were run on four real recordings for the first time (a studio voice-over at 96 kHz, a 1962 record transfer, a handheld stereo recording of a room full of people, and a shortwave numbers-station recording), each output lined up with its original and compared level by level over 50 ms blocks (the loudest 30 % of blocks against the quietest 30 %). Four things were wrong and are fixed; the rest held up or is noted below.
- Voicing (behind Dialogue Isolation, the voice gating in Wind, Rustle and Bleed, and the voice of Rebalance) read every real voice as unvoiced: it looked for harmonics standing over the gaps between them in the power spectrum, which the synthetic buzzes of the tests have and real voices, in 4,096-point frames, do not clearly enough (the voice-over's loud frames had a median voicing of 0, with 21 of 183 over the threshold; the shortwave voice 70 of 1,163). It now reads how well the frame repeats at the pitch's period (the normalised autocorrelation of the frame's 100 Hz to 1.5 kHz, its window's taper taken out, past the first trough, the shortest period within 15 % of the best), refuses a lone tone (which repeats as well at half the period) and anything without three harmonics each over both the gaps beside them, and the threshold at sensitivity 50 is 0.6 (0.75 at 0, 0.45 at 100). Dialogue Isolation holds a voice for 80 ms after the voicing drops within 0.15 of the threshold, at the last pitch read, and takes a fricative as unvoiced highs over the background that outweigh the lows. Measured: the voice-over's loud frames 71 of 183 voiced (the quiet ones 13 of 182), the shortwave voice 864 of 1,163 (95 of 1,162); Dialogue Isolation at 12 dB lowered the voice-over's loud blocks by 9.64 dB before and 4.35 dB now (the quiet ones 11.87 and 11.34), the shortwave voice's loud blocks by 10.71 before and 2.21 now (the quiet ones 11.81 and 9.23). The test scenes still read as they should: no frame of the noise-and-tone background and none of the rumble gust is voiced; the tests' buzzes read at 150 Hz with voicing 0.93 under their background.
- Adaptive Noise Reduction took a sustained word for the noise rising: its broad-rise rule (most bins up twice over the estimate) was met by any loud vowel that outlasted an eighth of the tracking span, and the estimate jumped to the voice. A rise now has to hold through the whole of the previous sub-window as well. Measured on the voice-over at 12 dB: the loud blocks lowered by 2.72 dB before and 0.99 now (the worst 9.39 and 4.22), the quiet ones by 9.41 and 9.40; the shortwave voice's loud blocks by 3.89 before and 2.86 now, its static by 11.11 and 11.01.
- Wow and Flutter summed each frame's speed change into the curve, so on real music, where notes change and peaks match the wrong partial, the errors walked: the record's curve read 0.603 to 2.166 (a 2,213 cent span) and the correction moved its loud blocks by 3.18 dB on average. It now follows each partial as a track and reads its deviation against its own track's median over 3 s, takes a frame's speed only where four fifths of the weight agrees within 0.3 % and three or more of the agreeing notes have held a second (a voice alone, whose harmonics also move together, seldom does), reads nothing past 3 %, and low-passes the curve with a fourth-order Butterworth run forwards and back instead of a moving average (the 1 % wow at 2 Hz of the test comes out within 0.038 %: 0.025 before with the moving average at the old 10 Hz default, 0.167 with it at 4 Hz). The default fastest drift is 4 Hz (wow; the Wow and flutter preset keeps 10). Measured: the record's curve reads 0.991 to 1.003 (a 21 cent span) and its loud blocks move 0.003 dB on average; the handheld recording (speech, which the dialog says little can be read from) reads a 53 cent span, 2,680 before.
- Mouth De-click and De-crackle took a voice's own pulses for clicks: the error's spread was its median, which a voiced sound's glottal pulses stand far over, so in the voice-over 252 of the loudest quarter's 1,626 ms were rewritten (21 of the quietest), 203 clicks in 6.5 s, and the loudest blocks lost up to 2.81 dB. For a voice the spread is now the larger of that and the error's 95th percentile over 1.96 (the same for a Gaussian error), through a new voice flag on the click repair settings that the two mouth jobs set. Measured: 133 of the loudest quarter's ms rewritten (19 of the quietest), 136 clicks, the loudest blocks lose at most 0.89 dB and 0.10 on average. Click and Pop Repair itself is unchanged (48 clicks and 29 skipped on the record, as before).
- Held up: Click and Pop Repair, De-crackle and De-plosive change the loud blocks of every recording by under 0.2 dB on average; Spectral Recovery finds no cutoff on the record or the handheld recording (both roll off smoothly, measured in third octaves) and does nothing; Azimuth reads the record's two channels within 0.002 frames of each other and leaves them; Rebalance now finds the record's singers (Voice -40 dB lowers its loud blocks by 3.21 dB on average and 9.48 at most, 1.05 and 4.41 before; Voice +6 dB raises them 2.44). The Azimuth dialog now says that on a recording made in stereo the readings follow whatever is loudest, which is not azimuth (the handheld recording read offsets from -3.5 to +5.3 frames).
- Not resolved, and noted: on the handheld recording (continuous talk and room noise) Adaptive Noise Reduction lowers everything by about 11 dB and Dialogue Isolation nearly everything (15 of 1,163 loud frames voiced), since nothing there is quiet for the span and the voices are far from the microphone; Breath Control takes the shortwave recording's gaps of static for 108 breaths; the voice-over (a character voice) still has only 39 % of its loud frames voiced, so Dialogue Isolation lowers it by 4.35 dB on average. Nothing was listened to; every number above is measured. The whole suite passed (1397 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.

**Milestone 318: Codec Preview**
- Effects > Level and Loudness > Codec Preview...: the selection as it will come back from an MP3 or an AAC file at a bit rate (the rates Export as MP3 or AAC offers, 96 to 320 kbps): written through the real encoder to a temporary file, read back, lined up with the original (the encoder's delay found by cross-correlation over the first second), brought back to the audio's rate where the file's differs, trimmed to the range and put in its place, so what plays in the preview is the codec's own output. Keep puts that output in the file, for an A/B or to work on what a listener will hear. Mono or stereo only, as the encoders are. Four factory presets.
- Measured by the tests (three seconds of a stereo tone with harmonics and a little noise): MP3 at 192 kbps comes back at 195 kbps on disk, lined up with no residual delay, its level within 0.007 dB and the error against the original 31.7 dB under it; AAC asked for 192 kbps comes back at 133 kbps on disk (the system encoder aims lower than asked), lined up, within 0.03 dB and 33.0 dB under; the audio before the range is untouched bit for bit; a bit rate the formats don't offer is refused; it can be cancelled. The whole suite passed (1397 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- This was the last of the mastering and repair jobs on the roadmap; all twenty-two are in (milestones 297 to 318).

**Milestone 317: Azimuth**
- Effects > Stereo and Channels > Azimuth...: the time offset between the two channels of a tape transfer (a head out of line with the tracks) found as it drifts and taken out, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. Automatic Phase Correction finds one offset for a whole range; a transfer's offset wanders as the tape moves across the head, so here it is measured over windows of two seconds, a second apart, each with phase correction's measure (the cross-correlation's peak, between frames, within the most looked for, 0.5 to 50 ms), with windows that give no clear reading (a correlation under 0.1, or silence) filled from their neighbours; the readings are smoothed over three (but the ends) and joined by straight lines, which run on past the first and the last reading. The channels are then moved by half the offset each, the late one earlier and the other later, by a windowed-sinc fractional delay (16 taps, Hann) whose delay follows the line sample by sample, scaled by the amount. Mono audio and a range under two seconds are left. Three factory presets.
- Measured by the tests (ten seconds of noise in both channels, the right late by an offset drifting from 0.2 to 1.8 frames): the drift is read as 0.34 frames at the first window and 1.66 at the last; corrected, the channels line up within 0.025 frames at 0.5 s, 0.0001 at 4.5 s and 0.024 at 8.5 s, where they were 1.00 and 1.66 frames apart; mono audio and a range under two seconds give nothing; it can be cancelled. The whole suite passed (1396 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real transfers; an offset that jumps (a splice) is smoothed across a second rather than followed at once.

**Milestone 316: Dialogue Contour**
- Effects > Time and Pitch > Dialogue Contour...: the pitch of a phrase's ending reshaped, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. The pitch of the range is tracked as pitch correction tracks it (every 5 ms); the ending is the last stretch of pitched frames before the selection's end, as long as asked for (0.1 to 1.5 s, shorter where the phrase is; a gap of over 100 ms ends it), and over it the pitch is moved by a ramp from nothing at the ending's start to the full move at its end (-12 to +12 semitones), by PSOLA, so the voice keeps its character. The curve (-1 to 1) bends the ramp, most of the move early or late. Frames without a pitch, and everything before the ending, are left as they are; a selection with no pitched sound says so. Pitch correction gains a public shiftBy (any per-frame shift over a track), which this uses. Four factory presets (Land the line, Firm ending, Lift, Question).
- Measured by the tests (a buzz at 200 Hz for 1.2 s): a fall of 4 semitones over the last 0.4 s leaves the phrase 0.05 cents from where it was at 0.5 s, has it 190 cents down at 1.0 s and 362 cents down at 1.17 s; a rise of 3 puts 1.17 s 272 cents up; the shifts are 0 before the ending; a move past 12 semitones and an empty range are refused; it can be cancelled. The whole suite passed (1395 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real speech, where the tracker's pitch can drop out on breathy endings and the ramp then stops short.

**Milestone 315: Ambience Match**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Capture Room Tone and Ambience Match...: the room tone of one part of a recording laid under another, so an edit, a replaced line or a silence between takes has the same background as the rest. The room tone is learned from a stretch of it alone (as a noise print is: the average power of each bin of 4,096-point frames, the phases thrown away). Laying it under a range makes new tone from that print, frames of random phase at the print's magnitudes overlapped and added with square-root Hann windows (steady, never repeating), scaled by the level (-24 to +12 dB) and added to the range, eased in and out over a frame at its edges. Fill only the quiet adds tone only where the range's own power (smoothed over the bins round each) falls under the print's, by the shortfall, so a background is evened out rather than doubled; Add regardless adds it all. The tone is the same for the same print and range, so applying twice gives the same result. Previewed on the selection; three factory presets.
- Measured by the tests (six seconds of pink-ish room tone at about -50 dB, then silence; the print from the first two seconds): laid under the silence, the new tone's profile is within 1.83 dB of the room's shape from 100 Hz to 10 kHz (the reading itself wanders by a decibel at the low end) and its level within 0.34 dB; it is eased in at the edge and the same twice over; over the room itself, filling only the quiet moves the level 0.81 dB and filling regardless adds 2.95 dB; a print at another rate, a level past 12 dB, an empty range and a print under a frame are refused. The whole suite passed (1394 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real room tone; a tone with a hum or a tonal element comes out with its tones' phases scrambled, as a steady wash rather than the hum itself.

**Milestone 314: Rebalance**
- Effects > Spectral > Rebalance...: the voice, the bass, the drums and the rest of a mix each turned up or down (-40 to +12 dB), previewed on the selection and applied in the background. The mix is analysed in 4,096-point frames at 75 % overlap, and each bin of each frame is shared among the four stems by soft masks that add up to one, so with every stem at 0 dB the mix comes back as it was. Drums are what is percussive: a bin whose magnitude in its own channel stands out across frequency (the median over 17 bins) more than across time (the median over 17 frames), the two medians' squares sharing it. Of the harmonic share, the bass is the part below the bass corner (60 to 300 Hz, a soft edge over half an octave); the voice is the part of the mid (what the channels share) within a quarter of the pitch of each harmonic in a frame the mix reads as voiced (as Dialogue Isolation judges it), from 80 Hz to 4 kHz, plus the mid's highs from 4 to 8 kHz in such a frame; and the rest is what is left, the sides included. The frames are kept in a ring of 17, so a long file costs no more memory than a short one. The stems are a reading of the mix, not a separation: a steady chord in the mid reads as voice, a kick's body as bass, a plucked bass as partly drums. Four factory presets (Voice up, Voice down, Drums down, Bass and drums up).
- Measured by the tests (a stereo mix: a buzz at 180 Hz in the middle from 1 to 3 s, a 55 Hz bass in the middle, 20 ms noise hits every 250 ms, and a pad of two tones on the sides only): every stem at 0 dB gives the mix back within 1.2e-7; the voice at -24 dB takes the buzz's harmonics down 13.3 dB and leaves the bass within 0.001 dB, the pad within 0.67 dB and the hits within 0.34 dB; the bass at -24 takes the 55 Hz down 23.7 dB and leaves the buzz within 0.001 dB; the drums at -24 take the hits down 22.5 dB and leave the bass within 0.02 dB; the rest at -24 takes the pad down 17.0 dB and leaves the buzz within 1.0 dB; a gain past 12 dB and a range under a window are refused; it can be cancelled. The whole suite passed (1393 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real mixes, where instruments share bins and the stems blur into one another; the voice stem keeps only the mid, so a voice panned to one side is read as the rest.

**Milestone 313: Spectral Recovery**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Spectral Recovery...: the highs missing from band-limited audio (a phone line, a low-rate MP3, an old transfer) rebuilt from the band that is there, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. The cutoff is found from the long-term spectrum (the highest frequency under which the spectrum holds within 30 dB of its level an octave lower, that level itself within 60 dB of the spectrum's mean, scanning down from the top) or given (1 to 16 kHz). In 4,096-point frames at 75 % overlap, the bins of the octave below the cutoff are copied up an octave onto the bins above it (a partial's harmonics where they would lie), their phases advanced for the doubled frequency so the copy stays coherent from frame to frame, scaled to continue the spectrum's slope just below the cutoff in each frame and then by the level (-24 to +6 dB), and copied again an octave higher from the first copy, up to the top asked for (2 to 20 kHz, under half the rate). Bins already holding more keep what they have; nothing below the cutoff changes. Rebuilt highs are a likeness, not the original. Four factory presets (Found cutoff natural and brighter, Phone line, Low-rate MP3).
- Measured by the tests (a buzz at 110 Hz with harmonics falling 3 dB an octave to 20 kHz, cut off at 6 kHz with a brick wall): the cutoff is found at 6,300 Hz, and none in the full audio; from 7 to 11 kHz the cut audio was 85 dB under the original, rebuilt it is within 1.24 dB of the original's profile; the band below the cutoff moves 0.0001 dB; a top past half the rate and a range under a window are refused; it can be cancelled. The whole suite passed (1392 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real band-limited recordings; noise-like highs (cymbals, breath) are rebuilt from the harmonic band below and sound tonal where the original was noise.

**Milestone 312: Wow and Flutter**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Wow and Flutter...: the pitch drift of a tape or a record found and taken out, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. The drift is read from the partials: in 4,096-point frames every 1,024, the spectral peaks (bins standing over their neighbours and over -60 dB, each placed between bins by a parabola) are matched to the nearest peak of the frame before within 3 %, and the frame's speed change is the power-weighted median of their ratios, so what every partial does together counts (the speed) and what one voice does on its own (vibrato, a slide) does not. The changes add up into a speed curve, smoothed to what moves no faster than the fastest drift asked for (0.5 to 20 Hz) and scaled to a mean of 1, so the length is kept. The audio is then played back at the curve's inverse (a varispeed with four-point interpolation), so a moment that ran fast is slowed back, pitch and time together, as the machine's wobble changed them; the amount (0 to 100 %) scales how much is taken out. Works on music with sustained notes; little can be read from speech or drums alone. Three factory presets (Wow only, Wow and flutter, Half way).
- Measured by the tests (three tones with harmonics played with 1 % of wow at 2 Hz): the speed curve reads the wow as 1.004 %; corrected, a partial that wobbled 0.93 % holds within 0.025 %; steady tones are moved 0.0000004 %; with one tone wavering 2 % at 6 Hz on its own, the others are moved 0.0006 %; a fastest drift past 20 Hz and a range under half a second are refused; it can be cancelled. The whole suite passed (1391 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real transfers; drift faster than the frame rate can follow (over about 20 Hz) and a passage where every partial changes together for musical reasons (a whole ensemble sliding) are read as the machine's.

**Milestone 311: Wind, Rustle and Bleed**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Wind, Rustle and Bleed...: three jobs on a speech recording that share one look at the audio, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. The level is measured in 10 ms blocks, in the whole, in the lows (below the wind corner, 60 to 400 Hz) and in the highs (above 1 kHz); every 20 ms a 4,096-point frame is judged for voicing as Dialogue Isolation judges it. Wind: the lows lowered while they gust, that is while they stand over their typical level (the lows' 30th percentile over five seconds either side) by more than 9 dB in an unvoiced block, eased over 50 ms. Rustle: the highs lowered during a burst of them (over their floor, the quietest block within three seconds, by more than 9 dB) that is unvoiced, lasts 20 to 400 ms and is more than 150 ms from any voiced block (a fricative sits next to a vowel; rustle does not), eased over 10 ms. Bleed: everything lowered between the words, in unvoiced blocks more than the threshold (6 to 40 dB) under the speech level (the loudest block within a second) and more than 100 ms from any voiced block, eased over 30 ms. Each reduction (0 to 40 dB) at 0 leaves that job out. Four factory presets (All three, Wind only, Rustle only, Bleed only).
- Dialogue Isolation's pitch finder, shared here, now measures the gaps between a candidate's harmonics as the median of the gaps' own densities over the harmonics' own span: a spectrum falling smoothly with frequency (a gust of wind) read as voiced against the quiet far above it, and one tone in a gap counted against every harmonic. Its own checks pass as before (the buzz's voicing 0.9999, its harmonics kept within 0.01 dB).
- Measured by the tests (twelve seconds: a 160 Hz buzz as words, a two-second gust of rumble falling 12 dB an octave above 40 Hz, a 150 ms burst of bright noise far from the words, and a 700 Hz tone and noise 29 dB under the words throughout; 12 dB each): the gust comes down 11.4 dB while the bleed between words is left within 0.03 dB; the rustle comes down 11.5 dB while the gust is left within 0.01 dB; the bleed between two words comes down 12.0 dB; the words' first six harmonics move at most 0.22 dB under each job; it can be cancelled; a corner under 60 Hz and a range under a tenth of a second are refused. The whole suite passed (1390 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real recordings; bleed that is itself voiced (another voice) is kept as speech, and a whispered word far from voiced speech can read as rustle.

**Milestone 310: Breath Control**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Breath Control...: the breaths between words found and lowered. The level is measured in 10 ms blocks; the speech level around a block is the loudest block within a second either side, the floor the quietest within three seconds either side. A block is a breath candidate when it lies more than the threshold (6 to 40 dB) under the speech level and more than 6 dB over the floor, and sounds like air rather than voice (its zero-crossing rate over 1,200 a second, where voiced speech sits well under). A run of candidates from 80 to 800 ms long is a breath, lowered by the reduction (0 to 40 dB), eased in and out over 20 ms. Words, pauses that are only room noise, and anything voiced are left alone; a selection with no breaths says so. A sequence step (breath) and three factory presets (Standard, Gentle, Nearly gone).
- Measured by the tests (six seconds of a 160 Hz buzz as words, with two 250 ms breaths of bright noise 18 dB under them, over room noise 42 dB under): both breaths are found where they are (within 30 ms) and nothing else; each is lowered 12.0 dB of the 12; a word moves 0 dB and the room noise between words is left bit for bit; a range under a tenth of a second gives nothing. The whole suite passed (1389 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real speech; a breath that runs straight into a word with no dip is found only up to where the word begins, and a whispered word can read as a breath.

**Milestone 309: Mouth De-click, De-crackle and De-plosive**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Mouth De-click... and De-crackle...: Click and Pop Repair's detection and rebuilding (an autoregressive model's prediction misses, the damaged samples re-made from the audio either side) with settings for each job, remembered on their own: mouth clicks are faint and up to 8 ms long (a threshold of 10 dB over the background, the longest click 8 ms), crackle fainter still and under a millisecond (8 dB, 0.8 ms). Each previews on the selection and repairs in the background, with three factory presets.
- De-plosive...: the thumps a p or a b puts into a microphone found and lowered. The sound is split at the corner (60 to 400 Hz; the multiband split, 24 dB an octave). The low band's envelope (2 ms up, 40 ms down) is compared with its own typical level (its slow average over the last second): where it stands over that by more than the threshold (3 to 30 dB) while the band above does not rise with it, a plosive is on, and the low band is lowered by the reduction (0 to 40 dB) for as long as it lasts, at most 250 ms, eased in and out over 5 ms. The typical levels follow slowly while a plosive is on, so a sound that starts loud settles as typical rather than counting as a plosive for good. The gain is found 10 ms ahead of the audio it is applied to, so it is down when the thump's first cycle arrives; applied to a file the result lands where the audio was. A sequence step (deplosive) and three factory presets.
- Measured by the tests (a voice-like buzz at 180 Hz over a steady 80 Hz hum, with two 60 ms thumps of 50 Hz): with an 18 dB reduction the thumps come down 15.5 and 13.4 dB; the voice's first six harmonics and the hum away from the thumps move under 0.00000003 dB; a voice without thumps passes within 0.00000001 dB; a corner under 60 Hz and an empty range are refused. The whole suite passed (1388 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real speech; the mouth de-click and de-crackle settings are starting points, not measured against recordings of lip clicks or crackle.

**Milestone 308: Adaptive Noise Reduction**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Adaptive Noise Reduction...: noise reduced with no noise print, the noise followed as it changes, in the selection or the whole file, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. Frames of 4,096 points at 75 % overlap, as the noise reduction; each bin's noise is tracked from the least its smoothed power has been over the tracking span (0.5 to 5 s; what is always there, even under speech), kept in four sliding sub-windows, and taken as the mean of the frames within 6 dB of that least (the frames of noise alone), so it is not the minimum's underestimate. Noise that rises is followed within a sub-window, but only where the rise is broad (most bins from 100 Hz to 8 kHz rising together), since speech starting raises its own bins alone; noise that falls is followed as the old sub-windows roll off. Only frames wholly within the audio there is are tracked, and the frame before the range is read for the tracking to settle on. Each frame then gets the noise reduction's Wiener gain per bin (the decision-directed estimate), never below the reduction (0 to 40 dB), with the threshold (0 to 12 dB) over the tracked noise. Four factory presets (Gentle, Standard, Strong, Changing noise). Offline only.
- Measured by the tests (six seconds: noise at -40 dB for three and -28 dB for three, a voice-like buzz at 150 Hz from 1 to 2 s and 4 to 5 s, a 12 dB reduction, a 1.5 s span): the gap after the first word comes down 11.9 dB and the gap after the second 11.9 dB (the noise followed through the 12 dB step; 0.1 to 0.5 s after it, 3.2 dB); the voice's first eight harmonics move at most 0.05 dB in either word; at 0 dB the audio comes back within 1.2e-7; it can be cancelled; a tracking span past 5 s and an empty range are refused. The whole suite passed (1387 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real recordings; a background that is itself tonal and steady is tracked as noise and reduced.

**Milestone 307: Dialogue Isolation**
- Effects > Noise Reduction and Repair > Dialogue Isolation...: speech kept and everything else lowered by a reduction (0 to 40 dB), in the selection or the whole file, previewed on the selection and applied in the background. The audio is analysed in 4,096-point frames at 75 % overlap (square-root Hann windows). Each frame is judged for speech: a pitch is looked for from 70 to 400 Hz by comparing, for each candidate, the power per bin within a quarter of it round its first twelve harmonics with the power per bin in the gaps between them (80 Hz to 4 kHz); a candidate counts only with at least three harmonics, and two thirds of those looked for, standing four times over the gaps (a lone tone is periodic but not speech; a candidate an octave under the pitch finds only every other one), the higher of candidates within 2 % wins, and the best one's voicing is its ratio over one plus itself. A frame with voicing over the sensitivity's threshold (0.85 at 0 % down to 0.6 at 100 %) is voiced speech, and keeps the bins within a quarter of the pitch of each harmonic up to 4 kHz and the bins from 4 to 8 kHz standing over the background; a frame whose power from 2 to 8 kHz stands 6 dB over the background where the rest does not is a fricative and keeps that band. The background is the running minimum of each bin's smoothed power over the last second and a half. Everything not kept is lowered; the gains ease down over a few frames. Four factory presets (Gentle, Standard, Strong, Keep more of the voice). Offline only.
- Measured by the tests (a voice-like buzz at 150 Hz with twenty harmonics, 1 to 2 s of three, over noise at -30 dB and a 525 Hz tone at -24 dB throughout): the buzz's pitch is found as 152.7 Hz with voicing 0.99, the background alone has none; with a 12 dB reduction the background before and after the voice comes down 12.0 and 11.8 dB, the voice's first eight harmonics move at most 0.008 dB, the tone under the voice (between its harmonics) comes down 11.9 dB, and a clean voice passes within 0.1 dB; it can be cancelled; a reduction past 40 dB and an empty range are refused. The whole suite passed (1386 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried: real speech over real backgrounds (music, traffic, other voices); a background that is itself periodic (a hum's harmonics, a sustained instrument) can read as voiced.

**Milestone 306: Dither and word length**
- Effects > Level and Loudness > Dither...: the audio rounded to 16 or 24 bits with triangular dither (two uniform random values added, a least-significant bit either way), so the rounding's error is noise rather than distortion that follows the sound: the last step before saving at that depth. Noise shaping feeds the error back through a short filter so the noise sits mostly high up, where hearing is least sensitive: None leaves it flat; Light pushes it up 12 dB an octave (the last two errors fed back with weights 2 and -1); Strong 18 dB an octave (the last three: 3, -3 and 1). Shaped noise is louder in all but quieter below a few kilohertz. Every sample comes out an exact multiple of the step within full scale; the noise is the same for the same audio, so applying twice gives the same result. A sequence step (dither) and four factory presets (CD, 16 bits flat, 16 bits strong shaping, 24 bits).
- Measured by the tests: every sample a multiple of the step at 16 and 24 bits, within full scale, the same twice over; a 1.2 LSB sine at 1 kHz rounded plainly has its 3rd harmonic 22.1 dB below it, dithered 35.0 dB (the noise floor's peak); the sine keeps its level within 0.001 dB; the noise per bin at 2.5 kHz / 15 kHz: flat -127.3 / -127.7 dBFS, Light -146.6 / -118.7, Strong -150.0 / -114.4; 20 bits and an empty range are refused. The whole suite passed (1384 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the dialog; not checked: that saving at 16 bits after dithering keeps the values bit for bit through the file writer.

**Milestone 305: Tonal Balance panel**
- Window > Tonal Balance: the long-term spectrum of what goes to the outputs against a target range for a style of material. The spectrum is the match EQ's profile (twelfth-octave points, 20 Hz to 20 kHz), averaged over the last few seconds of output (each new window weighing a tenth, ten a second) and drawn relative to its own mean over 100 Hz to 10 kHz, so only the shape counts. The target is a band for the chosen style, shaded behind the curve; where the curve leaves the band it is drawn in the warning colour, and the panel says how much of the range from 30 Hz to 16 kHz lies within. The styles are SpectraBlade's own reference shapes, starting points rather than measurements of any catalogue: each a slope against pink noise (3 dB an octave on this scale) with shelves and a tolerance, rolling off below 40 Hz and above 12 kHz. Full mix: pink, 3 dB either way. Bass-heavy: 3 dB more below 100 Hz, 1 dB less above 5 kHz. Bright: 2 dB less below 100 Hz, 2 dB more above 4 kHz. Speech: 6 dB less below 100 Hz and above 8 kHz, 4 dB either way. Orchestral: 1.5 dB an octave steeper than pink, 4 dB either way. The panel docks beside Frequency Analysis.
- Measured by the tests: pink noise sits within the Full mix target from 50 Hz to 10 kHz (98 % of the range within); white noise has 20 % within and stands 7.2 dB over the band's top at 10 kHz; the target's mean over 100 Hz to 10 kHz is zero; the Speech target is narrower at 40 Hz and 10 kHz; changing the style to Speech puts less of the pink noise within. The panel was rendered to an image and looked at. The whole suite passed (1383 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the panel in the app with real material.

**Milestone 304: Match EQ**
- Effects > EQ > Capture Match Reference (the selection, or the whole file), Capture Match Reference from File... (any audio file SpectraBlade reads, up to its first ten minutes, brought to the open file's rate), and Match EQ...: the reference's tone taken onto the selection. A profile is a sound's long-term spectrum at twelfth-octave points from 20 Hz to 20 kHz (8,192-frame Hann windows hopped by half, their power averaged, each point the mean power of the bins in its twelfth of an octave). The correction is the reference's profile less the sound's (measured over the range being matched), smoothed over a width (1 to 24 twelfths of an octave), with the mean over 100 Hz to 10 kHz taken out so the match changes the tone and not the level, no point moved by more than the most (0 to 24 dB), scaled by the amount (0 to 100 %), and applied in linear phase (the linear-phase engine now takes any curve), so the result lands where the audio was. The dialog previews on the selection and applies in the background; four factory presets (Natural, Gentle nudge, Broad strokes, Exact). Match EQ is offline only (a live version would need a reference stored with the rack).
- Measured by the tests: against a reference of white noise shaped by a +6 dB low shelf at 200 Hz and a -6 dB bell at 2 kHz, matching other white noise gives a correction holding the shelf within 0.52 dB and the bell within 0.63 dB; applied, the sound's profile comes within 0.58 dB of the reference's from 50 Hz to 15 kHz; the correction's mean over 100 Hz to 10 kHz is zero (5e-16) and the noise's RMS moves 0.16 dB; at 50 % the shelf goes half way (3.79 against 3.53 dB); smoothed over 2 octaves the bell is more than halved (-2.03 against -4.25); a most of 2 dB caps every point; a reference at another rate, a range under a window and an amount past 100 % are refused. The whole suite passed (1382 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: capturing from a file, the dialog, or on real material.

**Milestone 303: Transient Shaper**
- Effects > Dynamics > Transient Shaper: the attacks (the first moments of a hit) and the sustain (what rings on after) turned up or down on their own, by up to 12 dB, in each of three bands (the multiband split at two crossovers, 24 dB an octave). In each band three envelopes follow the loudest channel: a fast one (0.5 ms up, 20 ms down), a slow one (15 ms up, 50 ms down) and a hold of the recent peak (1 ms up, 150 ms down); the low band's come down slower (80, 150 and 300 ms), so the lowest notes' own cycles don't read as hits. Where the fast one stands over the slow by more than 1.5 dB the sound is attacking and the attack gain applies in proportion (fully at 12 dB over); where the hold stands over the fast by more than 1.5 dB the sound has fallen from its recent peak and is sustaining, and the sustain gain applies likewise, more the further it has fallen. Nothing acts under -60 dBFS; a steady sound is left as it is. The gain follows sample by sample, the same on every channel. No latency. Live in the rack and on tracks (Transient Shaper, under Dynamics), as a sequence step (transientShaper), with five factory presets (Punchier drums, Softer attacks, More room, Tighter low end, Snappy top).
- A band's gain reaches what leaks into it through the crossovers' skirts: with hits in the high band and a tone an octave below the low crossover, the empty middle band reads the hits' leaked tails as sustain and lifts the tone's leak with them, moving the tone by up to 0.5 dB at +6 dB of sustain.
- Measured by the tests (a 100 Hz tone at -14 dB with a 3 kHz hit at -2 dB every 125 ms dying away over 60 ms; crossovers at 200 Hz and 2 kHz): attack +6 dB lifts the first millisecond of each hit 4.9 dB and moves its tail 30 to 60 ms after by 0.05 dB; sustain +6 dB lifts the tail 2.2 dB and moves the tone 90 to 120 ms after by 0.5 dB; every gain at 0 passes within 0.00000001 dB; attack on the high band alone lifts the 3 kHz hit 4.3 dB and leaves the 100 Hz tone within 0.001 dB; the live effect in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames gives the offline samples bit for bit, no latency; state and presets; refused settings. The whole suite passed (1381 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the dialog, or on real drums.

**Milestone 302: Maximizer**
- Effects > Dynamics > Maximizer: a true-peak limiter for the last step of a master, beyond the plain Limiter. The gain (0 to 24 dB) drives the sound into the ceiling (-24 to 0 dBTP); no sample comes out above it, and the peaks between samples are held under it too, as near as a four-times interpolation draws them (the oversampler's new peak detector, 20 frames of delay). Character sets how the limiter moves: Transparent looks 10 ms ahead and recovers slowly (releases of 400 and 60 ms), so little pumps or distorts; Loud looks 2 ms ahead and recovers fast (80 and 10 ms), louder and rougher; Balanced sits between (5 ms; 200 and 30 ms). Two releases run side by side, a slow one for sustained loudness and a fast one for short peaks, and the gain is their blend, the character leaning toward one or the other. Transient Emphasis (0 to 100 %) lifts the attacks before the limiter, by up to 6 dB where a 0.5 ms envelope stands over a 10 ms one, so hits keep their edge through it. The whole comes out late by the detector's 20 frames plus the look-ahead (116 frames as Loud, 500 as Transparent at 48 kHz); applied to a file the result lands where the audio was, and the live effect reports the latency, again when the character changes it. Live in the rack and on tracks (Maximizer, under Dynamics, its gain reduction shown), as a sequence step (maximizer), with four factory presets (Gentle, Streaming, Loud, Punchy drums).
- A sound that starts loud at once comes out over the ceiling between samples through its first look-ahead (its samples never), since the frames before the first count as needing nothing, as the plain limiter's do.
- Measured by the tests: a 0.9 sine faded in and driven 12 dB into a -1 dBTP ceiling comes out with no sample over the ceiling and its true peak within 0.04 dB of it for every character (one that starts loud at once: 1.1 dB over at its start); a quiet sound with no gain passes bit for bit, where it was; on hits over a tone driven 10 dB, Transparent comes out at -13.0 dB RMS, Balanced -10.5 and Loud -7.8, and with Transient Emphasis at 100 % the tone 20 to 60 ms after each hit comes out 1.2 dB quieter than with none (-12.9 against -11.7 dB), the ceiling held; the live effect in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames gives the offline samples bit for bit, late by its latency, which it reports and changes with the character; state and presets; refused settings. The whole suite passed (1379 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the dialog, or on real masters.

**Milestone 301: Exciter**
- Effects > Saturation > Exciter: the sound split into lows, middle and highs at two crossovers (the multiband split, 24 dB an octave), each band driven into a saturation curve of its own and mixed back with the band as it was. Three curves, each with a slope of 1 at zero (a quiet sound passes as it is) and bounded: Tube rounds the halves unevenly (the negative half into two thirds of the swing), so it adds even harmonics with the odd; Tape rounds them alike (tanh), odd harmonics only; Transistor clips harder (a cubic to a flat top). The drive (0 to 36 dB) sets how far the band goes into the curve (the band scaled up, shaped and scaled back), so the harmonics grow while the level stays about where it was; the mix (0 to 100 %) blends the shaped band with the band as it was; an output gain after. A one-pole high-pass at 5 Hz after each band's shaping takes off the offset the uneven curve makes.
- The shaping runs at four times the rate (a new Oversampler4: the band raised through a 161-tap windowed-sinc low-pass, Kaiser beta 8, cut at the original Nyquist, shaped, and brought back down through the same), so the harmonics above Nyquist are filtered off rather than folded back; what it makes past four times the rate still folds (a 15 kHz tone's 13th harmonic lands at 3 kHz). The whole comes out 40 frames late; applied to a file the result lands where the audio was, and the live effect reports the latency so the rack lines it up. Live in the rack and on tracks (Exciter, under Distortion), as a sequence step (exciter), with five factory presets (Warm lows, Tape glue, Air and presence, Vocal edge, Crunch).
- Measured by the tests: the oversampler puts an impulse out at 40 frames (peak 0.975), passes DC at unity within 1e-6, passes 0.8 of Nyquist within 0.001 dB and has 1.2 of it 84.2 dB down; each curve's slope at zero is 1 within 1e-6 and stays within 1; a 1 kHz sine at half scale driven 18 dB: Tube's 2nd harmonic 25.8 dB below the fundamental and 3rd 11.4, Tape's 2nd 137.9 down and 3rd 11.7, 5th 19.4, Transistor's 2nd 134.0 down, 3rd 10.5, 5th 15.9 (stronger than Tape's); a 15 kHz sine driven 12 dB into Tape has its 3rd harmonic's fold at 3 kHz 77.7 dB below the fundamental where shaping at the rate itself leaves it 15.5 dB down; at a mix of 0 sines from 60 Hz to 12 kHz pass within 0.0000001 dB; the live effect in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames gives the offline samples bit for bit, late by its latency, which it reports; state and presets; refused settings. The whole suite passed (1375 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the dialog, or on real material; not measured: the CPU cost live.

**Milestone 300: Multiband Stereo Width**
- Effects > Stereo and Channels > Multiband Stereo Width: the sound split into lows, middle and highs at two crossovers (the multiband compressor's Linkwitz-Riley split, 24 dB an octave, so the bands add back flat), and each band's width set on its own: 0 % makes the band mono (its side, what differs between the channels, taken out; the low band at 0 % is a mono low end below the low crossover), 100 % leaves it, up to 200 % doubles its side. What the channels share is never touched, so a mono sound comes through unchanged. Live in the rack and on tracks (Multiband Stereo Width, under Spatial), as a sequence step (multibandWidth), with four factory presets (Mono below 120 Hz, Tight low end, wide top, Wider highs, Narrower middle).
- Measured by the tests: with the side's tones at 60 Hz, 500 Hz and 8 kHz and the mid's at 80 Hz, 700 Hz and 6 kHz, the low band at 0 % takes the side's 60 Hz down 32.1 dB (what is left comes through the middle band's skirt, 24 dB an octave, 1.3 octaves up), the middle at 100 % leaves 500 Hz within 0.04 dB and the high at 200 % lifts 8 kHz 6.01 dB; the mid is left within 0.00000002 dB at its three tones; every band at 100 % leaves the side within 0.00000002 dB; a mono sound is untouched bit for bit; crossovers under an octave apart or a width past 200 % are refused; the live effect in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames gives the offline samples bit for bit; its state and presets restore the settings. The whole suite passed (1370 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the dialog, or on real material.

**Milestone 299: Linear phase for the Parametric EQ**
- Linear Phase, a switch under the Parametric EQ's drawing (in the window and in the live EQ): the bands' curve applied as one symmetric impulse, so every frequency comes out at the bands' level with no phase turned, where the ordinary bands turn the phase round each band's frequency. The impulse is 16,383 frames long: the inverse transform of the bands' response sampled at 65,536 points, under a Hann window, so a feature narrower than about two bins of that window (some 6 Hz at 48 kHz) is drawn more broadly than the bands ask. Bands on the mid, the side, the left or the right are composed in order into a matrix of impulses (left from left, left from right, right from left, right from right), run through the convolver; a channel no band touches is only delayed, bit for bit. Applied to a file, the result lands where the audio was; played live, it comes out 8,703 frames late (181 ms at 48 kHz; the latency reported, so the rack lines it up), and a band moved while playing remakes the impulse on the spot, in the audio's time. The switch is kept with the window's settings and in presets (linearPhase). The Dynamic EQ has no linear-phase mode: its bands move as the sound does.
- Measured by the tests: sines from 30 Hz to 15 kHz through a +6 dB low shelf at 100 Hz, a -8 dB bell at 1 kHz (Q 2) and a +4 dB high shelf at 8 kHz come out within 0.007 dB of the bands' response (a bell at 40 Hz with a Q of 4, 10 Hz wide, 0.82 dB short of its peak); an impulse comes out symmetric about itself within 1e-7 (the minimum-phase one is 0.38 off); a band on the side leaves a sound with no side within 1e-5 and lifts the side's 3 kHz 12 dB with the mid's 1 kHz within 0.01 dB; a band on the right leaves the left bit for bit; the engine played in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames gives the offline samples bit for bit, late by its latency; the live EQ reports 8,703 frames of latency while on and 0 before and after, and plays bit for bit with offline late by it; the switch and presets. The whole suite passed (1369 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: in the app, in the rack, or on real material; not measured: the time the impulse takes to remake when a band moves while playing.

**Milestone 298: EQ bands on the mid, the side, the left or the right**
- Each band of the Parametric EQ and the Dynamic EQ has a channel: Stereo (every channel, as before), Mid (what left and right share), Side (what differs between them), Left or Right. A band on the mid or the side splits each frame into mid = (left + right) / 2 and side = (left - right) / 2, filters one of them and adds them back, so the other is left as it was, bit for bit in what it holds; a band on the left or the right filters that channel alone. On a mono sound, mid is the sound and side nothing; on more than two channels, mid and side come from the first two and the rest are left as they are. In the dynamic EQ, the band's part (what it listens to and moves) comes from that signal and its change goes back to it.
- The parametric EQ's row has a channel box after the type; the dynamic EQ's settings a channel box after Over/Under. A band on one channel wears its letter (M, S, L or R) on its handle, and the readout names it. The live EQs have the channel as a parameter a band (band1Channel to band5Channel), kept in states and presets; the factory presets name Stereo.
- The parametric EQ's bands now run through Equalizer::Stage (one band on the channels it works on, a block at a time), in the offline apply and the live EQ alike; bands on every channel give the same samples as before.
- Measured by the tests: with left = a + b and right = a - b (a 1 kHz, b 3 kHz), a +12 dB bell on the mid lifts the mid's 1 kHz 12.00 dB and leaves the side's 3 kHz within 0.000001 dB, one on the side the other way round, one on the left leaves the right bit for bit; on a mono sound a side band changes nothing and a mid band is the band on every channel; the live parametric EQ with bands on the mid, the side and the left, played in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames, gives the offline samples bit for bit; the row's box sets the channel and the presets keep it. The dynamic EQ: a band on the mid cuts the mid's 1 kHz 12.0 dB and leaves the side's 3 kHz within 0.000001 dB, and the other way round, the left alone, mono; live bit for bit with offline with a band on the mid and one on the side; the panel's channel box. The whole suite passed (1367 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: in the app, or on real stereo material.

**Milestone 297: Dynamic EQ**
- Effects > EQ > Dynamic EQ: five bands, each a bell or a shelf that moves only while its own part of the sound is over (or under) its threshold, by as much as that part is past it (at the ratio, 1 to 20), up to the band's gain (within 24 dB), with attack (0.1 to 500 ms) and release (5 ms to 5 s). A cut at 3 kHz over -30 dB leaves the sound alone until the 3 kHz part gets loud, then takes it down, and lets go as it falls; Under the threshold lifts (or cuts) what gets lost instead. Each band works as the sound plus its own part scaled (a band-pass at a bell's frequency with unity at the centre, a first-order pass at a shelf's corner), so a band moving 0 dB passes the sound bit for bit, a bell's width stays the same however far it moves, and a shelf is 6 dB an octave; the level a band reacts to is its part's mean square over a few cycles of its frequency, the loudest channel leading.
- The panel: a handle a band at its frequency and gain over the drawing (drag it for both, the wheel for a bell's Q, double-click to switch a band off or on, or on empty space to place one), each band's most as a dashed line in its colour and what it is doing now shaded under it, the bands together glowing over a faint line of their most, and the spectrum of what is playing behind. Under it, the picked band's settings (its number, On, Bell or shelf, Over or Under, and the sliders) and the output; beside it, the picked band's level as it plays with its threshold as a line across it, dragged to set the threshold by eye. Preview on the selection plays the live effect and draws its gains and levels on the panel. Presets (five factory ones: Tame harsh vocals, Control a boomy low end, Hold down sibilance and mud, Lift quiet presence, Air that never gets harsh).
- Live in the effects rack and on tracks (Dynamic EQ, under EQ), every setting a parameter, with the same window; played in blocks it gives the offline samples bit for bit; no latency.
- The parametric EQ's drawing code shared: the well, the grid, the spectrum, the glow, the handles and the readout now live in EqDrawing, used by both.
- Measured by the tests: every band at 0 dB (or under its threshold) passing the sound bit for bit; a bell cutting up to 9 dB at 1 kHz over -20 dB taking a loud 1 kHz (-6 dB) down 9.0 dB at a ratio of 20 with 100 Hz and 8 kHz kept within 0.05 dB, and letting go within 0.0001 dB once it falls to -34 dB; a high shelf lifting up to 12 dB under -30 dB at 2:1 lifting a 10 kHz at -40 dB by 4.87 dB (the static gain 5.0; the shelf at 4 kHz is not at its full lift at 10 kHz); a bell's response at its centre its gain exactly and -0.01 dB a decade off, a low shelf's +6 dB reaching 5.97 dB at 20 Hz and 0.02 dB at 5 kHz; refused settings; live bit for bit with offline in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames, the readings the core's, state and presets restoring every setting, the window both ways; the panel's mouse. The whole suite passed (1365 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the dialog in the app, the rack window, or on real material.

**Milestone 296: The EQs drawn anew**
- Parametric EQ: each band has a colour of its own; its own response is shaded in it from the 0 dB line, and its row of controls is marked with it. The bands together are drawn as a glowing curve. Each band switched on has a numbered handle; the one under the mouse (or being dragged) says its type, frequency, gain and Q beside it, and pressing a handle picks its band out (ringed, its row shaded). Double-clicking a handle switches its band off, leaving a ring on the 0 dB line; double-clicking the ring switches it on again; double-clicking anywhere else on the drawing puts the first band switched off there. In the Parametric EQ window, the spectrum of what is playing is drawn behind the curve (-90 to 0 dBFS), falling back 0.6 dB a frame at 25 frames a second.
- Graphic EQ (10 and 31 bands): the sliders are replaced by bars from the 0 dB line, each band's colour running from warm (low) to cool (high), with the response the bands make together drawn over them (where neighbouring bands add up, the curve shows it). Pressing sets the band under the mouse to the gain there; dragging across sets every band passed over along the line the mouse drew, so a shape can be sketched in one stroke; double-clicking resets a band; the wheel moves one 0.5 dB a step (0.1 with Shift). The band under the mouse says its frequency and gain.
- Our own look: nothing taken from another product's drawing.
- Measured by the tests: the parametric EQ's picking, double-clicking off, on and placing (a band placed at 700 Hz with the curve at +5 dB there), the spectrum's fall, a colour for each band; the graphic EQ's scales, pressing, a stroke across bands 2 to 6 from +6 to -6 dB setting the bands between along the line (to 0.1 dB), double-click, the wheel, and setting gains from outside without a change reported. Both panels were rendered to images in both themes and looked at. The whole suite passed (1358 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not done: the spectrum in the effects rack's EQ window (only the Parametric EQ window shows it).
- Not tried by hand: the panels in the app.

**Milestone 295: Browsing the Sound Library by folder**
- The Sound Library's left pane has By Folder and By Category; By Folder comes first. It lists the folders you added, each opening into the folders inside it that hold sounds (in them or deeper), in name order as the Finder lists them, with how many sounds are in each and below. Choosing one shows only those sounds (with the search and the other filters as before); right-clicking one shows it in the Finder. Changing to By Category shows every sound again, and back.
- The tree is read from the index, not the disk, so a network volume that is away doesn't hold it up. It steps from one subfolder to the next with one look-up each (and one for each file loose in the folder), and a folder's count reads only its own range of the paths' index. On a made-up index of 660,600 paths (60 folders of 20 of 548, and 3,000 loose files), listing the top folder's 60 took 11 ms and counting all of it 17 ms (measured with the same queries from Python's SQLite).
- Why: a category comes only from a UCS file name or iXML, which most libraries don't have (about 15,000 of the 658,000 sounds in the library this was tried on), so browsing by category left most sounds under No Category.
- Measured by the tests: the folders holding sounds, in name order, not a sibling whose name starts the same (Doors-Old, Doors0) nor an empty folder; 8 sounds in the library, 3 in Doors and deeper, 2 in Wood; a query within Doors finds its 3, searched within it 1; in the window, By Folder first, a folder chosen, By Category showing all again, a removed folder's sounds gone. The whole suite passed (1358 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the pane in the app, or on a real library.

**Milestone 294: The Effects menu in groups**
- The Effects menu was one list of 71 commands and the Fade Shape submenu. It is now twelve submenus by what the effects do: Level and Loudness, Fades (with Fade Shape), EQ, Dynamics (Noise Gate among them), Modulation and Delay, Reverb, Time and Pitch, Stereo and Channels, Silence, Generate, Noise Reduction and Repair, and Spectral. Apply Effects Rack and the command sequences stay at the bottom. The waveform's right-click menu shows the same groups.
- Every command the menu had is still in it, none twice (checked by comparing the command lists before and after); shortcuts are unchanged.
- Not tested by the suite (the menus are built in MainComponent, which is not in the test build). The whole suite passed (1353 checks); the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: opening the menus in the app.

**Milestone 293: Previews for the generators**
- Generate Tone, Generate DTMF Tones and Generate Noise get the Preview button (and Space) the other effect dialogs have. The preview plays the sound as it would go in: put in at the cursor (or the start of the selection) with what follows moved on, so you hear it against what is around it. It follows the settings as they change; Bypass plays what is there now. Of a sound longer than 30 s, the first 30 s play, and the dialog says so.
- Generate Noise keeps one seed while its dialog is open, so the noise put in starts as the noise previewed did.
- Measured by the tests: a noise preview is the noise put in at the cursor, bit for bit, with its place given right; with the cursor past the end it goes in at the end; of 45 s of tone, 30 s play and the note says so. The whole suite passed (1353 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the dialogs' Preview in the app (MainComponent, which wires them, is not in the test build).

**Milestone 292: SpectraBlade Stretch places hits on their highs**
- Each hit is now placed where its highs (the size of each sample's step) start to rise, and on the level only when the highs don't rise to a new high. Before, the level came first, and a bass line or a kick's body under a hit could move it: a hat over a bass note could be placed up to 8 ms away from where it starts, and a kick's click on the louder body that follows it.
- The drop at 200% reported for Milestone 291 on the drum loop (0.61 to 0.54) was the judge's, not the stretch's. The judge marked each hit where the 2 ms window whose level jumped begins, which is 2 ms before the hit (2.0 ms early on 19 of 23 synthetic hits with known times). At 200% that puts a well-placed hit right at the edge of the ±2 ms the likeness search allows. Measured from where the hit really starts, Milestone 291 scores 0.80, 0.60 and 0.61 on the drum loop at 75%, 150% and 200%, and level-only placement 0.73, 0.63 and 0.63. The figures below use the corrected judge.
- Measured on the scratch prototype the code was ported from, against Milestone 291:
  - Synthetic drums over a bass line, six takes with known hit times (43 kicks with a click, 40 snares, 40 hats, 37 hats 15 dB quieter): placed within 2 ms: 38 kicks (before 25), all 40 snares (before 33), all 40 hats (before 25) and 32 quiet hats (before 15). Each hit's first 10 ms correlates 0.925 to 0.938 with the original from 50% to 200% (before 0.849 at 50%, 0.901 at 75%, 0.847 at 150% and 0.784 at 200%).
  - 21 kicks with no click (a 2 ms fade-in) were not found at all, before or now.
  - Synthetic drums (a hit every 125 ms over a quiet chord): 1.00 from 50% to 150% and 0.98 at 200% (before 0.97 and 0.92).
  - Drum loop, at the 47 hits the judge finds over 4 kHz, where a hit's start is sharp: 0.984, 0.996 and 0.913 at 75%, 150% and 200% (before 0.985, 0.977 and 0.849). Allowing 10 ms either side, each of the 47 matches the original (1.00), before and now: what is left is hits placed 2 to 2.4 ms off, and one faint hat 17 ms before a louder hit, which the 30 ms gap folds into it.
  - Drum loop at all 96 judged hits, which include rises under 200 Hz: 0.80, 0.61, 0.62 (before 0.80, 0.60, 0.61).
  - Speech: 0.86, 0.88, 0.71 (before 0.85, 0.86, 0.70); over 4 kHz alone 0.900, 0.858, 0.655 (before 0.890, 0.791, 0.672). Guitar: 0.88, 0.67, 0.54 (before 0.82, 0.71, 0.56); over 4 kHz alone 0.989, 0.742, 0.544 (before 0.989, 0.701, 0.537). Electric piano: 0.73, 0.65, 0.68, as before.
