  - The busy kits' 69 hits (the test below) are placed as before: all within 1 ms.
- Measured by the tests:
  - 31 hats (bright noise dying away over 20 ms, about 20 dB under a 70 Hz bass): all found within 1 ms (the worst 4 samples) and nothing else. From 50% to 200% each comes out within 4 samples of where the stretch puts it, matching the input from 1 ms before to 2 ms after (within 0.01; the largest difference measured was 0.0000). On the prototype with the old order, 12 of the 31 were placed within 1 ms and the largest difference was 0.42.
  - The earlier checks still hold, the busy kit among them. The whole suite passed (1350 checks).
- Not tried by hand: listening in the app.

**Milestone 291: SpectraBlade Stretch catches more hits in busy drums**
- Finding attacks:
  - A hit counts when its spectral flux is over 1.5 times the median within 50 ms either side (it was the mean within 100 ms, which a loud neighbour pushed up) and over 0.8 dB (it was 2 dB). Hits may be 30 ms apart (it was 50 ms); of two closer than that, the earlier is kept.
  - Each hit is placed on the steepest 1 ms rise that reaches a new high, over the loudest 1 ms in the 15 ms before. It is taken from the level, or, when the level only falls (a quiet hit over a kick's dying thump), from the size of each sample's step, which follows the hit's highs. Before, the thump's own swings could win, and a ghost hit's nearest attack could be 37 ms away.
- Stretching round them:
  - Attacks too close for half a frame either side now share the room between them, half each. Before, an attack whose span did not fit was dropped and stretched like everything else.
  - The phases are set back at every attack, even one whose span is too short to hold a frame's centre.
  - Each attack's span (at least 1 ms before to 3 ms after) is copied in from the input, crossfaded over 2 ms. Frames outside the spans leave out the far side of a nearby attack: from 1 ms before one ahead of them, and up to 3 ms after one behind.
- Measured on the scratch prototype the code was ported from, against Milestone 290, with a stricter separate judge than Milestone 290's: an energy rise of 9 dB in the whole sound, under 200 Hz or over 4 kHz, that stays up.
  - Synthetic busy kits at 100 and 140 BPM (86 hits): 86 found, with 3 extra (before: 85, 2 extra). Synthetic drums (29 hits): 29, none extra, as before. Steady chord, white and pink noise, a sweep with tremolo: no attacks, as before.
  - Drum loop: 63 attacks found, matching 61 of the judge's 96 hits (before: 47 found, matching 48). Guitar: 13 of 15 (before 10). Speech: 51 of 74 (before 32). Electric piano: 1 attack found (before none).
  - Synthetic drums from 50% to 200%: each hit's correlation with the original is 0.97 (0.92 at 200%); before, 0.62 to 0.82. Energy before each hit is -10.2 to -10.5 dB against the hit (before -6.3 to -10.4).
  - Drum loop at 75%, 150% and 200%: 0.77, 0.64 and 0.54 (before 0.65, 0.62 and 0.61). It is worse at 200%, and changing how the hits are placed is what costs it: placed on the level alone, it was 0.61 at 200% but 0.71 at 75%.
  - Speech: 0.80, 0.76, 0.70 (before 0.75, 0.75, 0.69). Guitar: 0.77, 0.69, 0.63 (before 0.79, 0.68, 0.67). Electric piano: 0.72, 0.64, 0.65 (before 0.73, 0.70, 0.69), worse at 150% and 200%, where it now finds one attack.
  - Clicks every 80 ms: each comes out at its own peak at every ratio from 50% to 200%. Without the copies from the input, a peak was off by up to 0.29.
- Measured by the tests:
  - A busy kit's 69 hits (ghost hits 15 dB down under a dying kick, 50 and 60 ms apart): all found within 1 sample and nothing else. From 50% to 200%, each comes out within a sample of where the stretch puts it, matching the input from 1 ms before to 2 ms after (within 0.01; the largest difference measured was 0.0000). With the new detector but the old placement, 23 of the 69 were placed within 1 ms.
  - Steady white noise: no attacks.
  - The earlier checks still hold: the click train, chord, tone and speed. The whole suite passed (1349 checks).
- Not tried by hand: listening to either the drum loop or the kits in the app.

**Milestone 290: SpectraBlade Stretch, our own, with sharper attacks**
- Stretch and Shift Pitch get a Method menu: Signalsmith Stretch, as before and still the default, or SpectraBlade Stretch, our own phase vocoder (Source/PhaseVocoder), written from the published methods. Sequence steps carry the method; steps saved before have none and keep Signalsmith Stretch. Stretching session clips stays with Signalsmith Stretch.
- How it works:
  - 85 ms frames, four to a frame's length. Each frequency's speed comes from two transforms a hop apart.
  - All channels share one phase turn per frequency, so their phase relations are kept exactly.
  - Each frequency follows the peak it sits under (Laroche and Dolson's phase locking), so a note's partials move together.
  - Attacks are found first (rising spectral flux, then back to where the rise starts). Each stays where the stretch puts it, with half a frame either side played at its own speed and the input's own phases, so it comes out as it went in. The audio between attacks stretches to make up the length.
  - Shift Pitch with it stretches by the pitch ratio and resamples back, so formants move with the pitch.
- Measured on test signals, WSOLA (the stretch before Milestone 289), Signalsmith Stretch and ours, from 50% to 200%:
  - A click every 250 ms: ours brings every click out once, where it belongs (within 0.1 ms), its shape as it was (correlation 1.00), with silence before it as in the input. Signalsmith Stretch smears each click forward, with energy 31 to 44 dB under the click in the 25 ms before it.
  - Drums (a hit every 125 ms) over a quiet chord: the first 10 ms after each hit correlates 0.62 to 0.82 with the original's in ours, 0.20 to 0.40 in Signalsmith Stretch and 0.21 to 0.58 in WSOLA.
  - A steady four-note chord: energy more than 6 Hz from the notes is -43 to -53 dB in ours, -30 to -52 dB in Signalsmith Stretch and -30 to -31 dB in WSOLA.
- Measured on recordings at 75%, 150% and 200%, each judged at onsets found by a separate energy-rise detector rather than ours. The recordings are a drum loop, a funk guitar loop and an electric piano loop from the loops that come with macOS, and speech from its text-to-speech.
  - Drum loop: ours correlates 0.52 to 0.65 with the original after each hit, Signalsmith Stretch 0.33 to 0.41, WSOLA 0.31 to 0.39. Ours finds 47 of the judge's 88 onsets.
  - Guitar: ours 0.68 to 0.90, Signalsmith Stretch 0.50 to 0.55, WSOLA 0.53 to 0.63. Ours has less energy before each attack than Signalsmith Stretch at every ratio; at 75% WSOLA has the least.
  - Speech: ours 0.63 to 0.78, Signalsmith Stretch 0.56 to 0.59, WSOLA 0.56 to 0.63.
  - Electric piano (soft attacks): ours finds none, and all three come out alike (0.62 to 0.72).
- Limits: where attacks come closer together than a frame, less of each plays at its own speed; quiet attacks under a loud sound are missed; transients the detector misses are smeared as in any phase vocoder.
- Measured by the tests:
  - A tone from 50% to 200% keeps its length, pitch (within 0.5%), level (within 1 dB) and edges, with its channels exactly opposite.
  - 16 clicks are found within 1 sample. Each comes out in place at its own peak, with at least 155 dB of quiet before it.
  - A steady chord has no attacks found, and its energy more than 6 Hz from the notes stays under -40 dB.
  - Progress and stopping work. Shift Pitch keeps each pitch within 0.5%. The method works as a sequence step.
  - 10 s of dense stereo stretches to 15 s in about 0.1 s.
  - The whole suite passed (1347 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the Method menu, and listening to either method on real material in the app.

**Milestone 289: Stretching that doesn't stutter**
- Stretch, Shift Pitch, stretching session clips (by menu or by Option-dragging) and the Stretch and Shift Pitch sequence steps now use Signalsmith Stretch 1.4.0 (MIT licence), a phase vocoder, instead of our WSOLA, which repeated or dropped 40 ms pieces of the audio. The library and the Signalsmith Linear 0.6.4 FFTs it uses are fetched at their release commits; their licences go into the disk image.
- Measured on test signals, WSOLA against the new stretch:
  - A click every 250 ms (16 in 4 s): WSOLA turned them into 26 at 200% and 11 at 50%. The new stretch keeps 16 at every length from 50% to 200%, each within 5 ms of where it belongs. This was the stutter.
  - A four-note chord: energy more than 6 Hz from the notes was about -31 dB at every length with WSOLA. With the new stretch it is -44 to -52 dB at 50%, 75%, 90% and 110%, -39 dB at 200%, and still about -31 dB at 125% and 150%. There it sits within 30 Hz of the notes (a slow wobble on each note, as with WSOLA); further from the notes it is -64.5 dB (WSOLA -56.5 dB).
  - 60 s of dense stereo: 0.16 s to stretch to 50%, 0.63 s to 200%.
- The ends of a whole clip or file are padded with the audio turned through its end sample, so the level holds to the edges: off by at most 0.061 on a 0.5 tone from 50% to 200% (a plain mirror image was off by up to 0.62 at 200%).
- Channels are no longer bit-for-bit mirrors of each other when the input's are. They stay within 0.001 (anti-phase tone), and the test says so.
- Shift Pitch moves formants with the pitch, as before.
- Measured by the tests: the click train; the tone's length, pitch (within 0.5%), level (within 1 dB) and edges; pitch shifts within 0.5%; the joins in a file; progress and stopping. The whole suite passed (1341 checks).
- Not tried by hand: stretching or shifting real recordings and listening to them. The library's own notes say it sounds best between 75% and 150%.

**Milestone 288: The rest of the effect dialogs, and factory presets**
- Every settings dialog in the Effects menu now uses the new effect dialog: Meet Delivery Spec, Generate DTMF Tones, Noise Gate, Shorten Pauses, Trim Silence, Insert Silence, Fade Selection Edges, Fade Each Region, Normalize Each Region, Crossfade Loop and Attenuate Frequencies. So do the silence finders (Find Next Silence, Add Markers at Silences, Add Regions over Sounds), and Clip Gain and Normalize Clip in the multitrack view.
  - A new kind of field, a text field, takes DTMF digits and Insert Silence's length typed as 1:30. The silence finders now take their time in seconds, on a slider, instead of as typed text.
- Previews for:
  - Meet Delivery Spec, Normalize Each Region, the two fades, Crossfade Loop (the selection loops with its new seam) and Attenuate Frequencies (what is drawn in the spectrogram, or the selection's band). These process all of the selection, since they reach across it.
  - Noise Gate, which processes the first 30 s.
  - Shorten Pauses and Trim Silence cut the file and the generators insert audio, so they have no preview.
- 201 factory presets across 42 effects, listed first in each presets menu under "Factory", with the user's own under "Yours". They can't be changed or deleted; saving under a factory preset's name saves a preset of your own.
  - They are starting points for common jobs, for example: light, medium and strong dialogue noise reduction; vinyl crackle and mouth-click repair; 50 and 60 Hz hum; loudness targets for broadcast (EBU R128, ATSC A/85), podcasts, streaming and audiobooks; female and male vocal de-essing; master, vocal and drum compression; vocal and dialogue EQ curves; rooms and halls; PAL and film speed changes.
  - They are settings to adjust by ear. They weren't tuned by listening.
- Measured by the tests:
  - Every factory preset is a setting its effect takes: 105 run as steps without a problem, and on 72 for live effects every parameter is set within its range, except the compressor's side-chain switch, which presets leave alone. No name repeats within an effect.
  - The presets menu lists the factory presets first, applies the one chosen, and deletes only the user's.
  - A text field takes only its characters and leaves Space and Return to the dialog.
  - The whole suite passed (1340 checks).
- Not tried by hand in the app: any of these dialogs, the previews by ear, and the presets by ear.

**Milestone 287: Previewing every effect, and a better effect dialog**
- Every built-in effect with settings now has a preview, not only the ones that run live. Noise Reduction, Click and Pop Repair, Reduce Reverb, Remove Hum, Repair Clipping, Stretch, Shift Pitch, Pitch Correction, Level Speech, Centre Channel, Automatic Phase Correction, Gain, Normalize, Match Loudness, Balance, Stereo Width, Gain Ramp, Pan Ramp, Echo, Tremolo and EQ Band process the selection (or the file) in the background with the dialog's settings, then loop the result in place of the audio, before the effects rack. They process its first 30 s, or all of it when the result depends on all of it: Normalize, Match Loudness, the ramps, Phase Correction, and Repair Clipping keeping the peak level. A change of settings is processed once the settings have stood still for a quarter of a second; the last result plays meanwhile, and the dialog says when it is processing.
- One dialog for the built-in effects: a slider and its value for each number, a menu for each choice, presets for every one of them, Preview and Bypass (the audio as it is, to compare), and Apply and Cancel.
  - The mouse wheel steps a value up or down, as do the arrow keys; Shift steps a tenth as far, and double-clicking a slider sets it back.
  - Space starts and stops the preview; Return applies; Escape cancels.
  - Wide ranges of frequencies and times spread out evenly by ratio.
- Right-clicking the waveform gives a menu with Play/Pause, Loop Playback, Cut, Copy, Paste, Delete, Crop, Select All and the Effects menu. Loop Playback was already in the Transport menu (Cmd-L) and the transport bar.
- Measured by the tests:
  - A rendered preview is, bit for bit, what applying the step gives, and a stretched one says where its range now ends.
  - Only the newest request is answered: a newer one stops the one under way at its next progress check, and a cancelled one isn't answered.
  - The player plays a preview in place of the file, past the file's end too, until it is taken away. The live preview effect can be bypassed and put back.
  - In the dialog, a wheel notch or arrow key moves a value one step onto the step's grid, Shift a tenth of a step, within its range, and the wheel steps a menu. Space passes the fields by and starts and stops the preview; Return applies what is shown.
  - The whole suite passed (1337 checks).
- Not tried by hand in the app: the dialogs, the previews by ear, the right-click menu, and the wheel with a real mouse or trackpad. A trackpad's smooth scrolling counts a step for every 0.08 of scroll, a figure not tuned on a trackpad.
- Not converted yet: Meet Delivery Spec, DTMF Tones, Noise Gate and the fade, silence and region dialogs keep their old plain dialogs. Parametric EQ, the graphic EQs and the multiband compressor keep their own windows, which already had a live preview.

**Release 0.13.0: a Sound Library, a Pitch Editor, 7.1, surround effects, delivery specs and scrubbing**
- `SpectraBlade-0.13.0.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 248 to 286 below on top of 0.12.0. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1321 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.13.0, holds both architectures, and carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` and LAME's licence. The image's app was not launched; the same code, signed for development, was launched for each milestone. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried. The parts of each milestone not tried by hand are listed with it below.

**Milestone 286: File names give categories only as UCS writes them**
- Fixed: a file name gave a UCS category whenever it started with a CatID and an underscore in any case, so ordinary names were filed under categories they don't belong to: "Wind_Chimes" and "Wind_Up_Toy" under WIND, "beep_n_glitch" and "Beep_TargetLock" under BEEPS, names starting "test_" under TEST. A name now gives a category only when its CatID is in its own case, as UCS names are written (DOORWood_..., WIND_...). In one large library's file list, 3,181 of the 15,339 names that gave a category gave it only that way, all ordinary names (BEEP 2,463, WIND 289, RAIN 217, and others). A category in a file's iXML is still matched in any case, since it is given as a CatID.
- An index from before is brought up to date when it opens: the sounds whose names gave a category only ignoring case lose it, and are no longer found by its words; a category set by hand stays; and those files are read again on the next scan, so a category in their iXML is found.
- Category suggestions stay unbuilt: what was tried got 40% of categories right across vendors (see the roadmap for the measurements).
- Measured by the tests: DOORWood_ names give DOORS / WOOD and WIND_Gust gives WIND, but doorwood_Creak and Wind_Chimes give nothing; an index from M283 opens with Wind_Chimes no longer under WIND nor found by its words, a sound whose category was set by hand keeping it, and DOORWood_Creak keeping its own, and its next scan reads the two changed sounds again. The whole suite passed (1321 checks). Not tried: opening a real library's index from M283 (none was kept).

**Milestone 285: Inserting sounds from the Sound Library by keyboard**
- Return in the Sound Library's list (or Insert at Cursor in its right-click menu) puts the selected sound into the session at the cursor, on the selected track (a new track when none is selected), and moves the cursor to its end, so the next one follows it. With part of its waveform selected in the preview, only that part goes in (written beside the index, as dragging it out writes it). Several selected sounds go in whole, the first on the selected track and the rest on new tracks below, as dropping them does. It is one undo step, and the main window shows the session.
- Measured by the tests: Return hands the selected sound's file to the main window, nothing selected (or no main window) inserts nothing, and a refused insert reports it; with 1,200 to 3,600 of a 4,800-frame sound selected in the preview, Return inserts a file of those 2,400 frames with its 2 channels and 24 bits, outside the library's folders; with two sounds selected both go whole, in the list's order. The whole suite passed (1320 checks). Not tried: the main window's side (the clip at the cursor on the selected track, the cursor moving to its end) has no test, since the tests don't build the main window, and none of it was tried with a real keyboard in the running app.

**Milestone 284: Writing categories and tags into files**
- The Sound Library's right-click menu has Write Category and Tags into Files..., which asks first and then writes the selected sounds' categories and tags into their WAV files, where other apps read them: the iXML chunk's USER block (CATID, CATEGORY, SUBCATEGORY, CATEGORYFULL, and KEYWORDS with the tags separated by commas) and its ASWG block (catId, category, subCategory). It runs on a thread of its own; the line below the list says how many files were written, skipped (those with neither a category nor tags) or not written and why, and the folders are rescanned after.
- Safely: each file is written beside the old one as a hidden temporary file, every chunk but the iXML (the audio among them) copied byte for byte and in its order, and the rest of the old iXML kept. The new file is read back and checked before it replaces the old one in one step: the same chunks, every one but the iXML identical, the category and tags read back, every other element, attribute and text of the old iXML still there, the audio readable at the same length, rate and channels. The file keeps its permissions. Anything that fails leaves the old file as it was and no temporary file behind.
- Not written, saying why: AIFF, FLAC, MP3 and other files that aren't WAV; RF64 files; files whose chunks run past their end; read-only files; an iXML that isn't XML, or holds a NUL or other control byte (as some recorders leave in theirs), which rewriting would lose. The description isn't written.
- Measured by the tests: a WAV file with fmt, data, bext, an odd-sized chunk, an iXML holding another CatID and other elements, and LIST gets the category and tags in USER and ASWG, its old CatID replaced and the rest of its iXML kept, every other chunk byte for byte and in place, a right RIFF size, its 0640 permissions and no temporary file; a file without iXML gets one at its end, and tags alone write only KEYWORDS; an AIFF file, an RF64 file, an unreadable iXML, an iXML with a NUL inside, chunks past the end, a read-only file and an unknown CatID are refused and left byte for byte; the window writes two sounds, skips one with neither (left byte for byte), rescans, and the index keeps the category and tag. On copies of the first two WAV files in each top folder of a real library of 658,358 files (62 copies, from many vendors and recorders; the library itself only read), 58 were written (494 chunks, every one but the iXML kept byte for byte, the category and tags read back, the audio readable) and 4 refused for a NUL or control bytes in their iXML, left as they were. The whole suite passed (1317 checks). Not tried: the menu item and its question with a real mouse in the running app.

**Milestone 283: Ratings, favourites and tags in the Sound Library**
- Each sound can have a rating (one to five stars), be marked a favourite, and carry your own tags, all kept in the index. They stay when the file changes and is read again; a file gone from its folder takes them with it.
- In the Sound Library window: a heart and five stars after each name (clicking a star rates the sound, clicking its own rating again clears it; clicking the heart marks it), a Tags column, and, with the list in front, the keys 0 to 5 to rate the selected sounds and F to mark them (or unmark them when all are). The right-click menu rates, marks and unmarks the selected sounds, adds a tag to them (Add Tag...) and removes one of theirs. Rating or tagging keeps the selection.
- A bar above the list shows only favourites, only sounds rated at least so many stars, or only those with one tag (each tag listed with how many sounds have it), together with the search and the category on the left. Sorting by the Rating column puts the best rated first.
- Tags are searched like the words of a name. A tag is one line of up to 64 characters, trimmed; tags differing only in case are the same tag.
- An index from before ratings is brought up to date when it opens; nothing is read again.
- Measured by the tests: ratings (clamped to 5), favourites and tags (trimmed, one line, at most 64 characters, the same in any case, never empty) are kept per sound; queries show those rated at least 3 stars, the favourites, a tag in any case, and favourites with a tag; searching finds tags; sorting by rating puts 5 stars first; a changed file read again keeps them all and a removed tag leaves its search words; a file gone leaves no tags behind; an index from M282 opens and takes them; in the window, rating keeps the selection, the 5 key rates and F marks and unmarks, clicking the second star rates 2 and again clears it, clicking the heart marks, and the bar's tag, rating and favourites filters show 1 of 3 each. On an index of a million sounds, rating 100,000, marking 10,000 and tagging 5,000 took 0.73 s; counting those rated 4 stars or more took 46.4 ms, the favourites 0.2 ms, the tagged 5.6 ms (with "creak" 37.8 ms), and the first page of the tagged 6.3 ms. The whole suite passed (1313 checks). Not tried: the stars, heart, keys, menu and filter bar with a real mouse and keyboard in the running app.

**Milestone 282: UCS categories in the Sound Library**
- The Sound Library knows the Universal Category System's categories (v8.2.1, its final list: 753 subcategories in 82 categories, each with its CatID, explanation and synonyms), bundled with the app. The list is UCS's own spreadsheet converted by `Scripts/ucs_convert.py` (standard library only; the source's SHA-256 is recorded in the converted file).
- A scan gives each sound its category from its name when that follows UCS (CatID_FXName_CreatorID_SourceID: the CatID before the first underscore, in any case), or else from its WAV file's iXML: USER CATID, USER CATEGORY and SUBCATEGORY, or the ASWG catId.
- On the left of the window, the categories: All Sounds, No Category, then each category, opening into its subcategories, each with how many of the sounds found (by the search) are in it. Choosing one shows only those sounds. A Category column names each sound's, and sorts by it (those with none last). Searching also finds a sound by its category's names, so "doors" finds DOORWood_Creak.
- Right-clicking rows sets their category by hand (a menu of the categories and their subcategories), or clears what was set by hand. A category set by hand stays when the file changes and is read again; cleared, the sound has its name's or iXML's again.
- An index from before categories is brought up to date when it opens, and its next scan reads every file again, once, to find their categories.
- On the 658,228 sounds of the library M281 was measured on: 15,339 have a category from their UCS names (12,158 with the CatID exactly as UCS writes it, the rest in another case) and the rest none; the files there whose iXML gives a category also have UCS names, which win (three of them copied under plain names took their categories from their iXML). The scan took 1,417 seconds, as fast as without categories, into an index of 462 MB; counting the whole library by category took 11.6 ms (8.9 ms for the sounds matching "door"), and listing DOORS (58 sounds) 0.3 ms.
- Not read: Soundminer's own metadata chunk (SMED), which most of the files in the library this was tried on carry; its format is not published.
- Measured by the tests: the bundled list holds 753 subcategories in 82 categories with synonyms, DOORWood is DOORS / WOOD (found in any case and by its names) and the 8.2.1 fix is in; UCS names give their CatIDs and plain names none; iXML gives USER CATID, USER CATEGORY and SUBCATEGORY, or the ASWG catId, and an unknown CatID none; a scan of seven files takes each one's category from its name or iXML (the name winning); queries by category (AIR: 3), subcategory and none, counts by category, "doors" finding the 3 doors by their category, sorting with the uncategorised last; categories set by hand stay through a rescan of changed files and clear back to the file's own; an index made before categories opens, gains the columns and reads its files again; in the window, the category list's counts follow the search, choosing a category or subcategory shows only those, the Category column names it, and setting a selected sound's category moves it. The whole suite passed (1307 checks). Not tried: the category list, its counts and the right-click menu in the running app.

**Milestone 281: Sound Library preview**
- The Sound Library window previews the sound selected in its list below the list: its whole waveform, with a playhead moving across it as it plays. With Play on Select on (the default) a sound plays as soon as it is selected; Play/Stop and the space bar (unless you are typing in the search box) play and stop it; Loop loops it; a volume slider (-40 to +6 dB) sets the preview's level only.
- Clicking the waveform plays from there. Pressing the strip along its top and dragging scrubs: the sound plays from wherever the mouse is.
- Dragging across the waveform selects a part, which then plays (and loops) alone. Dragging from inside the selection carries only that part out, as a WAV of its own at the sound's rate, channels and bit depth (float when the sound is), named after the sound and the part's times, into the main window's multitrack view or editor, the Finder or another app. Dragging rows of the list still carries the whole sounds. The parts are kept in a Parts folder beside the index; the same part dragged again uses the file already written.
- The preview plays through the app's audio device alongside whatever else plays, reading the file through a buffer filled on a thread of its own, so the audio thread never waits for the disk. A sound at the device's rate plays sample for sample; one at another rate is resampled; mono plays in both channels; 5.1 and 7.1 are folded down to stereo.
- Scanning is faster: headers are read through a buffer (the readers ask for a few bytes at a time, and unbuffered each ask was a read from the disk) and eight files at a time. On a 5.4 TB library of 658,358 audio files on a drive attached to the Mac, the old scan read 79,401 files in its first 651 seconds; the new one indexed all 658,228 it could read (10 could not be read) in 1,423 seconds (24 minutes; the third pass over that library that day, so some of it may have been in the disk cache), into an index of 452 MB, and a rescan with nothing changed took 45.5 seconds. Searches on that index: "door" counted 27,013 sounds in 1.1 ms and fetched its first page in 6.7 ms, "door creak" 1,977 in 1.1 ms (2.0 ms), "footsteps gravel" 764 in 0.6 ms (1.1 ms), and a page halfway down the whole list sorted by name took 7.6 ms.
- Fixed: a scan held a read of the index open the whole time, so SQLite could never move what it wrote from its log into the index: stopped after 79,401 files, the index file was still 4 KB beside a 183 MB log. Statements are now reset as soon as they are read and a scan ends by emptying the log; on the whole library the log stayed under 4.5 MB (sampled every 30 seconds) while the index grew.
- `SpectraBladeTests --library <folder>` runs that check on any folder: a new index in a temporary folder, a scan, a second scan, searches, the index deleted afterwards; it only reads the library.
- Measured by the tests: a stereo sound at the device's rate plays sample for sample and stops at its end, silent after; a part (frames 1000 to 3000) loops sample for sample; -6 dB of preview volume plays at 0.501 of the level; mono plays the same in both channels and 5.1 as the fold-down gives it; a 44.1 kHz second on a 48 kHz device plays for 48,000 device frames; a file that can't be read plays silence (and no tail of the sound before it); a part of a 96 kHz 24-bit sound is written at its rate, channels and bits, sample for sample, and asked again gives the same file unwritten, and a float sound's part is float; with the mouse, dragging across a quarter of a waveform selects frames 12000 to 24000, dragging from inside the selection carries out once a file of just those frames, a click outside drops the selection and plays from there, and the strip at the top scrubs; selecting a row shows its sound in the preview and a search keeps it there; after a scan SQLite's log is empty. The whole suite passed (1300 checks). Not tried: hearing the preview through an interface in the running app, the space bar in the window, and dropping a dragged part or sound into the main window with a real mouse.

**Milestone 280: Sound Library window**
- Window > Sound Library opens the Sound Library in a window of its own, which reopens where it was left (on any screen); closing it hides it, and a scan goes on.
- It scans only the folders you choose: it starts with none and says so, Add Folder... adds one (scanning it at once), and the Folders menu rescans them all, removes one or shows it in the Finder. A folder that is missing, already there, or inside or around one that is, is refused, saying why.
- Scans run on a thread of their own, the table following them every 2 seconds and the line below counting the files looked at, then saying what the scan added, updated, removed and couldn't read.
- The table lists the sounds a page at a time from the index (so a million rows scroll like ten): name, length, rate, channels, bits, format, folder and BWF description, sorted by clicking a header. The search box narrows it to the sounds with every word typed at the start of a word of their name or description.
- Dragging selected rows carries their files out as a file drag, as from the Finder: into the multitrack view (onto a track, at a time) or the editor of the main window, the Finder or another app. Double-clicking a row opens the sound in the editor.
- The index is kept with the app's settings, in Application Support.
- Measured by the tests: with no folders the window scans nothing and says to add one; a missing folder is refused, saying why; an added folder is scanned on the scanning thread and its 10 sounds listed ("10 sounds in 1 folder. Last scan: 10 added, 0 updated, 0 removed"); "creak" shows one row reading 0.50 s, 48 kHz, 2 channels, 24 bits, WAV, its folder and description; sorted by length the longest (1.27 s) comes first, and by rate 44.1, 96 and 192 kHz in order; a drag starting on one of 3 selected rows hands those 3 files out once it has moved 6 pixels, and one starting on a row not selected hands out nothing; Rescan adds a new file; removing the folder empties the list. The whole suite passed (1290 checks). Not tried: the window in the running app (opening it, its place kept, the Add Folder chooser and Folders menu), and dropping dragged sounds with a real mouse into the main window, the Finder or another app.

**Milestone 279: Sound Library index**
- The groundwork for the Sound Library, with nothing to see in the app yet: the window that uses it is the next milestone.
- An SQLite index (the one built into macOS, with its full-text search) of the audio files in the folders you choose to watch, and only those: it starts with none and never picks a folder itself. A folder inside or around one already watched is refused.
- A scan reads only each file's header (format, length, rate, channels, bit depth and BWF description), and on later scans only files that are new or whose size or modification time changed; files gone from a watched folder leave the index. It can be stopped, keeping what it did and removing nothing.
- Searching finds the start of words in a sound's name and description, every word needed. Names are split into words at punctuation, case changes ("DOORWood" gives "DOOR" and "Wood") and between letters and digits, so "wood" finds DOORWood_Creak. Results come a page at a time, sorted by name, folder, length, rate, channels, format or date.
- Measured by the tests: a scan of a chosen folder indexes its 30 WAV files in nested folders with their header fields and BWF descriptions, skips a text file and counts a broken WAV as unreadable; a missing folder, one already watched or one inside it is refused; "wood" finds DOORWood_Creak, "door creak" one sound, "door gravel" none, "loose" a sound by its description, "grav" the start of a word, and quotes or SQL in a search are only words; pages of 7 by length list all 30 once in order; a rescan with nothing changed reads no file again, and with one file rewritten, one deleted and one new updates, removes and adds just those; a stopped scan removes nothing; a second connection reads the index meanwhile; removing the folder takes its sounds out, kept when the index is opened again. On an index of 1,000,000 sounds with UCS-like names (made in 22.8 s, not scanned from disk): counting all 9.6 ms, counting "creak" (100,152) 2.3 ms, "wood creak" (9,771) 4.1 ms, the first page of "wood creak" by name 13.6 ms, page 5,001 of everything by name 10.0 ms, the longest 100 of "creak" 38.1 ms. The whole suite passed (1286 checks). Not tried: scanning a real library of that size from disk, and how long that takes.

**Milestone 278: Batch delivery report as a file**
- When Batch Process brings files to a delivery spec, it also writes "Delivery Report <date> <time>.txt" in the batch's folder (or beside the first file written), numbered " 2", " 3"... when that name is taken. The report gives how many files meet the spec, then each file's report as Meet Delivery Spec shows it (integrated loudness and true peak with PASS or FAIL, the range, the loudest momentary and short-term loudness and the sample peak, each beside what it was before) and the file it was written as, or why a file wasn't written. The batch's summary says where the report is.
- With a spec as the level, Batch now calls the same step as Meet Delivery Spec directly, rather than through the sequence step, so it keeps the measurements; the audio and the notes are the same.
- Measured by the tests: two files batched to the -14 LUFS spec and one that couldn't be read give "Delivery Report 2026-11-06 210509.txt" in the batch's folder ("... 210509 2.txt" the second time), starting "Delivery report: 2 of 3 files meet Online -14 LUFS.", with each file's PASS lines and the file it was written as, and "gone.wav: not written. It can't be read."; no report for another level, or when no file was written; the batched file still measures -14.0 LUFS, true peak -10.6 dBTP, with the same notes as before. The whole suite passed (1279 checks). Not tried: a batch to a spec in the running app, and the summary's line saying where the report is.

**Milestone 277: Custom delivery specs**
- Meet Delivery Spec offers a custom spec after the four fixed ones: a target from -70 to 0 LUFS, a tolerance from 0.1 to 10 LU and a true-peak ceiling from -30 to 0 dBTP, set in the same dialog. It is kept in the settings, and Mix Down, Export Stems and Batch Process offer it too, with its values shown. Until one is set it is -20 LUFS +/- 1 LU at -1 dBTP.
- A recorded Meet Delivery Spec step, and Batch Process, refuse a spec out of those ranges, where a step used to take any tolerance from 0 to 10 LU.
- Measured by the tests: the ranges hold at their edges and refuse just past them (-70.1 and +0.1 LUFS, 0.05 and 10.1 LU, -30.1 and +0.1 dBTP); a custom spec of -20 LUFS +/- 1 LU at most -2 dBTP brings a programme with clicks to full scale to -20.0 LUFS, true peak -2.1 dBTP, through the limiter, and its report reads "Meets Custom spec."; Batch Process refuses a tolerance of 0.05 LU or a ceiling of +0.5 dBTP, saying the ranges. The whole suite passed (1278 checks). Not tried: setting the custom spec in the Meet Delivery Spec dialog, and choosing it in Mix Down, Export Stems and Batch Process, in the running app.

**Milestone 276: Reverse Clip keeps takes**
- Reverse Clip now keeps a clip's takes, as Stretch Clip does since Milestone 273: each take's part under the clip is reversed too, written to a file of its own, and kept lined up, so choosing another take afterwards plays it back to front in the same place. Before, the other takes were dropped.
- Measured by the tests: a clip with three takes (one shorter than the clip) reversed keeps all three, the one playing still chosen; choosing the first afterwards plays what the clip played of it, sample for sample back to front, in the same place and length; takes of other lengths are refused. The whole suite passed (1276 checks). Not tried: Reverse Clip on a clip with takes in the running app.

**Milestone 275: Loudness of 7.1 audio**
- Fixed: eight-channel (7.1) audio was measured with every channel weighted 1.0, the LFE included, so 7.1 loudness (in Loudness Statistics, Match Loudness, Meet Delivery Spec, and 7.1 mixdowns and stems to a spec) read wrong whenever the LFE or the side surrounds had sound. Now it is weighted as BS.1770-4 places the channels: the LFE left out, the side surrounds (at 90 degrees) counted 1.41 times (+1.5 dB), the rear surrounds (at 135 degrees) and front channels once. 5.1 was already weighted this way.
- EBU's test signals (Tech 3341) have no 7.1 case, so the checks below are against BS.1770's sum.
- Measured by the tests, each channel alone with a 1 kHz sine at -20 dBFS: the 7.1 LFE alone does not count; each side surround reads -21.5 LUFS (BS.1770: -21.51); a rear surround and a front channel read -23.0 LUFS (BS.1770: -23.00); all eight together read -14.1 LUFS (BS.1770: -14.07). The 5.1 checks still pass. The whole suite passed (1275 checks). Not tried: a real 7.1 recording measured against another loudness meter.

**Milestone 274: Ducking on 7.1 tracks, tested**
- No change to how it works: ducking on 5.1 and 7.1 tracks, and with them as the key, was there since those tracks were, but untested. Now it is tested.
- Measured by the tests: in a 7.1 master, a 7.1 track ducked under a stereo voice comes down on all eight channels by the gains a steady stereo track ducked the same way does (within 0.000000030), and a 5.1 track on all six (within 0.000000028), both to -12.00 dB, and untouched bit for bit before the voice; a 7.1 track as the key ducks a stereo track as a stereo key playing its fold-down does (within 0.000000030, to -12.00 dB). The whole suite passed (1269 checks). Not tried: ducking on surround tracks in the running app.

**Milestone 273: Keeping takes when a clip is processed**
- Stretch Clip, Shift Clip Pitch and stretching by Option-drag now keep a clip's takes. Each take is processed the same way from the part of it the clip plays (silence where a take is shorter than the clip), written to a file of its own beside the active one's, and kept lined up, so choosing another take afterwards plays it in the same place and at the same length. Before, the other takes were dropped.
- The Pitch Editor keeps them too: the notes moved belong to the take playing, so only that take gets the new audio, and the others play just what they did, from their own files.
- Measured by the tests: a clip with three takes (the one playing chosen after a trim, one take shorter than the clip) shows each take as it plays it, sample for sample, with silence past the short take's end; stretched 1.5 times it keeps all three, each the stretched part of its own take, lined up, so choosing another plays it in the same place at the new length; takes of another length are refused; with notes moved, the active take is replaced and the other two play the same frames in the same place as before, also once chosen; the Pitch Editor's clip test (B3 +100.1 cents, A3 0.0 cents) passes through the new path. The whole suite passed (1267 checks). Not tried: Stretch Clip, Shift Clip Pitch, stretching by drag and the Pitch Editor on a clip with takes in the running app, and choosing a take there afterwards.

**Milestone 272: Meet Delivery Spec in sequences and Batch Process**
- Meet Delivery Spec is recorded as a sequence step, which keeps the spec's numbers (target, tolerance and true-peak ceiling), so a saved sequence plays the same on any machine. It is named after its spec ("Meet EBU R128"), and when played it notes what the result measures and whether it meets the spec.
- Batch Process's Level can be a delivery spec too: each file is brought to it as Meet Delivery Spec does, and the batch report says, for each file, what it measures and whether it meets the spec.
- Measured by the tests: the step gives Meet Delivery Spec's result bit for bit (one of the 35 steps checked against their commands); it is named "Meet EBU R128", or "Meet Delivery spec -20.0 LUFS" for numbers that match no spec, and refuses a tolerance past 10 LU; played on a part of a file it notes "Meet EBU R128: meets it at -23.0 LUFS, true peak -16.2 dBTP", and on silence it leaves the audio with a note; a batch to the -14 LUFS spec writes a file measuring -14.0 LUFS, true peak -10.6 dBTP, and its report says it meets it. The whole suite passed (1265 checks). Not tried: recording the step from the menu, and the Batch Process dialog's spec choices, in the running app; a batch file that needs the limiter (the limiter path is the one tested in Milestone 270).

**Milestone 271: Delivery specs on mixdowns and stems**
- Mix Down has a Loudness choice: as mixed, or one of the delivery specs. With a spec, the mix is brought to it as Meet Delivery Spec brings a file (gain, and the true-peak limiter only where gain alone would pass the ceiling) before it is written, and the report follows, with Save Report... suggesting a name beside the mixdown.
- Export Stems and Export Folder Stems ask the same before they start. Stems can't each be limited and still add up to the mix, so every stem gets one gain: the one that brings the whole mix to the spec's loudness. The mix is rendered once more to measure it, and the report gives the mix's lines with that gain; where its true peak would pass the ceiling, the report fails that line and says the mix would need limiting. Stems are written as 32-bit float, so the gain does not clip them.
- Measured by the tests: two stems to EBU R128 each get 9.38 dB, the one gain that brings their mix from -32.4 LUFS to -23.0 LUFS, and summed they differ from the gained mix by at most 0.000000016, with a report that meets the spec; stems whose mix would pass -1 dBTP at -14 LUFS are reported as failing the true peak and needing limiting, and are not limited; a silent mix is refused. The mixdown uses the same step as Meet Delivery Spec, tested in Milestone 270. The whole suite passed (1263 checks). Not tried: the Mix Down and Export Stems dialogs in the running app, the files they write with a spec, and Save Report from them.

**Milestone 270: Loudness to a delivery spec**
- Effects > Meet Delivery Spec... brings the selection (or the whole file) to a loudness spec in one step, as one step to undo: EBU R128 (-23 LUFS +/- 0.5 LU, true peak at most -1 dBTP), ATSC A/85 (-24 LKFS +/- 2 dB, at most -2 dBTP), or an online target of -16 or -14 LUFS (+/- 1 LU, at most -1 dBTP). The online targets are common published ones, not any one service's current rules.
- It uses gain alone where that keeps the true peak under the ceiling, and otherwise gain into the true-peak limiter Match Loudness uses, set 0.1 dB under the ceiling so the result measures within it.
- Then it measures the result and shows a report: integrated loudness and true peak, each PASS or FAIL against the spec, with the loudness range, the loudest momentary and short-term loudness and the sample peak for information, each beside what it was before. Save Report... writes it as a text file.
- Measured by the tests: the specs' values; -22.5 LUFS at -1.0 dBTP passes EBU R128 and -22.4 LUFS or -0.9 dBTP fails on that line alone; a quiet programme at -33.1 LUFS meets EBU R128 with 10.08 dB of gain alone (-23.0 LUFS, true peak -17.2 dBTP), the report passing each line and showing the loudness before; a programme with clicks to full scale at -23.0 LUFS and +0.8 dBTP meets -14 LUFS through the limiter (-14.0 LUFS, true peak -1.1 dBTP); on a selection only it changes, the rest bit for bit; silence is refused. The whole suite passed (1261 checks). Not tried: the dialog, the report window and Save Report in the running app, and ATSC A/85 and -16 LUFS on audio (only their values are checked).

**Milestone 269: Stretching session clips by dragging**
- Option-dragging a clip's end (or start) in the multitrack view stretches it instead of trimming it: while dragging, an outline shows where it would play with its new length as a percentage; let go, and Stretch Clip runs on it (the same time stretch, in the background, its own file), as one step to undo. Dragged by the end it keeps its start; by the start, its end. It snaps as trimming does, and goes from half to twice its length, as Stretch Clip does.
- A drag that ends at the length it began with does nothing, and without Option an edge still trims.
- Measured by the tests: Option-dragging a 2 s clip's end to 4 s shows it ending at 3.998 s (to the pixel) without changing the session, and letting go asks for a stretch of 1.4988 keeping its start; from its start back to 0 s, 1.4988 keeping its end; past twice its length, exactly 2; a drag out and back to where it began asks nothing; without Option the edge trims. A clip stretched by 1.25 from its start ends where it did, 2.500 s long, and one that would start before 0 is refused. The whole suite passed (1257 checks). Not tried: snapping while stretching, the half-length limit, and the drag and the stretch it starts in the running app.

**Milestone 268: The Pitch Editor on session clips**
- In a session, Analyse in the Pitch Editor reads the selected clip (unlocked): all of it that plays, or the part under the time selection when there is one across it (up to 30 s, as for files). Its notes move, split, join and move in time as in a file.
- Apply moves them in the part of the clip's audio that plays, writes that to a file of its own (beside recordings, as Shift Clip Pitch does) and has the clip play it from then on, in the same place, as long, with its fades and gain envelope, as one step to undo. Like Shift Clip Pitch, the clip's other takes go. If the clip's audio, offset or length changes before Apply, or while it works, nothing is applied and it asks for a new analysis.
- Measured by the tests: the part worked on is what the clip plays after its offset, or the part under a time selection across it, and all it plays when the selection misses it; in a clip playing a melody from 0.3 s into its audio, B3 moved up a semitone plays 100.1 cents higher from the clip's own copy, A3 beside it 0.0 cents from where it was, and the clip keeps its place, length and fades. The whole suite passed (1254 checks). Not tried: Analyse and Apply on a clip in the running app, including refusing a clip that changed after analysis.

**Milestone 267: Effects while scrubbing a session**
- Scrubbing a stopped session now plays the mix through its track and bus effects, in time with any latency they have. The roadmap had planned only effects with no memory or latency; instead every effect is run, as follows.
- While the head moves forward, the mix is rendered on from where it was last rendered, so the effects run through it unbroken, as when playing from where the scrub began; the stretch most recently rendered is kept (at least twice the scrubber's widest window), so going back over it plays what was rendered. Further back, or a jump of more than 4096 frames ahead, resets the effects and runs them over the 4096 frames (85 ms at 48 kHz) before the window first. Effects whose memory is shorter than that, such as EQs and short look-ahead, sound as when played; reverb tails and slow releases are heard only with what those frames leave in them.
- An edit while scrubbing is heard from the next block, and playing after a scrub starts the effects afresh. While input is being monitored, scrubbing leaves the effects out, as before, since they are busy with the input.
- Measured by the tests: noise through an EQ (which changes it by up to 0.25), scrubbed forward, back and fast ahead in blocks of 256, against scrubbing the file of the mix as played from where the scrub first primed its effects: within 0.000000000 forward, and within 0.000000000 back and ahead, where it went past what was kept and the EQ was warmed up again; an effect with 240 frames of latency scrubs bit for bit as with no effect; a volume change made mid-scrub is heard from the next block (within 0.000000000 of scrubbing it changed from the start); playing after a scrub is bit for bit playing without one; a memoryless effect (a gain of a half) is heard, or left out when asked. A second of scrubbing 16 tracks, each through an EQ and a compressor, at four times normal speed took 23.0 ms forward and 134.7 ms back on this Mac in the whole suite's run (25.8 and 151.7 ms in an earlier run). The whole suite passed (1252 checks). Not tried: scrubbing with effects by hand in the running app, and effects with long memory (reverbs) by ear.

**Milestone 266: Automation in track templates**
- Save Track as Template now keeps the track's envelopes too: volume, pan, surround pan and its effects' parameters. Added from the template, the new track has them at the same times; an effect's envelope keeps its slot, the one the template's effect loads into, as a session file's effects and envelopes do. In a session at another sample rate each point moves to the same time there (to the nearest frame). A track with no envelopes writes none, so its template is as before.
- Session files write and read envelopes with the same code as before, moved where templates can share it; the file format is unchanged.
- Measured by the tests: a track's volume, pan, surround pan and effect envelopes come back through a template file exactly, the effect's envelope on the slot id the template's effect has; added at twice the sample rate every point is at twice the frame with the same value, at 44.1 kHz rounded to the nearest frame; a track with no envelopes writes none and no rate. Session files, which now use the same envelope code, keep passing all their earlier checks. The whole suite passed (1247 checks). Not tried: adding a template with automation in the running app.

**Milestone 265: 7.1 tracks**
- A track's header menu has 7.1 Track beside 5.1 Track (one step to undo; not on buses, folders or frozen tracks). A 7.1 track plays its clips' eight channels, L R C LFE Lrs Rrs Lss Rss, through its effects, polarity, volume and ducking, without pan, straight to a 7.1 master's speakers. A 5.1 clip on it plays its surrounds from the side pair, as a 5.1 track does in a 7.1 master; a mono clip from C and a stereo one from L and R.
- In a 5.1 master a 7.1 track's L, R, C and LFE play as they are and each side's rear and side surround go to Ls or Rs at -3 dB each; in a stereo master, into a bus, a send or an interface pair, it is folded down as a 7.1 file is.
- Its effects run as four stereo copies (L R, C LFE, Lrs Rrs, Lss Rss); the built-in compressor and limiter link all eight. A 5.1 track made 7.1 gets a third copy of each effect, and a mixdown makes its own.
- Eight-channel audio brought onto a new track in a surround master makes a 7.1 track. The session file and track templates keep it, it freezes to eight channels, and an eight-channel bounce lands on a 7.1 track. Its clips draw all eight channels, named.
- Measured by the tests: a 7.1 clip's eight channels go to a 7.1 master's eight bit for bit, and a 5.1 clip's to L R C LFE and the side pair with the rears silent; volume and polarity act on all eight, the pan does nothing, and an effect with 37 frames of latency is given all eight and comes out in time (blocks of 97); in a 5.1 master the rears and sides go into Ls and Rs at -3 dB (within 1e-6), and in a stereo master the fold-down is bit for bit; import, the edits (other counts, buses and folders refused), the session file, templates and an eight-channel bounce; frozen, it plays from the file bit for bit as live, in a 7.1 and a 5.1 master; an EQ and a phaser in four pairs are bit for bit the same effects on stereo tracks, two copies become three when a 5.1 track becomes 7.1, the compressor and limiter linking eight are bit for bit compressing and limiting the eight as one file, and a mixdown's three copies give playback's eight channels bit for bit; in the app a 7.1 track's effect has three copies, two once it is 5.1 and none in stereo. The whole suite passed (1246 checks). Not tried: the header menu and the eight-channel clip drawing in the running app, ducking on a 7.1 track, and listening on 7.1 speakers.

**Milestone 264: A 7.1 master**
- Multitrack > 7.1 Master mixes the session to 7.1 (L R C LFE Lrs Rrs Lss Rss, as a 7.1 file is ordered), beside 5.1 Master; choosing the one that is on goes back to stereo, and each is one step to undo. The session file keeps it.
- Each track's and bus's surround pad then places it among seven speakers: L and R 30 degrees either side of the front, C in front, the side surrounds at 90 and the rear ones at 135 degrees, as ITU-R BS.2051 places 7.1, and the pad draws them so. The pan works as in 5.1 (constant power between the two speakers either side, the rest spread evenly as the point nears the middle, the width and LFE share as before); as it starts, left still goes to L and right to R alone.
- A 5.1 track in a 7.1 master plays L, R, C and LFE to the same channels and its Ls and Rs from the side surrounds, as 5.1's surrounds are usually placed in 7.1; the rear surrounds get nothing from it. Frozen or bounced, it stays 5.1.
- The master plays folded down to stereo as a 7.1 file is (milestone 263), or to separate outputs with C to Rss on the six outputs after the pair, through the effects rack as 5.1's channels go (three pairs of copies, the compressor and limiter linking all eight). A mixdown writes eight channels. The mixer's master meter has eight named bars.
- Measured by the tests: as it starts, a 7.1 master's L and R are the stereo mix bit for bit and the other six silent; over 200 random points the pan keeps the power, in the middle it spreads evenly over all seven speakers, at the left side it plays from Lss alone, and straight behind from Lrs and Rrs equally; placed tracks and a bus sound in all eight channels, played folded down they are the eight folded as a 7.1 file is, to separate outputs L and R go to the mix and C to Rss to the six outputs after the pair, and a mixdown is the eight in file order, all bit for bit, in blocks of 97 as of 512; a 5.1 track plays L, R, C and LFE to the same channels and Ls and Rs to Lss and Rss bit for bit, the rears silent, and freezes as 5.1; the master's edits and the session file; the mixer's master meter shows eight bars and Lss's level; on the 30-output test interface, a 7.1 master's six channels past L and R reach outputs 3 to 8 through an EQ's three copies and a compressor that takes them all itself. The whole suite passed (1236 checks). Not tried: listening on 7.1 speakers, and the pad and meter in the running app.

**Milestone 263: 7.1 files**
- A file of eight channels is taken as 7.1, in the order of WAV's channel mask: L R C LFE, then the rear surrounds Lrs and Rrs, then the side surrounds Lss and Rss. Its lanes are named so; Play Surround Folded Down plays it folded down to stereo, as Make Stereo (now Fold Down to Stereo for it), Mix to Mono, a batch's or a sequence's channels step do: each side gets its front channel and the centre, rear and side surround on that side at -3 dB, and the LFE is left out. Before, eight channels played as their first two, were made stereo by keeping those two, and were made mono by averaging all eight, the LFE with them.
- Saved as WAV, a 7.1 file is marked with the 7.1 speakers (mask 0x63F: front left, right, centre, LFE, back left, right, side left, right). Before, it was saved with the mask JUCE gives eight channels by default, 0xFF, which names two of them front left-of-centre and right-of-centre (the old SDDS layout), so other programs would have put the side surrounds in front.
- File > New File from Mono Files... puts eight files named ... L, R, C, LFE, Lrs, Rrs, Lss, Rss in 7.1 order; Save Channels as Mono Files... names them so.
- Measured by the tests: eight channels are named L R C LFE Lrs Rrs Lss Rss; made stereo they are each side plus the centre and its rear and side surrounds at -3 dB, bit for bit against that arithmetic done in the test, and made mono the average of the two sides; a 7.1 WAV is written with speaker mask 0x63F and reads back bit for bit (32-bit float); eight files named ... L, R, C, LFE, Lrs, Rrs, Lss, Rss go in 7.1 order whatever their order and case; a 7.1 file plays folded down on the mix bit for bit, or with its six channels past L and R on the next six player channels. The whole suite passed (1230 checks). Not tried: a 7.1 file from another program, and listening on 7.1 speakers.

**Milestone 262: Formants on large shifts in the Pitch Editor**
- Moving a note down by more than about half an octave in the Pitch Editor (or with Pitch Correction) works now. An octave down used to come out at the pitch it had, and nine semitones down with no clear pitch at all: the grains PSOLA puts back are two of the old periods long, and spaced further apart than that they left gaps, which let the audio as it was through or were scaled up from almost nothing. Where the pitch is lowered, the gaps now stay silent, as between the pulses of a lower voice, and the grains' quiet ends are no longer scaled up into the sound; raising the pitch, and leaving it, give the same samples as before.
- Measured, the voice keeps its formants where they were on large shifts, as PSOLA should (each grain carries the voice's resonance at its own frequencies): the harmonics of what comes out lie nearer the envelope the voice had than one moved with the pitch, by a wide margin raising it and by less lowering it. The Pitch Editor has no control to move formants on purpose.
- Measured by the tests: a buzz at 220 Hz with a formant at 800 Hz, moved by PSOLA, lands on its pitch and keeps its envelope: an octave down 0.01 cents off, its harmonics 2.19 dB (root mean square, up to 3 kHz) from the envelope it had against 3.64 from one moved with the pitch; a fifth down 0.00 cents, 1.14 against 2.21 dB; a fifth up 0.01 cents, 0.24 against 2.16 dB; an octave up 0.09 cents, 0.00 against 3.62 dB. Before the change, measured the same way while working on it, an octave down came out at 220 Hz and nine semitones down had no pitch the detector could find. Noise still passes through Pitch Correction as it was (within 0.00000001). The whole suite passed (1227 checks). Not tried: a real voice moved by large steps, and listening.

**Milestone 261: Moving notes in time in the Pitch Editor**
- Dragging a note sideways in the Pitch Editor moves it in time. It keeps its pitch and its length; a note touching it before is stretched or squeezed to meet it, as is one touching it after, and the audio between notes that don't touch (a pause, a breath) is squeezed or stretched to fill, so the selection keeps its length. A drag moves a note in pitch or in time, whichever way it first goes (4 points from where it was pressed), so an up or down drag doesn't nudge it in time. Moves are limited so that nothing is squeezed past the next note or stretched or squeezed by more than four times; a drag past that stops there.
- Apply moves the notes in time and pitch together as one step to undo. Time is moved the way pitch is (PSOLA): each period of the output takes the grain of the input from the moment it now plays, so periods are repeated or left out rather than played faster or slower. Reset puts notes back in time as well as pitch. A split piece of a moved note stays where it was shown; a joined note moves as the first of the two did.
- Measured by the tests: in a test melody, C4 moved 100 ms later keeps its pitch (0.02 cents off); B3 before it, stretched to meet it, is still B3 where C4 used to start (25.5 cents flat over 80 ms of its vibrato); C4 now sounds on to 1.6 s (1.01 of its level), the pause after it is squeezed to 100 ms (silent), and the last note stays as it was (0.00 cents, 1.000 of its level), the length unchanged; C4 moved 300 ms later (through the 200 ms pause) is refused, as is the last note moved earlier until the pause is under a quarter of its length; in the panel, dragging C4 sideways moves it 20 frames (100 ms) and not in pitch, dragged on into the pause it stops where the pause is a quarter of its length, a drag mostly up moves only the pitch, and Reset puts every note back. The whole suite passed (1226 checks). Not tried by hand: dragging in the running app, and listening to moved notes in a real voice.

**Milestone 260: Splitting and joining notes in the Pitch Editor**
- Cmd-clicking a note in the Pitch Editor cuts it in two where it is clicked, so part of a note can be moved on its own (a sung note that drifts flat at its end, say). Right-clicking a note offers Split Here, Join with Previous Note and Join with Next Note, each where it can: a piece is at least 10 ms long, and only notes that touch (no gap without pitch between them) join.
- Each piece takes the pitch of its own frames, as Analyse gives a note's, and keeps the note's shift; a joined note takes the pitch of all its frames and the shift of the longer of the two. Where pieces with different shifts meet, the shift eases over 25 ms, as between any two notes. Reset leaves the splits and joins as they are.
- Measured by the tests: B3 of a test melody (sung with vibrato) split in the middle is two notes at 58.92 and 58.93, each with the note's shift; splits within 10 ms of an end are refused; joined again it is bit for bit the note as found, and two halves with different shifts join with the longer one's; notes with a pause between them don't join; with the second half moved up a semitone and the first left, the first sounds as it was (0.00 cents) and the second a semitone higher (0.87 cents off); in the panel, Cmd-clicking B3 splits it, and the right-click menu of a half offers Split Here and Join with Previous Note, which makes it one note again. The whole suite passed (1223 checks). Not tried by hand: the menu showing in the running app, and listening.

**Milestone 259: Linking surround in the effects rack**
- The built-in compressor and limiter in the effects rack now hear all of a 5.1 master's or file's channels at once when they play to separate outputs (Play Surround to Separate Outputs), and turn them all down together, as on a 5.1 track since milestone 258. Each takes the channels past L and R itself (its input and output made as wide as the channels, up to eight), so it gets no copies; folded down, or with a stereo master or file, it is two wide again. Other effects in the rack still run on each pair through copies of their own (milestone 256), and the pairs go from those copies into a linking effect and out of it, in the rack's order.
- Applying the rack to a file of more than two channels does the same: the compressor and limiter take all the channels at once, the other effects each pair, so what is applied is what was heard.
- Measured by the tests: applying a compressor and a limiter in the rack to a 5.1 file is bit for bit compressing and then limiting the six as one file, and the two playing with their buses six wide, as the engine makes them, give the same samples 240 frames (the look-ahead) later, in blocks of 512; an EQ and a phaser applied to a 5.1 file still give each pair what they give a stereo file of it; on the 30-output test interface, a 5.1 master's C, LFE, Ls and Rs go through an EQ's copies into a compressor and a limiter with no copies of their own, and out of the limiter to outputs 3 to 6, straight into them without the EQ, and a compressor folded down to stereo is two wide again. The whole suite passed (1220 checks). Not tried: hearing it through a real multichannel interface (the tests check the connections and the effects' samples, not sound coming out of the device).

**Milestone 258: Linked surround dynamics**
- The built-in compressor and limiter on a 5.1 track now hear all six channels at once and turn them down together: the gain each frame comes from the loudest of the six, and the same gain goes on every channel. Before, each pair (L R, C LFE, Ls Rs) went through a copy of its own and reacted only to itself, so a loud centre turned the dialogue down without the music and effects beside it, and the image moved. Playback, freezing and mixdowns all do it this way.
- Other effects on a 5.1 track run on the three pairs as before (milestone 254), and so does the compressor with Sidechain on, since every pair then follows the same key anyway.
- Not changed: the effects rack on a 5.1 file or 5.1 session master still takes each pair through copies of its own, while playing and when applied, so the two agree. Linking there needs the rack to carry six channels; it is on the roadmap.
- Measured by the tests: a compressor and a limiter (240 frames of look-ahead) on a 5.1 track give bit for bit what compressing and then limiting the six channels as one file gives, in time, in blocks of 97; every channel gets the same gain each frame (within 0.0000002), and the loudest sample is at -1.00 dBFS under a -1 dB ceiling; a 5.1 track with them freezes to what it played live, bit for bit; a parametric EQ and a phaser still run on the three pairs, each pair bit for bit what they make of it on a stereo track; a compressor with Sidechain on, and the EQ, don't link. The whole suite passed (1218 checks). Not tried by hand: listening to it on 5.1 speakers.

**Milestone 257: Scrubbing a session**
- Dragging along the multitrack view's time ruler (the strip of times at the top) while stopped scrubs the session: its mix under the mouse plays as it moves, as dragging the editor's ruler does for a file (milestone 248), with the same feel: about 50 ms behind the mouse, no faster than four times normal speed, the level following the speed, and a 10 ms fade when let go. The cursor follows the mouse and stays where you let go. Dragging in the marker lane below the times, or over the lanes, still selects a range; while playing, the ruler works as before.
- What is scrubbed is the session's mix of its clips, through each track's volume, pan, polarity, mute and solo, the buses and folders. Track and bus effects are left out, because they can't follow a sound going backwards or jumping; a frozen track plays its frozen audio, effects and all. A 5.1 master is folded down to stereo. The sound goes out through the effects rack, as playback does.
- Measured by the tests: a track's clip scrubs bit for bit as the file of it would (dragged forward at normal speed, back at one and a half times, then fast ahead, in blocks of 256); at -6 dB of volume it is that much quieter (within 0.0000001), a muted track beside it isn't heard, and an effect on it that would halve it is left out; a frozen track scrubs its frozen audio, and a 5.1 track in a 5.1 master scrubs folded down, each bit for bit as a file of it would; the player, stopped and showing the session, plays the scrubbed mix bit for bit, and showing the file instead doesn't play the session; through the multitrack view, a drag along the time ruler while stopped scrubs with the cursor following (2.10 s to 4.20 s) and stops when let go, while a drag in the marker lane still selects a range; a second of scrubbing 32 tracks and 2 buses at four times normal speed takes 24.3 ms to work out (2.4% of real time). The whole suite passed (1216 checks). Not tried by hand: the drag in the running app, and how it sounds.

**Milestone 256: The effects rack past left and right**
- Applying the effects rack to a file of more than two channels now runs it on every channel, two at a time (L R, C LFE, Ls Rs for 5.1; a last odd channel on its own), each pair through instances of the rack's plug-ins of its own. Before, it folded a 5.1 file into the plug-ins' two inputs, wrote what came out to L and R, and left C, LFE, Ls and Rs silent; other files of more than two channels kept their first two, through the rack, and lost the rest.
- Played with Play Surround to Separate Outputs, a 5.1 master's C and LFE and its Ls and Rs (and a file's channels past the first two) now go through the rack too, as copies of each slot: another instance of the plug-in for each further pair, given the slot's state when made, taking its parameters before every block, and bypassed with it. Their latency is the slot's, so everything reaches the outputs in time. Closing an effect's window makes the copies again with its new state. A plug-in that can't be opened again is named in a note, and the outputs of that pair are silent. Folded down, the rack works on the folded stereo as before.
- Measured by the tests: a copy of a rack slot (a compressor) plays a pair bit for bit as the slot's own plug-in would, with settings set on the slot after the copy was made; bypassed with the slot it passes the pair as it is; once prepared it reports the copy's latency (240 frames for a limiter). Applying a rack of a compressor and a limiter to a 5.1 file gives each pair bit for bit what it gives a stereo file of that pair, and a three-channel file's last channel what it gives a mono file of it. On the test interface (30 outputs), a 5.1 master played to separate outputs has a copy of each rack slot for each of its other two pairs (2 for one slot, 4 for two, 2 again when one is removed), and C, LFE, Ls and Rs reach outputs 3 to 6 through them rather than straight from the player; with an empty rack they go straight there, and folded down or with a stereo master there are no copies. The whole suite passed (1211 checks). Not tried by hand: listening on separate outputs, and third-party plug-ins as copies.

**Milestone 255: Freezing 5.1 tracks**
- A 5.1 track can now be frozen (Multitrack > Freeze Track, as any track). It renders the track alone, its clips through its effects (the three stereo copies of each, as milestone 254 made them), to a six-channel file, and plays that to its six channels in place of its clips and effects; its volume, polarity, ducking and sends act after it, as on a frozen stereo track. In a stereo master it is folded down as before. Unfreeze Track plays its effects live again. While frozen it can't be made a stereo track, as before.
- Frozen audio with fewer than six channels (a file replaced by hand) plays as a clip of that many would on a 5.1 track: mono to C, stereo to L and R.
- Measured by the tests: a 5.1 track with a built-in compressor and limiter, at -4.5 dB with its polarity inverted, frozen as the app freezes (the track alone, mixed down with its effects loaded afresh) gives six channels, and plays them frozen as it played live, bit for bit, in a 5.1 master and folded down in a stereo one; two-channel frozen audio on it plays to L and R alone; the session file keeps it frozen with its six channels; unfrozen, it plays through its effects as before, bit for bit. The whole suite passed (1208 checks). Not tried by hand: the Freeze Track command on a 5.1 track in the running app (its enabling was changed, not clicked), and listening.

**Milestone 254: Effects on 5.1 tracks**
- A 5.1 track's effects now run, where before they were skipped. Each effect runs as three stereo copies with the same settings: the effect itself on L and R, and two more of it on C and LFE and on Ls and Rs. This works with every built-in effect and every plug-in. Dynamics (compressors, limiters) react to each pair on its own, not linked across all six channels. The track is read ahead by the effects' latency, as a stereo track is, so its six channels stay in time with the rest of the mix.
- The copies are made when a track becomes a 5.1 track, and for effects added to it later. They are dropped when it becomes stereo again. They take the effect's parameters before each step, so moving a knob or automating a parameter moves all three. Anything an effect keeps outside its parameters (a convolution reverb's impulse response, for one) reaches the copies when they are made and again each time the effect's window is closed. An effect that can't be opened twice more is named in a note, and its C, LFE and surround channels play silence through it.
- Mixdowns, stems and bounces make copies of their own, so they sound as playback does. A 5.1 track still can't be frozen, and the master rack still works on L and R only (both are on the roadmap).
- Measured by the tests: a built-in compressor and limiter (240 frames of look-ahead) on a 5.1 track give each pair, L R, C LFE and Ls Rs, bit for bit what the same two effects give on a stereo track holding that pair, in time, played in blocks of 97 (loudest -1.00 dBFS under the limiter's -1 dB ceiling). The copies were made when the track became 5.1 and for an effect added after, and followed settings changed after they were made. A mixdown makes its own copies and gives the same six channels, bit for bit. In the app's engine, an effect added to a 5.1 track gets its two copies, which are dropped when the track is made stereo and made again when that is undone. An effect's automation moves all three copies step by step, bit for bit, in blocks of 37. State outside the parameters reaches the copies when they are made and when refreshed, not before, and with 240 frames of latency all six channels stay in time. An effect that can't be opened again is named, and its other four channels play silence. A test effect given all six channels comes out in time with 37 frames of latency. The whole suite passed (1205 checks). Not tried by hand: effects on a 5.1 track in the running app, third-party plug-ins as copies, and listening on a 5.1 speaker system.

**Milestone 253: Pitch Editor**
- Window > Pitch Editor shows a panel for moving the notes of one voice or instrument by hand. Analyse reads the pitch of the selection (or the whole file without one, up to 30 s) and draws it as a curve over a grid of semitones, split into notes. Drag a note up or down to move it to whole semitones, or by cents with Option held; double-click a note to snap it to the nearest semitone, or Snap to Notes to snap them all; Reset puts them back. Each moved note shows its shift in cents, and the curve shows the pitch as it will be over the pitch as it is.
- Apply moves the notes in the file as one step to undo (Move Notes), in the background, with the same PSOLA as Pitch Correction; the shift eases from one note to the next over 25 ms. If the audio has changed since the analysis, Apply asks for a new one instead. It works in the editor, not on sessions, and isn't recorded into command sequences.
- Notes are found in each run of the pitch, split where the pitch (the median of five frames) stays more than 0.6 of a semitone from the note's for 20 ms, so vibrato and small slides stay in one note; a note's pitch is the median of its frames'.
- Pitch Correction now shares its moving of the pitch with the Pitch Editor; it reaches the same notes as before (measured below).
- Measured by the tests, on a voice-like melody at 48 kHz (A3, B3 with a 30-cent vibrato, C4, a pause, and a note at 228 Hz): four notes are found, at 57.00, 58.92, 60.00 and 57.62 (MIDI numbers; within 5 cents of each, 8 for the one with vibrato, whose median over its 2.5 swings isn't its centre). B3 moved up a semitone sounds a semitone higher (0.71 cents off), the 228 Hz note snapped moves 38.2 cents to A#3 (0.01 cents off), and the notes left alone stay (0.03 and 0.02 cents); the shift changes by at most a fifth of a step a frame between notes; with nothing moved a note stays (0.02 cents); a pitch track that doesn't reach the range, and an empty range, are refused; 30 s of a held note is analysed and found as one note in 0.15 s. The panel, driven with synthetic mouse events: it shows the four notes, a drag of about one and a half semitones lands a note on a whole semitone (107.8 cents for that note), an Option drag of 0.37 of a semitone's height moves it 35.6 cents, a double-click snaps one (38.2 cents), Apply hands the notes over, and once the audio has changed Apply asks for a new analysis instead. Pitch Correction's results are the same as in milestone 252. The whole suite passed (1199 checks). The panel was drawn and checked by eye from the tests (rendered to an image, not in the app's window); dragging in the running app, and how the moved notes sound, were not tried; tested on synthetic sounds only.

**Milestone 252: Pitch Correction**
- Effects > Pitch Correction... moves the pitch of one voice or instrument at a time (a singer, a solo line) to the nearest notes of a scale, in the selection or the whole file, keeping its length.
