You choose the key and the scale (chromatic, major or natural minor; A = 440 Hz), the speed (0 to 500 ms: how long a correction takes to settle, 0 at once, slower keeping more of the slides and vibrato) and the strength (0 to 100%: how much of the way to the note). It is one step to undo, runs in the background with a progress window, and can be recorded into command sequences (and so run in Batch Process).
- The pitch is found with YIN every 5 ms, from 60 to 1000 Hz, from the mix of the channels; moments with no clear period (breaths, consonants, noise, silence) are left as they are. A note keeps its target until the pitch strays a tenth of a semitone past halfway to the next, so vibrato near the middle doesn't flip between two notes, and each note's correction eases in from its start over the speed.
- The pitch is moved by PSOLA: grains two periods long, cut a period apart from the loudest point of each note's first period, are put back closer together or further apart, each at its exact place between frames (read with a cubic curve), every channel the same. A selection's edges ease from the audio as it was over 10 ms.
- Measured by the tests, on a voice-like buzz (30 harmonics shaped by a formant) at 48 kHz: its pitch is found at 98, 220, 440.5 and 830 Hz as 98.00, 220.00, 440.51 and 829.89 Hz (0.23 cents at worst); white noise has a pitch in 0 of 200 frames, silence in none. A note at 228 Hz goes to A#3 in the chromatic scale (0.01 cents off) and to A3 in C major (0.03 cents off), half way at half strength (19.1 cents for 19.1), its level kept (0.03 dB). A glide from 200 to 260 Hz corrected at once is within 5 cents of a note in 549 of 560 frames; corrected over 200 ms, a note 38 cents flat is still 2.1 cents from where it was 30 ms in and 0.01 cents from A# by 1.5 s. A note in tune stays in tune (0.01 cents); noise passes as it was (within 0.00000001); a range moves the pitch in it (0.01 cents from A#) and leaves the audio outside bit for bit; settings out of range are refused and it can be stopped. Recorded into a sequence, it gives the command's own result bit for bit. The whole suite passed (1194 checks). Tested on synthetic sounds only, not on real singing, and not listened to; whether a voice's formants are kept was not measured; the dialog was not tried by hand.

**Milestone 251: DTMF Tones**
- Effects > Generate DTMF Tones... puts telephone signalling tones for typed digits in at the cursor, or the start of the selection, the same on every channel, moving what follows on, as one step to undo. DTMF (touch-tone) has 0-9, * and #, and A-D, each a row tone (697 to 941 Hz) with a column tone (1209 to 1633 Hz); MF (R1, the signalling between exchanges) has 0-9, K for KP and S for ST, each two of 700 to 1700 Hz. A comma is a pause as long as a tone.
- Each pair lasts 20 to 2000 ms (100 to begin with), with 0 to 2000 ms of silence between pairs; the level is the pair's peak (-96 to 0 dBFS, -6 to begin with), each tone at half of it, and each pair rises and falls over 2 ms so it starts and stops without a click.
- Measured by the tests, at 48 kHz: all 16 DTMF keys and a pause, as 70 ms pairs 50 ms apart, read back from the generated audio in order (123A456B789C*0#D,5), each tone of a pair at half the level (within 0.000 dB), the other six DTMF frequencies 54.0 dB down or more, silence between pairs and in the pause, the length exact, the peak -6.00 dBFS for -6; MF's KP, ten digits and ST read back (K1234567890S), the other four MF frequencies 79.6 dB down or more; lower-case letters give the same audio; characters the kind lacks, only pauses, timings or a level out of range, and a sample rate too low for the highest tone are refused. The whole suite passed (1190 checks). The dialog was not tried by hand, and the tones were not played into a telephone decoder.

**Milestone 250: Automatic Phase Correction**
- Effects > Automatic Phase Correction... finds how far the right channel of a stereo file is late or early on the left (two microphones at different distances, a tape head out of line), up to a most offset of 0.1 to 50 ms either way (5 ms to begin with), and takes it out by moving each channel half of it, in the selection or the whole file. A right channel found upside down is turned over too, unless you choose to leave it. It is one step to undo, runs in the background with a progress window, and can be recorded into command sequences (and so run in Batch Process), measuring again on each file; it is on for stereo files only.
- The offset is where the channels' cross-correlation peaks: the cross-spectrum is summed over blocks of the range, the peak found at whole frames, then between them from the cross-spectrum itself. The channels are moved with a windowed-sinc fractional delay (64 taps), easing from the audio as it was over 10 ms at the edges of a selection.
- Afterwards a note says what was found: how far the right channel was late or early (in ms and frames), whether it was upside down, and the correlation; or that no offset was found and nothing changed.
- Measured by the tests, on white noise up to 16 kHz at 48 kHz: the right channel 7.3 frames late, 12.6 early, 20 late and 0.25 late is found as 7.300, -12.600, 20.000 and 0.250 frames, and moved back the channels differ by 104.2 to 130.9 dB less than their power; the same sound 4.6 frames apart, each channel with its own noise 10 dB down, is found as 4.601 frames (correlation 0.909); a right channel upside down and 24 frames late is found as 24.000 frames at correlation -1.000, and turned over and moved back the channels differ by 102.2 dB less than their power. Unrelated channels show a correlation of 0.007 (taken as none); silence, an offset past the most looked for, a mono file, too short a range and settings out of range give nothing, and it can be stopped. Over a range the audio outside is left bit for bit and the edges ease in with no jump; moved by nothing the audio is the same bit for bit. Recorded into a sequence, it gives the command's own result bit for bit. The whole suite passed (1187 checks). Tested on synthetic signals only, not on real recordings; the dialog and its note were not tried by hand.

**Milestone 249: Centre Channel**
- Effects > Centre Channel... finds what is panned to the middle of a stereo mix (a voice, dialogue, a lead instrument) and either removes it or keeps it alone, lowering the rest, by a reduction of 0 to 40 dB, in the selection or the whole file. Only frequencies between a low and a high (20 Hz to 20 kHz; 100 Hz to 8 kHz to begin with) are changed, easing out over a third of an octave past each, so the bass of a mix can stay when the voice goes. It is one step to undo, runs in the background with a progress window, and can be recorded into command sequences (and so run in Batch Process); it is on for stereo files only.
- It works band by band in short frames (about 43 ms): in each band the part the two channels share is estimated from their common power against each one's own, averaged over neighbouring bands and smoothed over time, and that share of the mid channel is taken as the middle. A sound off to the side in the same band at the same moment can't be told apart from it fully, so some of each stays with the other.
- The README's list of the effects command sequences record now also names Reduce Reverb, Level Speech, Stretch and Shift Pitch (recorded since milestones 244 to 246) and Centre Channel.
- Measured by the tests, at 48 kHz: removed by 20 dB (100 Hz to 8 kHz), a 440 Hz tone in the middle goes down 20.00 dB in both channels while tones on one side only (1 and 1.5 kHz) keep their level (within 0.000 dB) and a 50 Hz tone in the middle, below the band, stays within 0.034 dB; extracted by 20 dB, the side tones go down 20.00 dB, the middle one keeps its level (within 0.000 dB) and the 50 Hz tone stays within 0.005 dB. A voice-like buzz in syllables in the middle, 6.8 dB over unrelated white noise on each side, over the whole band at 40 dB: removed, the result is the noise alone but for an error 18.5 dB below the voice; extracted, the voice alone but for an error 9.6 dB below the noise. A range leaves the audio more than a frame outside it bit for bit; at no reduction the mix comes back within 0.00000018; a mono file, an empty range and settings out of range are refused, and it can be stopped. Recorded into a sequence, it gives the command's own result bit for bit. The whole suite passed (1182 checks). Tested on synthetic signals only, not on real mixes, and not listened to; the dialog was not tried by hand.
**Milestone 248: Scrubbing**
- Dragging along the ruler above the waveform while stopped scrubs: the sound under the mouse plays as it moves, like tape moved by hand past the head. Dragged at normal speed it plays as it is, faster raises its pitch, backwards plays it backwards, and holding still lets it catch up and fall silent. The cursor follows the mouse and stays where you let go. The sound goes out through the effects rack like playback.
- The sound follows the mouse over about 50 ms, no faster than four times normal speed, with its speed smoothed over 10 ms and the samples read between with a cubic curve; its level follows its speed, and letting go fades it out over 10 ms. It is worked out sample by sample, so the device's block size changes nothing.
- In the editor only: a multitrack session isn't scrubbed yet.
- ROADMAP.md lists what comes next again.
- Measured by the tests, on a 440 Hz tone at 48 kHz: dragged at normal speed it plays at 440.0 Hz and its own level (-0.00 dB), twice as fast at 880.0 Hz, backwards at 440.0 Hz, the sound 47 ms behind the mouse; silent where it starts until the mouse moves, and held still the head catches up and the sound is below -100 dBFS half a second later; a jump 20 s ahead is followed at 3.96 times normal speed over the first second; rendered in blocks of 64 and of 1000 frames the sound is the same bit for bit; let go, it is below -100 dBFS from 50 ms on; a 44.1 kHz file on a 48 kHz device plays at 440.0 Hz; a mono file is the same on both sides; past the end of the file there is silence. The whole suite passed (1177 checks). The drag in the app itself, and how it sounds, were not tried: the tests drive the scrubber the player runs, not the mouse.

**Release 0.12.0: stretch and pitch in files, reverb reduction, a speech leveller and video in sessions**
- `SpectraBlade-0.12.0.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 244 to 247 below on top of 0.11.0. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1171 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.12.0, holds both architectures, and carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` and LAME's licence. The image's app was not launched (a new ad hoc signature makes macOS ask again for the microphone); the same code, signed for development, was opened in the app for each milestone. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried. Reduce Reverb and Level Speech were tested on synthetic signals only, not on real recordings, and not listened to.
- The suite counts from Milestone 238 to 247, and in Release 0.11.0, were each one too many when first written here (the summary line "ALL PASSED" was counted as a check); they are corrected above and below.

**Milestone 247: Video in sessions**
- A multitrack session can have a video: File > Attach Video... in the Multitrack workspace gives the session one, File > Video Start... sets where in the session its first frame plays (typed, or at the cursor), and File > Detach Video takes it away (it takes a file's away too). In a session each is a step to undo, and the video and its start are kept in the session file (relative to it first, as audio is) and so in projects.
- The Video panel follows the session's playhead while the session is shown, black before the video starts, and the file's video again in the editor.
- File > Save Mix into Video... (Save Sound into Video in the editor) writes a copy of the session's video with the session's mix from the video's start for the video's length, its effects included, so the picture streams are copied as they are (needs ffmpeg).
- Fixed: Stretch, Shift Pitch, Reduce Reverb and Level Speech (milestones 244 to 246) stayed on in the Multitrack workspace, where they would have changed the file in the editor rather than the session. They are now off there, as the other effects on files are.
- Measured by the tests: a video starting 2.5 s in shows black at 1 s and its 0.75 s at 3.25 s, and takes the session's frames 120000 to 216000 for its 2 s at 48 kHz; the video and its start (2.5 s) are kept in the session file, found beside the session when both are moved, and a session without one has none; attaching is a step to undo; a stereo tone from 1 s in, with the video starting at 0.5 s, goes into a MOV copy with the picture's packets unchanged (same MD5), equal to the session's own mix of that time within 1 step of 24 bits (worst 0.99 steps), the tone's first sample at frame 24000 (0.5 s; its first non-zero one at 24001); stopping writes nothing, and a missing video or writing over the video itself is refused. The whole suite passed (1171 checks). Turning the four effects off in the Multitrack workspace is not covered by a test.

**Milestone 246: Level Speech**
- Effects > Level Speech... evens out the level of speech in the selection, or the whole file without one: quiet passages are raised and loud ones lowered towards a target loudness (-40 to -5 LUFS), by at most the largest boost and cut (0 to 30 dB each). It is one step to undo, runs in the background with a progress window, and can be recorded into command sequences (and so run in Batch Process).
- Loudness is measured as BS.1770 measures it, in 400 ms blocks every 50 ms; a block counts as speech when it is above the silence threshold (-80 to -20 LUFS) and within 10 LU of the loudest block it overlaps (so one only just reaching into speech at a pause's edge doesn't), and a moment whose own block is speech takes as its level the mean of the speech blocks within 750 ms of it. Pauses (the other moments) take the gain of the speech either side, so the room noise between words is not raised on its own. The gain is smoothed over half a second either side, is the same on every channel, and eases in and out over 10 ms at a selection's edges.
- No sample comes out above -1 dBFS: where raising would take a peak there, the gain around it is held lower (and where the audio already peaks above it, it can be cut by more than the largest cut).
- Loudness gives step-by-step K-weighted power for it to measure with.
- Measured by the tests, on syllable-like bursts of noise with room noise (about -72 dBFS) between them: passages at -12.0, -30.0 and -21.0 LUFS (18.0 LU apart) come out at -19.9, -19.9 and -19.9 LUFS for a -20 target, measured after their first half second; room noise in a 4 s pause between speech already at the target changes by 0.69 dB rather than being raised; a file of room noise alone is left bit for bit; speech at -45 LUFS is raised by the largest boost and no more (12.00 dB); a click at -6 dBFS in speech raised 12 dB comes out at -1.01 dBFS while the speech a second from it still reaches -19.8 LUFS; every channel gets the same gain (a right channel that was the left's negative still is, bit for bit); a selection's surroundings are bit for bit as they were and its first and last samples within 0.00001 of what they were; stopping part way and settings out of range give nothing; each of the 31 sequence steps gives its command's own result bit for bit. The whole suite passed (1166 checks). It was not tried on real recordings of speech.

**Milestone 245: Reduce Reverb**
- Effects > Reduce Reverb... lowers a room's reverberation in the selection, or the whole file without one: the tail after each sound goes down by up to the reduction (0 to 30 dB), while the sound itself and notes held steady are kept. It is one step to undo, runs in the background with a progress window, and can be recorded into command sequences (and so run in Batch Process).
- It predicts, in each frequency band, the reverberation now from the sound 80 ms before, dying away as the room's reverb time says (late-reverberation suppression after Lebart, Boucher and Denbigh, with Polack's model of reverberation), and lowers the band by how much of it is that prediction. The reverb time is set in the dialog (0.1 to 10 s): longer takes away more of the tail, shorter leaves more.
- It does not take out reverberation under the sound itself (early reflections, and the tail of one sound under the next): that is mixed in with the sound in each band and stays.
- Measured by the tests: bursts of noise in a synthetic 1 s room (velvet noise, as loud in all as the sound), with the time set to 1 s and 12 dB: the reverberation after each burst 9.1 dB lower, the bursts themselves changed by -0.01 dB; with the time set to 0.5 s the tail goes down 1.7 dB, at 2 s 11.9 dB (the bursts -0.02 dB); a steady tone at a 2 s time and 30 dB keeps its level (0.000 dB); the channels are worked alone (a right channel that was the left's negative still is, bit for bit); audio more than a frame (2048 samples at 48 kHz) outside a selection is bit for bit as it was; stopping part way and settings out of range give nothing; each of the 30 sequence steps gives its command's own result bit for bit. The whole suite passed (1157 checks). It was not tried on real recordings of rooms.

**Milestone 244: Stretch and Shift Pitch in files**
- Effects > Stretch... makes the selection, or the whole file without one, longer or shorter (50% to 200%) keeping its pitch, and Effects > Shift Pitch... makes it higher or lower (up to 12 semitones either way) keeping its length, each as one undo step, worked in the background with a progress window that can cancel. They use the time stretch the session's clips already had. The rest of the file moves with a stretch; markers and regions inside the stretched part keep their place in it, and none is lost. Where the processed part meets the audio either side, the two are crossfaded over 5 ms so the joins don't click. A selection stays over the processed part. A file with a video gets black picture over the processed part, as for other new audio.
- Both can be recorded into command sequences, and so run in Batch Process.
- The time stretch no longer fades in at the start of shortened audio or out at the end of lengthened audio: past the input's ends it now reads the audio mirrored rather than silence, so it keeps its level up to its edges. That changes Stretch Clip and Shift Clip Pitch in sessions too: a shortened clip's first 20 ms or so had faded in from nothing. Within a file, the stretched part is worked with a quarter of a second of the audio either side of it, so its edges carry on from what is there.
- The video panel's check now names which of its parts failed. Naming them showed the failing part: the macOS player can still be reading a video's length and picture size when the panel is ready, so the check now gives it up to 5 s to have them (and says what it saw if they are wrong), and up to 8 s rather than 3 to stop at a place.
- Measured by the tests: stretched from 50% to 200%, a 440 Hz tone's first and last 128 samples peak at its 0.5; the middle second of a 3 s tone at 150% makes it 3.5 s, the audio either side bit for bit as it was, the pitch 440.00 Hz, and the largest step from one sample to the next at the joins 0.0302 and 0.0289 against the tone's own 0.0293; 7 semitones up, the middle second plays 659.25 Hz (659.26 wanted), as long as before, the audio either side bit for bit, the largest step at the joins 0.0433 against the higher tone's own 0.0431; markers before the part stay, those inside keep their place in it, those after move on, and a region across its end grows with it; as sequence steps, the range and markers follow the new length (a gain step after acts on the stretched part), nothing happens at 100% or 0 semitones, out-of-range settings are refused and stopping part way changes nothing; each of the 29 sequence steps gives its command's own result bit for bit. The earlier time stretch and pitch shift checks (pitch within 0.5%, level within 1 dB, clips) still pass. The whole suite passed (1150 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the two dialogs and their progress windows in the app (the tests run the same steps the commands run), and listening to stretched or shifted speech and music.

**Release 0.11.0: batch processing, command sequences, 5.1, video and projects**
- `SpectraBlade-0.11.0.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 230 to 243 below on top of 0.10.2. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1145 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.11.0, holds both architectures, and carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` and LAME's licence. The image's app was not launched (a new ad hoc signature makes macOS ask again for the microphone); the same code, signed for development, was opened in the app for each 5.1 milestone. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried. The video panel's check (the real macOS player, timed) failed on some runs under load during development and passed on the rerun; it passed in this one.

**Milestone 243: Writing the surround pan while playing**
- With Write Automation on (W), the mixer's surround pad, width and LFE stay live even when automated, and moving them while the session plays records into their envelopes, as the track headers' volume and pan do: from when the control is grabbed to when it is let go, heard as it is written, then back to what was there over 0.1 s, and undone as one step ("Write Surround Automation"). The pad writes its left-right and front-back envelopes together. A control held still goes on writing its value as play moves on. Stopped, they set the pan as before; with writing off, automated ones follow their envelopes.
- An automation pass can now write several envelopes at once.
- Measured by the tests: one pass writes the x and y envelopes together, at the play positions given, going back to the setting after, and undo takes both off in one step; with the mixer tied to the view and writing on, its automated controls come alive; stopped, the pad sets its place and an automated width changes nothing; with the session playing (a clip of silence, the play position moved between moves), dragging the pad from front left to back right writes both envelopes at the play position and goes back to the setting after, and the width slider writes its own. The whole suite passed (1145 checks); on earlier runs the video panel's check (the real macOS player, timing on a loaded machine) failed twice and passed on the rerun.
- Not tried by hand: dragging the pad with the mouse while listening.

**Milestone 242: A six-channel master meter**
- In a 5.1 master the mixer's master meter has six bars, named L R C LFE Ls Rs, and the master strip is as wide as the others. They show the 5.1 master as mixed: before the effects rack (which is stereo) and before any fold-down, so the centre, LFE and surrounds show even when they are folded into a stereo output. A stereo master's meter is as before: what goes to the outputs, after the rack.
- A 5.1 track's clips show all their channels in the lane, up to six, each named down the left when there is room; a stereo track still shows the two channels it plays.
- Measured by the tests: the mixer's peaks of a 5.1 master's six channels equal the peaks of each output channel bit for bit, for each reader on its own and from 0 after each take, the same when folded down; a stereo master raises none; the mixer's master meter has six channels in a 5.1 master and shows each one's level, and two again in a stereo master. The whole suite passed (1141 checks).
- Not tried by hand: watching the meter while a 5.1 session plays.

**Milestone 241: Surround pan automation**
- A track's or bus's place in a 5.1 master can follow envelopes: its left-right and front-back position, its width and its LFE share each have one (track automation menu: Show Surround Envelope), drawn and edited in the lane like the volume and pan envelopes, and cleared with Clear Shown Surround Envelope. Left is at the top of the left-right lane and the front at the top of the front-back one; the LFE is off at the bottom (-60 dB).
- The panner's gains are worked out from the envelopes every 32 frames of session time and go in a straight line between, so a sweep is smooth and plays the same however the audio is split into blocks. With no surround envelope nothing changes: a track with the pan as it starts still goes straight to L and R, bit for bit.
- In the mixer, a part that follows an envelope shows its value at the play position (moving as it plays) and can't be set there: the pad's point is dimmed, the width or LFE slider greyed. A 5.1 track and a stereo master take no notice of the envelopes; they are kept in the session file, and inserting, copying and pasting time carry them.
- Not done: writing the surround pan while playing (drawing it in the lane works); track templates don't carry automation of any kind, as before.
- Measured by the tests: a sweep from front left to back right, narrowing, with the LFE coming in, follows the envelopes (within 1e-6 of gains worked out at each 32-frame point and joined by straight lines) and is the same, bit for bit, in blocks of 97 and 512; an envelope at the setting's value plays as the setting does (within 1e-6); a bus's surround envelopes act as a track's; a stereo master and a 5.1 track play bit for bit as without them; values are kept in range, folders refused, and inserting, copying and pasting time move the points; the file keeps all four; the mixer strip shows the values at the play position and refuses changes to automated parts, and the pad can be dragged again once its envelopes are cleared. The whole suite passed (1139 checks).
- Not tried by hand: drawing the envelopes with the mouse, and listening on 5.1 speakers.

**Milestone 240: 5.1 tracks**
- A track can be a 5.1 track (right-click its header: 5.1 Track, an undo step). Its clips play six channels, L R C LFE Ls Rs: a 5.1 clip as it is, a mono clip from C, a stereo one from L and R. In a 5.1 master they go straight to their own speakers through the track's polarity, volume (and its automation), mute, solo and ducking, with no pan or surround pan; the mixer strip and track header show none. Six-channel audio brought onto a new track in a 5.1 master (importing or dropping a 5.1 file) makes it a 5.1 track.
- What a 5.1 track feeds besides a 5.1 master gets it folded down to stereo (centre and surrounds at -3 dB, LFE left out): its sends, a bus or interface pair it plays into, what ducks under it or listens to it, its meter, and a stereo master.
- Its effects don't run: track effects are stereo. A 5.1 track can't be frozen (there is nothing running to freeze); bounced in a 5.1 master it renders six channels and lands on a new 5.1 track. The session file and track templates keep it.
- Measured by the tests: a 5.1 clip on a 5.1 track plays its six channels to the master's six, bit for bit; a mono clip plays from C alone and a stereo one from L and R; -6 dB of volume, as a setting or an envelope, with the polarity inverted scales and turns over all six (within 1e-6) while its pan and surround pan change nothing; effects on it don't run and add no delay; sending it into a bus whose effects have 64 frames of latency gives the bus the fold-down, and the six channels line up with the bus's output, in blocks of 97; in a stereo master or played into a bus it is the fold-down, bit for bit; six-channel audio on a new track in a 5.1 master makes a 5.1 track (not stereo audio, or in a stereo master); buses, folders and frozen tracks can't be 5.1 tracks and a 5.1 track can't be frozen; the file, templates and bounces keep it; made one from the view as an undo step, its mixer strip drops the pan and surround controls. The whole suite passed (1130 checks); on one earlier run the video panel's play-along check was out by a hair under load (the real macOS player; it passed on the rerun at 30.4 ms).
- Not tried by hand: the header's menu item with the mouse, and listening on 5.1 speakers.

**Milestone 239: Surround strips that fit**
- In a 5.1 master the mixer's strips are now wider (132 points instead of 84), with the surround pad, width and LFE on the left and the fader, meter and volume readout on the right. Stacked in one column as milestone 238 had them, they left no room for the fader and meter at the Multitrack workspace's mixer height: seen in the app, the strips showed no fader or meter, and the mute, polarity and solo buttons were squashed flat.
- The strips' pan now shows "C" at the centre, and the width "W 0%", when a strip first shows them; they showed "0" until moved (the pan since the mixer was added).
- Measured by the tests: at the workspace's mixer height (210 points) the pad is a 70-point square beside a 90-point fader, and the strip is 132 wide; the pan, width and LFE show "C", "W 0%" and "LFE off". A drawing of the mixer from the tests showed every control in place. The whole suite passed (1119 checks).
- Tried in the app: a 5.1 session opened from Finder showed each track's and bus's pad at its saved place, width and LFE (before this fix, which was then checked in the drawing, not the app).

**Milestone 238: A 5.1 master**
- Multitrack > 5.1 Master mixes a session to 5.1 (L R C LFE Ls Rs) instead of stereo, as an undo step, and the session file keeps it. Each track and bus going to the mix is placed in the room by a surround pad on its mixer strip: the room seen from above, front at the top, with the five speakers on its edge. Drag the point to move it, and double-click it to put it back in front. Under the pad, Width sets how far apart the left and right channels play (at 100% they sit 30 degrees either side of the point, so in front they go to L and R; at 0 both play at the point, as one mono source), and LFE sends a share to the LFE as well (off at the far left, then -60 to +10 dB).
- How it pans: each channel's direction from the middle is panned between the two speakers either side of it with constant power, and its distance from the middle keeps that much in those speakers, spreading the rest evenly over all five. With the pan as it starts, a stereo track plays to L and R exactly as in a stereo master.
- Up to the pan, everything is as in a stereo master: clips, the track's own pan, effects, volume, sends and buses stay stereo. A bus is placed by its own surround pan, not by the tracks in it. Tracks and buses playing out of interface pairs still do.
- Playing: Transport > Play Surround Folded Down folds the master to stereo (centre and surrounds at -3 dB, LFE left out); Play Surround to Separate Outputs plays L and R on the output pair, through the effects rack and the master meter, and C, LFE, Ls and Rs on the four outputs after it, which skip the rack and the meter.
- Mix Down, Export Regions and the stems write six channels in file order. Bouncing and freezing render the track's own stereo sound, which its surround pan then places; a bounce's new track takes the source's surround pan. Track templates keep the surround pan.
- Measured by the tests: with the pans as they start, the 5.1 master's L and R are the stereo mix bit for bit and the other four are silent; the gains keep the power at 200 random points and widths (within 1e-5), spread evenly over the five speakers from the middle, and put a mono track at width 0 in front into C alone at its own level (within 1e-6); behind, the channels play from Ls and Rs and nothing in front; the LFE gets 0.7071 times the share of each channel. Played folded down, the master is the six channels folded as a 5.1 file is, bit for bit. To separate outputs, L and R go to the mix and C to Rs to the four channels after the pair (the engine's list of outputs to turn on holds those four; it was not tried with the interface). Blocks of 97 and 512 frames give the same result. A bus at Ls's direction plays from Ls alone. A mixdown has six channels and leaves out what plays out of interface pairs. The session file, track templates, freezing and bouncing keep the right things. On the mixer strips the controls show once the master is 5.1; a click and drag on the pad is one undo step; and the width and LFE sliders set their values. The whole suite passed (1118 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the pad and sliders with the mouse in the app, and listening on a 5.1 speaker system. Clips with more than two channels still play their first two; 5.1 clips and surround automation are not in this milestone.

**Milestone 237: Undo history in projects**
- Saving a project now keeps each file's undo history too: opened again, a file is at the step it was at, with undo and redo back through every step (audio, markers, tags and the cover, sample rate and picture map), and knows which step was saved. The audio the steps share is written once per block, as 32-bit float WAVs, into "<project> Files/History", written afresh each time the project is saved. That can take a lot of room (every version of the audio the history holds), so File > Keep Undo History in Projects turns it off; a file with no steps keeps none.
- When the audio kept for a history is gone, the file opens as it was saved, and the project says so.
- Measured by the tests: a file with a gain, a marker, tags with a cover, a cut and the cut undone, saved in a project and opened again after the project's folder moved, comes back at the saved step (not dirty, redo available) with that step's marker and tags (the cover's bytes equal); redo brings back the cut, bit for bit, with its picture map; going back to the start gives the file as first opened, bit for bit; its five steps' audio was written as 2 shared blocks; files with no steps keep none; with the blocks deleted it opens as saved and that is named. The whole suite passed (1094 checks).
- Not tried by hand: saving and reopening a project with histories through the app's menus (the tests run the same Project code the menus call, but not the menu commands and the background save), and a long history of a large file (the space and time it takes).

**Milestone 236: Picture follows edits**
- The picture of a video now follows edits that change the sound's length. After a cut the picture jumps where the sound does; audio pasted from elsewhere in the file brings its picture with it; audio from nowhere in the file (inserted silence, generated or time-stretched audio) has no picture, and the Video panel shows black there. Edits in place leave it as it was, as does a sample rate conversion. It goes back and forth with undo and redo.
- File > Save Sound into Video... with the length changed cuts the picture to follow, on whole frames, black where the sound has none or runs past the video's end, and encodes it again (H.264 at CRF 18 in MP4, M4V, MOV and MKV; VP9 in WebM; MPEG-4 Part 2 in AVI), without chapters, which no longer fit. With the length as it was, the picture is still copied as it is.
- Measured by the tests: through a gain, a cut, inserted silence, a paste from 8.5 s to the start, a fade, two undos and a redo and a rate conversion, the map is exactly what each edit should give (cut jumps, black for the silence, the pasted picture, unchanged by edits in place and the conversion) and reads back from text; a 4 s, 25 fps video whose every frame has its own brightness, with 1 s cut and 0.4 s of silence inserted, is written as 85 frames for 3.4 s, each one the source frame the map gives or black (0 wrong), as H.264 with AAC sound.
- Not tried by hand: the Video panel going black over inserted audio while playing (the tests check the map it reads, not the panel), and long or high-resolution videos re-encoded (the time it takes).

**Milestone 235: MKV and WebM pictures**
- macOS can't show MKV or WebM pictures, so the Video panel now has ffmpeg make a preview of them (H.264, up to 1280 wide, silent) and shows that instead, labelled "preview" with its size. Previews are kept in ~/Library/Caches/SpectraBlade/Video Previews, named by the video's path, size and date, so a video opened again shows at once; a changed video gets a new one. Any other video macOS refuses gets a preview the same way. Saving sound into the video still uses the original's picture.
- Measured by the tests: the panel shows MP4, M4V, MOV and an MPEG-4 AVI itself and MKV and WebM through previews of the right size and length (2 s, 320 x 240, silent); opened again, the kept preview is used without being made again and shown within 23 ms. In the app, a 6 s 1920 x 1080 MKV showed its picture as "preview 1280 x 720".
- Not tried by hand: a long MKV (the preview takes a while to make the first time; the panel says so meanwhile).

**Milestone 234: Projects**
- File > Save Project and Save Project As... save everything open as a project (.sbproject), and File > Open Project... (or opening or dropping one, or Open Recent) brings it all back: the files open in the editor, in order, each with its selection, cursor, zoom and spectral band, and the video it goes with; the one shown; the effects rack beside the editor, with its effects' settings; the multitrack session; and whether the editor or the session was in front. It works without a session too, for waveform editing and effects alone. Opening a project asks about unsaved changes first, then replaces what is open.
- A project points to its audio rather than holding it. Saving one leaves the files you opened as they are: a file with unsaved changes, or one never saved, is written as a 32-bit float WAV into "<project> Files" beside the project, and the open file becomes that copy, saved. A session with changes is saved to its own file; an untitled one with tracks is saved into the same folder. Paths inside the project's folder are kept relative, so the folder can be moved or copied whole; files outside it are found by their full path.
- Not kept: undo histories (each file reopens as saved), the multitrack view's scroll and zoom, the window layout and app settings (kept by the app as before). A file or session no longer there is left out and named when the project opens.
- The window title shows the project's name after the file's or session's; dropping a session on the editor now opens it too.
- Measured by the tests: saving a project with an unchanged file, an edited one and a never-saved one, an attached video, a built-in EQ in the rack and an untitled session with a clip writes the two changed files bit for bit into the project's folder and leaves the files opened byte for byte as they were; after moving the project's folder and closing everything, opening it brings back the three files in order with their audio bit for bit, their places and the video, the second one shown, the EQ's Band 1 Gain (0.370 for 0.370), the session with its track and clip, and the session in front; a missing file is left out and named; a non-project, a newer version or no file is refused; saving again unchanged writes no audio. The whole suite passed (1090 checks).
- Tried in the app: a project of two files opened from Finder showed the second file with its selection, cursor and zoom, and the project's name in the title. Not tried by hand: Save Project and Open Project from the menu, Open Recent, dropping a project or a session on the editor, and a project with plug-ins from other makers in the rack.

**Milestone 233: Video**
- A file opened from a video (MP4, M4V, MOV, MKV, WebM, AVI; its sound through ffmpeg, as before; M4V is now offered in Open too) remembers it, through Save As and switching files, and opens the Video panel (Window > Video), docked above the effects rack. Any file can be given one with File > Attach Video....
- The Video panel shows the picture, without its sound, following the editor: stopped, the frame at the cursor; playing, the picture plays along. AVPlayer was measured to start about 0.107 s after it is told to, and changing its speed made it stall for about 0.2 s, so the picture plays at normal speed and learns how far ahead of the sound to aim when it starts or jumps, putting itself right when more than 0.04 s off. In the multitrack session it stays where it is. The picture is shown by macOS (AVFoundation): MP4, M4V, MOV and an MPEG-4 AVI were shown; MKV and WebM can't be, and the panel says so (their sound can still be edited and put back).
- File > Save Sound into Video... writes a copy of the video with the file's sound in place of its own, through ffmpeg: the picture streams copied as they are (not re-encoded), with the video's tags and chapters; the sound as AAC at 320 kbps in MP4 and M4V, 24-bit PCM in MOV and AVI, 24-bit FLAC in MKV and Opus at 256 kbps in WebM. Other sound tracks and subtitles are left out. The copy is the same type as the video; writing over the video itself is refused. The sound's time 0 is the picture's: edits that change the length (cutting, inserting) put them out of step, and nothing lines them up again.
- Measured by the tests, with 2 s test videos of each type made by ffmpeg: each one's sound opens; after Save Sound into Video, the picture's packets have the same MD5 as the original's, there is one sound track of the stated codec, and the sound read back is within one 24-bit step of what went in for MOV, MKV and AVI (worst 0.99 steps), 65.5 dB signal to error for AAC (MP4, M4V) and 45.0 dB for Opus (WebM), 96,000 frames of 96,000 each. With a simulated player starting 0.1 s late, the picture learns to aim 0.100 s ahead in 2 seeks and keeps within 0.04 s; on the real player (a 320 x 240 MP4, the panel not on screen), stopped it showed 1.240 s for 1.24, and playing it was 3.6 to 7.2 ms from the sound after 1.5 s over four runs. The whole suite passed (1086 checks).
- Not tried by hand: watching a video play along with its sound, attaching a video, and Save Sound into Video from the menu were not done in the app; a 1280 x 720 MP4 opened in it showed the panel with its first frame.

**Milestone 232: 5.1 surround files**
- A file with more than two channels used to play only its first two. Transport > Play Surround Folded Down (the default) plays a 5.1 file (L R C LFE Ls Rs) folded down to stereo on the output pair, with the ITU-R BS.775 gains: each side plus the centre and its surround at -3 dB, the LFE left out, not scaled down (a loud mix can pass 0 dBFS). Transport > Play Surround to Separate Outputs plays the first two channels on the output pair, through the rack, and the rest straight out of the outputs after it, one each, past the rack (5.1 on outputs 1/2: C, LFE, Ls and Rs on 3 to 6), for up to 8 channels; the device's outputs are turned on as needed. Other files past stereo play their first two channels when folded down. The choice is remembered.
- The waveform names each lane's channel: L and R, or L R C LFE Ls Rs for 5.1, otherwise its number.
- Make Stereo on a 5.1 file reads Fold Down to Stereo and folds it down with the same gains; Mix to Mono gives the average of the two folded sides. Before, 5.1 made stereo kept L and R only, and made mono averaged all six channels with the LFE. The same goes for a sequence's Make Stereo step and Batch Process's Stereo and Mono.
- File > Save Channels as Mono Files... writes each channel to a WAV of its own in a chosen folder ("Mix L.wav", "Mix R.wav", "Mix C.wav", "Mix LFE.wav", "Mix Ls.wav", "Mix Rs.wav"; numbered past those or for other files), at the file's bit depth, with its markers and tags. File > New File from Mono Files... puts the chosen files' channels side by side as a new file (up to 32 channels), in name order, or in 5.1 order for six named ... L, R, C, LFE, Ls and Rs; they must share a sample rate, and shorter ones end in silence.
- 5.1 WAV files are written as WAVE_FORMAT_EXTENSIBLE with the speaker mask for L R C LFE Ls Rs; FLAC keeps the six channels; MP3 and AAC still hold one or two (fold down first). Loudness already measured 5.1 by BS.1770 (LFE left out).
- Interface outputs set for session tracks are now connected only while the session plays, and a file's extra channels only while the file does.
- Measured by the tests: the fold-down, mono, a sequence step and a batch from 5.1 are bit for bit the arithmetic worked out in the test; a 5.1 WAV carries speaker mask 0x3f and reads back bit for bit, and a FLAC keeps six channels; each channel saved alone is that channel and the six put back are the file bit for bit; files of different lengths end in silence; six named in any order and case go in 5.1 order; the player gives the fold-down on the mix, or L and R on the mix and C, LFE, Ls and Rs on the next four channels with the rest silent, bit for bit, in blocks of 40,000 frames; on the 30-output interface a 5.1 file playing to separate outputs turns on and connects outputs 3 to 6 and no others, and a stereo file or folding down disconnects them. The whole suite passed (1076 checks).
- Not tried by hand: no 5.1 file was listened to, on separate outputs or folded down; the menu choices and the two file commands were not used in the app (the lane names were seen on a 5.1 file opened in it). Sessions still mix in stereo: there are no 5.1 tracks, surround panning or 5.1 master, and a 5.1 clip in a session is folded down.

**Milestone 231: Recorded command sequences**
- Effects > Record Sequence records the effects you apply, with the settings you gave them, until Stop Recording Sequence..., which lists the steps and saves them under a name (replacing one of that name only when you say so). While it records, the bar over the editor says how many steps it has. Effects > Play Sequence... shows the saved sequences and their steps, and plays one on the selection, or the whole file without one, as one step to undo; there it can delete one too. File > Batch Process... can play one on every file, on the whole file, after the effects rack.
- What records (27 commands): Gain, Normalize, Match Loudness, Invert, Reverse, Remove DC Offset, Swap Channels, Invert Left or Right Channel, Copy Left to Right and Right to Left, Mix to Mono, Make Stereo, Balance, Stereo Width, Echo, Tremolo, EQ Band, Compressor, Limiter, De-esser, Chorus, Flanger, Phaser, Reverb, Convert Sample Rate and Apply Effects Rack (the rack's plug-ins and their settings as they were). Other edits made while recording (fades, cuts, the spectral tools, the parametric, graphic and multiband panels, noise reduction and the other repairs, markers) aren't recorded, and stopping lists the ones that weren't, undos included. A step works on the selection when the sequence plays, so steps that need one of their own (fades, gain and pan ramps, silence) aren't offered.
- Those commands now apply through the same code a played step uses, so a step played back gives what the command gave with those settings. Sequences are files in the app's data folder (SpectraBlade/Sequences, one .sbsequence XML file each); a file holding a step this version doesn't know isn't played.
- Measured by the tests: each of the 27 steps gives its command's own result bit for bit on part of a stereo file (the rack step, through the built-in Compressor, is what Apply Rack renders); steps with nothing to do leave the audio, and unknown steps, missing or out-of-range settings (an EQ band above half a 22.05 kHz rate) are refused; four steps on a part of the file (gain, 44.1 kHz, compressor, normalize) give the commands' results one after another with the part and the markers moved by the rate change; a stopped run, or one with a step that can't run, changes nothing and says which step; settings come back exactly from the saved file (1/3 too), with the rack's plug-in and state; the store lists, replaces and removes; in a batch, a sequence gives what it gives on the file, bit for bit, at the new rate with the markers moved, and a step that can't run on a file leaves it unwritten. Batch's own level step now runs through the same code (its tests still pass). The whole suite passed (1070 checks), and the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: recording, saving, playing and deleting sequences in the app, the bar's step count, and the Batch dialog's sequence choice were not used in the app (the tests run the sequence code directly).

**Milestone 230: Batch processing**
- File > Batch Process... puts many files through the same steps and writes each as a new file; the files themselves are never changed. Choose files or folders (folders are searched, their folders too, for every type the app opens, hidden files left out), then the steps, run in this order: the effects rack (fresh copies of its plug-ins, at the file's own rate, latency compensated, as Apply Rack does), a sample rate (44.1 to 192 kHz), mono or stereo, and the level: the peak in dBFS, or the integrated loudness in LUFS under a true-peak ceiling, either stopping short of the target or reaching it through the true-peak limiter (as Match Loudness does). Then the type: WAV, AIFF or FLAC (keeping each file's bit depth where the type holds it, or 16, 24 or 32-bit float), MP3 or AAC at a bit rate, or with ffmpeg installed Opus, Apple Lossless, WavPack or AC-3 (at the nearest bit rate the type offers).
- The new files go beside each source or into a chosen folder, named after the source with an optional suffix, and numbered (" 2", " 3") when the name is taken or is the source itself. Markers (moved with a rate change), tags and the cover go along where the type keeps them. A report lists each file: written as what, or why not, with notes for what couldn't be done as asked (a silent file left at its level, a ceiling that stopped the loudness short, markers a type doesn't keep, a bit depth a type doesn't hold). The settings are remembered.
- Measured by the tests, on WAV, AIFF, a silent and a text file: a folder gives its four audio files once each, in name order; converted to 48 kHz a file holds the sample-rate converter's samples bit for bit (32-bit float), with its markers moved and tags kept; with nothing asked a 24-bit file comes back at 24 bits and 44.1 kHz with no sample moved by more than 1 LSB (the dither every integer save adds); normalized and made mono its peak is -1.0000 dBFS; matched to -20 LUFS it measures -20.0 LUFS; at -4 LUFS the -1 dBTP ceiling stops it at -4.4 LUFS (said in the report), and through the limiter it reaches -4 LUFS within 0.06 LU with its true peak at -1.0 dBTP; through the rack (the built-in Compressor) it is bit for bit what the rack renders on its own; FLAC at 24 bits, MP3 and AAC at the source's length of 88,200 frames with tags, Opus (asked 320 kbps, written at its highest, 256, with a note) and Apple Lossless through ffmpeg all read back. The whole suite passed (1061 checks), and the app launched and quit cleanly.
- Not tried by hand: the Batch Process dialog, its file and folder choosers, the progress window and the report were not opened in the app (the tests drive the batch code directly). Cancel stops at the file being processed: files already written stay, and no report is shown. Each file is read whole into memory.

**Release 0.10.2: interface outputs and sidechain inputs**
- `SpectraBlade-0.10.2.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 228 and 229 below on top of 0.10.1. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1050 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.10.2, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` and LAME's licence, and launched from the mounted image (the main window in 3.1 seconds on that first launch and 1.2 seconds on the next, reopening the last file) and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 229: Sidechain inputs**
- An effect on a track or bus that has a sidechain input (a second input bus, as many compressors, gates and duckers have) gets a Sidechain submenu in the track's Effects menu: None, or any other track, whose output after its volume (as ducking hears it) goes into that input. The slot shows its key ("sidechain from Kick"). A mono sidechain input gets the key's two sides averaged; a key that doesn't play (muted, or left out by a solo) feeds silence.
- The built-in Compressor has a sidechain input and a Sidechain switch: on, it follows the level at that input in place of its own. In the rack and in Effects > Compressor nothing feeds it, so there it should stay off. Plug-ins applied to a file through the rack now get the file on their main input only, with any sidechain input silent, as the live rack had it.
- Keys are mixed before the tracks that listen to them, and a key is read far enough ahead that it lines up with the audio going into the effect, whatever plug-in latency comes before it, in it, on the key, or on the buses involved. A loop is refused (a key that listens to its listener, through any number of sidechains and ducking keys); ducking now refuses loops through sidechains too. Kept in session files; undoable; deleting the key removes it.
- Freezing, bouncing and stems bring a listener's keys along (and a ducked track's key, which Bounce and stems left out before, so a bounced ducked track now ducks): they feed the effects without being heard in the render. Track templates don't keep sidechain keys.
- Measured by the tests, with a test plug-in that adds its sidechain input to its output: the listener plays its own sound plus the key's after the key's volume (worst difference 6.0e-8); mono inputs get the average; with 120 frames of latency before the effect, 250 in it, 300 on the key, and 500 plus 80 on a bus with a sidechain effect, the mix is bit for bit the one with no latency, in 333- and 2900-frame blocks and from a jump; the built-in Compressor keyed gives the Dynamics core's samples frame for frame, 21.7 dB down while the key plays and back to 0.00 dB after, and on a session track matches the result worked out by hand (6.0e-8); a bounce and a freeze of a listener hear its key, with latency lined up (3.0e-8); a bounce of a ducked track matches what it plays in the session (1.5e-8). The whole suite passed (1050 checks).
- Checked with installed plug-ins: Kickstart 2, Neutron 4 Compressor and bx_townhouse Buss Compressor (VST3) show a sidechain input, which SpectraBlade turns on; Nectar 4 Gate has none. Whether they respond to the key was not checked: their sidechain switches are in their own windows (bx_townhouse played silence here even without a key).
- Not tried by hand: the Sidechain submenu, and listening to a keyed compressor while playing.

**Milestone 228: Track outputs to interface outputs**
- A track's or bus's Output list (under its Sends or Output button) now also lists the pairs of the audio interface's outputs, by their names (for example "Analog 3 / Analog 4"): the track plays out of that pair in place of the mix, for a headphone or cue mix, or outboard gear. Its sends are as they were. It goes past the rack and the master meter, held back by the rack's latency like the click, so it stays in time with the mix; within the session, plug-in latency is lined up as for the mix. The pair is turned on at the interface when it is chosen; a bus's lane says which pair it plays out of. Up to 32 outputs (pairs to 31/32); a pair the interface doesn't have stays silent, and is listed as "not on this interface".
- What plays out of an interface pair is not in the mixdown (Export Session Mixdown is the mix alone). Stems, Export Folder Stems and Bounce render the track's own sound wherever it plays; the bounced track plays out of the source's pair, or into the source's bus, which Bounce didn't keep before. Kept in session files and track templates; freezing renders the track's own sound, as before.
- Measured by the tests: a track out of outputs 3/4 is there bit for bit as it would be in the mix, and the mix lacks it (worst difference 8.9e-8); a bus out of 29/30 carries what it would have added to the mix (1.5e-8); with 300, 500 and 64 frames of plug-in latency along the way, the mix and both pairs line up bit for bit; the player puts a track out of 5/6 on those channels as it would be in the mix (worst 0) and nothing on the others. On the development Mac's 30-output interface, choosing outputs 7/8 turned them on and connected them, pair 31/32 was left out, and setting the track back to the mix disconnected them. The whole suite passed (1039 checks).
- The player now hands the graph the mix and 32 interface channels: a 512-frame block of a playing session took 17.3 microseconds on the development Mac, against 10.0 with the mix alone (a block lasts 10.7 milliseconds at 48 kHz).
- Not tried by hand: the Output list in the app, and listening on outputs other than 1/2.

**Release 0.10.1: tags in AIFF and FLAC files, and cover art**
- `SpectraBlade-0.10.1.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 226 and 227 below on top of 0.10.0. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1031 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.10.1, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` and LAME's licence, and launched from the mounted image (the main window in 3.7 seconds on that first launch and 1.3 seconds on the next, reopening the last file) and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 227: Cover art**
- File > File Info shows the front cover beside the tags, with its size and type, and Choose Image... (a JPEG or PNG of up to 8 MB) and Remove. A change is an undo step with the rest of the info, and the image is shared between undo steps rather than copied.
- Kept when saving WAV (in an "id3 " chunk, an ID3v2.3 tag with the text tags again, as the players that show covers read them there; a cover alone writes no INFO chunk), AIFF (in its ID3 chunk) and FLAC (a PICTURE block marked as the front cover, with the image's size, replacing any it had), and when exporting MP3 (an APIC frame) and AAC (a covr item; the file now keeps room for the cover before its audio, and asks the encoder for about 1.2 times the room the tags and cover take, where it asked for about twice before). Read from all of these, and from WAV files other programs gave an "id3 " chunk (whose tags fill in what their INFO chunk lacks). Not kept: exports through ffmpeg, and covers in Ogg files (Opus, Vorbis), which aren't read.
- Measured by the tests (with ffmpeg 9.0.2 as an outside reader): a 600 x 600 JPEG (12.2 KB) and a 320 x 200 PNG (46.6 KB) come back byte for byte from WAV, AIFF, FLAC, MP3 and .m4a files beside the tags, each file still read at its full length (48,000 frames); ffmpeg copies the same picture out of all five, byte for byte, and the cover ffmpeg attaches to MP3, .m4a and FLAC files is read byte for byte; a 2.5 MB cover fits in an .m4a and comes back byte for byte, with 573,231 bytes left free beside it; a cover alone goes into a WAV's id3 chunk with no INFO chunk; tagging a FLAC file with another cover leaves one PICTURE block, and tagging it with none removes it; a GIF, or an image over 8 MB, is not taken as a cover. The whole suite passed (1031 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the cover in the File Info dialog, choosing an image, or how the Music app and Finder show the covers.

**Milestone 226: Tags in AIFF and FLAC files**
- Saving as AIFF or FLAC now keeps the file info's tags (title, artist, album, genre, year, track and comment): AIFF in an "ID3 " chunk (ID3v2.3) at the end of the file, as Apple's apps and ffmpeg keep them; FLAC in a Vorbis comment block (TITLE, ARTIST, ALBUM, GENRE, DATE, TRACKNUMBER and TRACKTOTAL, COMMENT) after its other metadata blocks and before any padding, replacing one it had. Opening reads them back, and also reads the older NAME, AUTH and ANNO chunks other programs write into AIFF files. The broadcast-wave details stay WAV-only: saving them as AIFF or FLAC keeps the tags and says the broadcast details were left out. Formats that keep neither still say so, now naming WAV, AIFF and FLAC.
- Measured by the tests (with ffmpeg 9.0.2 as an outside reader): 24-bit AIFF, 16-bit AIFF and 24-bit FLAC files keep every tag, Unicode too, and read back at their full length; tagging a copy of each file saved without info leaves its audio bit for bit; ffmpeg reads the same tags (from FLAC, the track as 3 with a total of 12) and decodes each file without a complaint; tagging a FLAC file again leaves a single Vorbis comment block, with the new tags; files saved with no info have none; the NAME, AUTH and ANNO chunks ffmpeg writes into an AIFF file are read, and so is the ID3v2.4 chunk it writes into a 32-bit float AIFF-C file. The whole suite passed (1026 checks).
- SpectraBlade itself writes AIFF at 8, 16 or 24 bits only, so it never writes AIFF-C; it reads tags from AIFF-C files others write.
- Not tried by hand: saving from the app, or opening the files in other players (the Music app, Finder's info).

**Release 0.10.0: repair and generator tools, spectral drawing and healing, bus routing and ducking, MP3, AAC and ffmpeg, file tags**
- `SpectraBlade-0.10.0.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 208 to 225 below on top of 0.9.5. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (1015 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.10.0, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE` and LAME's licence, and launched from the mounted image (the main window in 7.1 seconds on that first launch, while macOS checked the new image, and 1.4 seconds on the next, reopening the last file) and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 225: Buses into buses**
- A bus can play into another bus in place of the mix: its header has an Output button (where a track has Sends) with the mix and the buses it may play into. A bus can't play into itself, nor into a bus that already plays into it, through any number of buses. Each bus is mixed before the buses it plays into, whatever their order in the list. A bus's lane says which bus it plays into, and the lane of the bus it plays into lists it as "(output)".
- Plug-in latency is lined up along the chain: a track feeding a bus is read ahead by that bus's latency plus that of the buses after it. Muting a bus silences the buses played into it; soloing a bus brings the buses it plays into and those that play into it, with the tracks feeding them. Deleting a bus sends the buses that played into it to the mix. Session files keep it, and so do a bus's templates, by the bus's name. Buses still have no sends.
- Measured by the tests, with a bus played into a second bus beside a track playing straight into the mix: the mix matches sums made by hand (worst difference 1.2e-7), with the buses either way round in the list; muting the second bus silences the first, and soloing either brings both and the tracks feeding them while leaving the other track out (worst 6e-8); with 300, 500 and 64 frames of latency on a track and both buses (564 along the chain), the mix lines up bit for bit with the dry one in 333- and 2900-frame blocks and from 2500 frames after priming; loops of two and three buses are refused and a chain of three allowed; session files and templates keep it. Mixes without bus outputs are unchanged: every earlier bus, latency, ducking and mixer test still passes. The whole suite passed (1015 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the Output button and list in the app.

**Milestone 224: File info (tags and broadcast-wave details)**
- File > File Info shows and changes a file's title, artist, album, genre, year, track and comment, and its broadcast-wave details (description, originator, originator reference, origination date and time; the time reference is shown, and kept). A change is an undo step, and other edits carry the info on.
- Opening reads it from WAV files (their LIST INFO and bext chunks), MP3 (ID3v2.3 and 2.4), MPEG-4 files (.m4a and the like, iTunes-style tags), FLAC and Ogg files such as Opus (Vorbis comments), and through ffmpeg from the formats it opens.
- Saving as WAV keeps all of it: the tags in a LIST INFO chunk (INAM, IART, IPRD, IGNR, ICRD, ITRK, ICMT), the broadcast details in a bext chunk. Export as MP3 or AAC writes the tags as ID3v2.3 (Latin-1, or UTF-16 where needed) ahead of LAME's tag frame, or into the .m4a's moov box, in room kept for them so no audio moves; Export with ffmpeg passes them to ffmpeg. AIFF and FLAC files saved here don't keep it, and Save says so; nor do WAV files over 4 GB.
- Measured by the tests (with ffmpeg 9.0.2 as an outside reader where it is installed): a WAV keeps every field, Unicode included, beside its markers, with its audio the same bit for bit; ffmpeg reads the same tags and broadcast details from it (showing bext's description and date in place of INFO's comment and year); a WAV with no info has neither chunk; the MP3 and .m4a exports keep the tags (ffmpeg agrees) and still read back at their exact length; a 4,000-character comment fits in an .m4a; ID3v2.4 tags written by hand (UTF-8, TDRC, a genre with its old number) are read; an Opus export through ffmpeg keeps them; a FLAC file ffmpeg wrote has its comments read; files with no info get no tags; the undo step.
- Also fixed: stopping an ffmpeg export could be missed when ffmpeg finished within its first 50 ms. A check in M220 that compared two .m4a files byte for byte now compares their audio data, as the header holds the time a file was written.
- Not tried by hand: the File Info dialog.

**Milestone 223: Opening and exporting through ffmpeg**
- With ffmpeg installed (it is not part of SpectraBlade; it is looked for in /opt/homebrew/bin, /usr/local/bin, /opt/local/bin and the PATH), files SpectraBlade can't read itself open through it: Opus, WMA, E-AC-3, Monkey's Audio, TTA, MKA and others, and the first audio stream of a video (MP4, MOV, MKV, WebM, AVI). They are decoded in full to 32-bit float; the open dialogs list them. Without ffmpeg, opening one of those types says that it needs ffmpeg.
- File > Export with ffmpeg writes the file or the selection as Opus (64 to 256 kbps), Apple Lossless, WavPack or AC-3 (192 to 640 kbps), through a temporary file, in the background; it can be stopped.
- Measured by the tests with ffmpeg 9.0.2, writing 2 s of 44.1 kHz stereo tones and opening the result again: WavPack the same bit for bit; Apple Lossless within 1.2e-7 (24-bit); Opus at 48 kHz, exactly 96,000 frames; AC-3 within 6.2e-4 of the original, with its last block filled out to 89,088 frames. WMA and MP2 were tried and left out: they came back shifted and short or long. Stopping leaves no file, and a file ffmpeg can't read gives its error. The tests skip themselves where ffmpeg isn't installed.
- Not tried by hand: the menu command and the dialogs.

**Milestone 222: File tabs only if you want them**
- The open files no longer show as tabs over the editor unless Window > File Tabs is ticked (kept for next time). The Files panel, in the Editing workspace by default, lists them either way; clicking one switches to it.
- Not tried by hand: the menu item.

**Milestone 221: Typing a volume into the mixer**
- Double-click a strip's dB readout in the mixer to type its volume: a number from -60 to +12, with or without "dB" or a "+", rounded to 0.1 dB as the fader sets it. It sets the track and its fader as an undo step; anything else (out of range, not a number) is refused and the readout shows the volume again. The master strip's readout and an automated volume's aren't typed into.
- Measured by the tests: the parsing (8 accepted and 8 refused examples), opening the readout with the volume in it, "-9.25 dB" setting -9.3 dB as "Track Volume", and "99" refused.
- Not tried by hand: double-clicking in the app.

**Milestone 220: Export as MP3 or AAC**
- File > Export as MP3 or AAC writes the file or the selection as MP3 (constant 96 to 320 kbps, or variable V0, V2 or V4) or as AAC in a .m4a file (96 to 320 kbps, an average the encoder aims at), in the background; it can be stopped. MP3 is encoded with LAME 4.0, which CMake fetches from its source release (checked against its SHA-256) and builds into the app; AAC with the encoder macOS has. Both hold one or two channels; audio above 48 kHz is written at 44.1 kHz (for the 44.1 kHz family) or 48 kHz. Markers aren't kept. The disk image now carries LAME's licence.
- At constant rates up to 224 kbps, LAME turns the audio down by 2 to 5% against clipping; the export undoes that, so the file keeps the audio's level. The .m4a files keep about 2.5 KB free for their index rather than the 55 KB the system leaves by default.
- Measured by the tests, with 4 s of 44.1 kHz sweeps (100 Hz to 8 kHz, half scale), read back by the app: every setting gives the same length and the same first sample (0 frames off, 0 over); the sweeps stay at 0.9986 to 1.0006 of their level, and above the error by 77.1 (MP3 320), 76.9 (192), 63.4 (128), 64.4 (V0), 37.6 (V2), 33.6 (V4), 57.7 (AAC 320), 50.3 (256), 43.4 (192), 37.3 (128) and 34.4 dB (96). On noise, constant MP3 rates come out 1.9% over (the tag frame, in 4 s), AAC 8.5 to 12.9% over; V0, V2 and V4 come to 255, 186 and 147 kbps. The MP3 files start with LAME's tag frame, the .m4a files hold 4,096 bytes besides the audio, writing again gives the same bytes (MP3) and the same audio data (.m4a, whose header holds the time it was written), 96 kHz is written at 48 kHz at the right length, mono stays mono, and three channels, unoffered settings and 4 kHz are refused, leaving the old file and no temporary one.
- Not tried by hand or listened to: the command, the dialog, and the files in other players.

**Milestone 219: Ducking**
- A track's routing panel (its Sends button) has Duck Under: another track that brings this one down while it is heard, music under a voice say. While the key track's output (after its volume) is above the threshold (-40 dB to start, -80 to 0), the track comes down by the amount (12 dB, 1 to 60) over the attack (10 ms, 1 to 500), and goes back up over the release (300 ms, 10 to 5000) once the key falls below it, its level read from its peaks falling away with a 20 ms time constant (so 78 ms after a voice at half scale stops, at -40 dB). It acts with the volume: the track's output and post-fader sends dip, its pre-fader sends don't. Keys are mixed before the tracks they duck, wherever they are in the list; a muted key, or one a solo leaves out, ducks nothing. The edits refuse a track keyed to itself, a bus as key or ducking on a bus, and loops of any length; deleting the key turns the ducking off, and freezing renders without it. Session files keep it.
- The key is lined up with the track it ducks unless the buses the ducked track feeds have more plug-in latency than the key's do; then the ducking comes late by the difference.
- Measured by the tests, with steady music ducking under a voice that talks from 0.5 to 1 s: the music is untouched bit for bit before the voice, -7.59 dB 10 ms in (the attack's -7.59 expected), -12.00 dB while it talks (within 0.0006 dB), -4.41 dB 300 ms into the release (-4.41 expected) and back within 0.006 dB by 3.4 s; the mix is the same bit for bit in 37-frame blocks as in 512, with the voice listed after the music; a muted voice ducks nothing; a pre-fader send stays at 0.000 dB while a post-fader send dips to -12.00; the edits and session files as above. Mixes with no ducking are unchanged: every earlier bus, latency and mixer test still passes bit for bit. The whole suite passed (940 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the controls in the app.

**Milestone 218: Track outputs into buses**
- A track's Sends panel now starts with Output: Mix, or a bus that the track plays into in place of the mix, after its volume, so several tracks can share a bus's effects and fader. Its sends stay as they were. The Sends button lights while a track plays into a bus, and the bus's lane lists it as "(output)" among what feeds it.
- Muting the bus silences the tracks played into it (their sends still play); soloing the bus brings them, and soloing such a track brings its bus. Plug-in latency on the track and the bus is lined up as for sends. Deleting the bus sends its tracks back to the mix; freezing renders without the output, which then still applies. Session files keep it, and so do track templates, by the bus's name.
- Still not offered: a bus played into another bus, a track sent to other outputs of the audio interface, and sidechain inputs on plug-ins.
- Measured by the tests: a track played into a bus is heard through it and not in the mix, its sends unchanged (worst difference 6e-8 against sums made by hand); muting and soloing as above, likewise; with 300, 500 and 64 frames of latency on the track and both buses, the routed mix lines up bit for bit with the dry one, from the start and after priming; the edits refuse a bus, a track as the destination, and what is already so; deleting the bus and freezing as above; session files and templates keep it, and a template says when there's no such bus. The whole suite passed (935 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the Output list in the app.

**Milestone 217: Lasso, brush and Heal in the spectrogram**
- Edit > Lasso Selection (D) and Brush Selection (P): a plain drag in a spectrogram draws round what to select, or paints over it; Shift-drag adds to the drawing. Box Selection (E) is the way it was: drag to select time, Option-drag a box. The brush is a circle on screen (12 pixels to start, 3 to 60 with [ and ]) shown under the mouse. The drawing is the selection: its span in time is the time selection, and choosing any other selection clears it; the file keeps it when you switch tabs.
- Effects > Heal (Shift-Cmd-H) replaces what is inside a drawing or a box with the sound around it: in each frequency bin the level across it is drawn evenly in dB from the level just before to the level just after (the RMS of four frames each side), the phase carried on from the frame before at the rate its spectral peak turned there, then 16 rounds of Griffin and Lim's method bring the frames to agree. Attenuate Frequencies and Remove Frequencies take drawings too. Drawings are edited with a 2048-point transform (43 ms at 48 kHz), whose frames count as inside wherever their window meets the drawing; the edges fade over two frames and two bins.
- Measured by the tests: a lasso holds what is between its edges and a brush dab its ellipse; removing a lasso drawn over a 4 kHz tone for a second takes it down 144 dB while a 500 Hz tone beside it and the 4 kHz tone before it change by under 0.001 dB, and every sample more than 3072 from the drawing stays bit for bit; a brush stroke at -12 dB lowers the tone by 12.00 dB. Healing: a 30 ms 3 kHz burst 28 dB above noise heals to 1.2 dB from the noise alone, the band below it unchanged; a click 26 times the noise's RMS heals to a peak of 4 times it, the level around it 0.5 dB from the noise's; a 1 kHz tone under a 50 ms burst of loud noise runs on through at its level with the burst down 34.8 dB; and healing 10 s of stereo at every frequency takes 0.27 s. In the view: a drawn lasso selects what is inside it and the time it spans, Shift adds a second, a click without drawing clears it, the brush paints a band as wide as its circle, another selection clears the drawing and the file's place keeps it. The whole suite passed (930 checks).
- Not tried by hand: drawing with the mouse in the app, or healing a real recording.

**Milestone 216: Generating tones and noise**
- Effects > Generate Tone... puts a tone in at the cursor, or the start of the selection, moving what follows on, and selects it: sine, square, triangle or sawtooth, 1 Hz to below half the sample rate, the same on every channel. An end frequency unlike the start makes a sweep, even in octaves. The level is the peak (-96 to 0 dBFS), the ends fade over 5 ms to start (0 to 1000 ms), and the length goes up to ten minutes. Square and sawtooth have their jumps smoothed (polyBLEP), and the triangle is the smoothed square integrated, so they alias less than drawn straight.
- Effects > Generate Noise... puts in white, pink or brown noise the same way, each channel its own. The level is the RMS: white up to -6 dBFS, pink and brown up to -20, so the peaks stay below full scale. Each run is new noise.
- Both dialogs keep their settings and presets, and Repeat runs them again. They need a file open: there is no new empty file yet.
- Measured by the tests: a 1 kHz sine at -12 dBFS peaks at -12.000 dBFS on both channels alike and fades in and out; square, sawtooth and triangle at 100 Hz peak at the level (the sawtooth at 0.996 of it) with the ideal fundamental to 0.1%; a 5 kHz sawtooth's sixth harmonic folds back to 18 kHz at -28.4 dB against the fundamental, against -15.6 drawn straight; a 100-1600 Hz sweep over 4 s is at 200 Hz after 1 s and 800 Hz after 3 s; 10 s of each noise at its loudest is exactly that RMS, with 2-4 kHz against 200-400 Hz at +10.2 dB for white, +0.2 for pink and -9.7 for brown, the channels uncorrelated (at most 0.055) and the peaks at -1.2, -7.0 and -8.2 dBFS; the same seed repeats; and generated audio goes in bit for bit with the rest unchanged. The whole suite passed (917 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the dialogs in the app.

**Milestone 215: Repair Clipping**
- Effects > Repair Clipping... rebuilds clipped peaks in the selection, or the whole file. A clipped peak is two or more samples in a row, of one sign, held level at or above a share of each channel's own peak (99% to start, 50 to 100%), so clipping at any level is found and a sine's rounded top is not. Each is rebuilt as the cubic that best fits the six samples on either side of it, never dipping back inside the clipped level; the samples that weren't clipped stay as they were. Rebuilt peaks rise above the clipped level, so Keep the Peak Level (on to start) lowers the selection until they reach it and no higher. Clipping longer than 5 ms is left as it is, and the app says how many such stretches it found, or that it found nothing to rebuild.
