- Measured by the tests: a 500 Hz sine at 1.5 clipped at 1.0 gets all 200 peaks rebuilt, its peak back to 1.438, the clipped samples' error against the true sine down from 0.367 to 0.043 RMS, every other sample left bit for bit; keeping the peak scales it by 0.6953 to a peak of 1.0; the same clipping at half scale is found alike; unclipped sines at 100 Hz, 500 Hz and 3 kHz have nothing found; 25 ms flat stretches are counted and left. The whole suite passed (909 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the dialog in the app, or a recording that really clipped.

**Milestone 214: Remove Hum**
- Effects > Remove Hum... takes mains hum out of the selection, or the whole file: a narrow notch at 50 or 60 Hz and at each harmonic up to the number given (8 to start, up to 40, below 0.45 of the sample rate), each as wide in hertz as the first (the mains frequency over the narrowness: 1.7 Hz at 50 Hz and 30). The notches settle first on a second of audio: what comes before the selection, and where there isn't a second of it (at the file's start), the audio turned about its first frame, which runs on into it without a jump or a kink, so the hum goes from the first frame. The dialog keeps its settings and presets; it has no preview.
- Measured by the tests, on two seconds of 50 Hz hum with its third harmonic and a 1 kHz tone: the hum down 105 dB on both channels in the second second and 48 dB in the first tenth of a second, the tone changed by less than 0.001 dB; on a selection starting half a second in, the audio outside left bit for bit and the hum inside down 59 dB; at 60 Hz, 60 Hz down 125 dB with 50 Hz changed by 0.04 dB; and the limits refused. The whole suite passed (904 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the dialog in the app, or a recording with real hum.

**Milestone 213: Going to edges, and repeating the last effect**
- Transport > Go to Previous Edge and Go to Next Edge (Cmd-Left and Cmd-Right) move the cursor to the nearest edge before or after it, scrolling it into view. In a file the edges are its start and end and the selection's ends; moving the cursor takes the selection away, as a click does, so from a selection Next Edge goes to its end and then to the file's end. In a session they are every clip's start and end on the tracks shown in the lanes, the time selection's ends, and the session's start and end; the time selection stays.
- Edit > Repeat (Shift-R) runs the last command from the Effects menu again, named in the menu ("Repeat Normalize", say); an effect with a dialog opens it again. It works on files, not in the session.
- The Audition-style key set gives these the same keys (Cmd-Left, Cmd-Right, Shift-R), and its note says so; Cmd-R stays Apply Effects Rack.
- Measured by the tests: the edges of a file with and without a selection, walked both ways from either end and from inside the selection, none beyond the ends; a session's clip edges on two tracks in time order both ways, a hidden track's clips passed over, the time selection's ends among them, and an empty session with nowhere to go. The whole suite passed (899 checks).
- Not tried by hand: the keys and menu items in the app; Repeat has no test of its own (it lives in the main window, which the tests don't build).

**Milestone 212: Waveform colours**
- View > Theme has a Waveform section: the theme's own colour, or Green, Blue, Amber, Rose or Grey, each in a lighter tone for the dark themes and a darker one for Light. The choice is kept for the next launch and applies at once, as the theme does.
- docs/SHORTCUTS.md lost the comma from two keys (Nudge Earlier and Audio Settings); both are put right, and it lists the keys and gestures added in Milestones 209 to 211.
- Measured by the tests: each of the six waveform colours stands out at 3:1 or better against the canvas and both lane colours of all four themes (3.7:1 at least); a chosen colour replaces the theme's own, in the darker tone on Light, and "Theme" or an unknown name keeps the theme's. The whole suite passed (893 checks).
- Seen in captures of the window: Amber on Dark and Rose on Light. Not tried by hand: choosing from the menu.

**Milestone 211: The times under the editor**
- The strip under the editor shows, left of the zoom controls, the cursor's time, the selection's start, end and length ("none" without one) and the view's start and end, updated ten times a second. Click the cursor's time for Go to Time, or the selection's for Set Selection; their tooltips show the keys. When the strip is narrow the view's field is left out first, then the selection's. The Selection/View panel stays, for those who prefer it.
- Measured by the tests: the cursor (0:00.025), no selection and the whole view of a 0.1-second file, then a selection from 0:00.020 to 0:00.050 shown with its length and the cursor at its start; the fields left to right before the zoom controls, a click on the cursor's running Go to Time, on the selection's Set Selection and on the view's nothing; and, narrowing the strip a pixel at a time, the view's field going at 848 pixels, the selection's at 680 and the cursor's at 418, none overlapping the controls. The whole suite passed (891 checks).
- Seen in a capture of the window, in the Light theme. Not tried by hand: clicking the fields in the app, or the times while playing and dragging.

**Milestone 210: Vertical zoom, and zoom controls under the editor**
- The waveform can be drawn taller than full scale, to see quiet audio: from 1x (full scale reaching the lane's edges) to 64x, peaks beyond the edges clipped there, and the level the edges stand for shown at each lane's top left ("edge -12.0 dB"). With a file showing, View > Zoom In Vertically and Zoom Out Vertically (Option-Cmd-= and Option-Cmd-minus, the keys that make tracks taller and shorter in a session) halve or double it, Full Scale Vertically (Option-Cmd-0) puts it back, and Option-scrolling over the waveform zooms vertically.
- A strip under the editor holds the zoom controls: zoom out, zoom in and Fit, then the vertical zoom ("Level 1x") with its out, in and full-scale buttons. Each runs its command, and its tooltip shows the keys. The buttons that would do nothing are greyed out. It hides in the multitrack view.
- Measured by the tests: a -12 dB sine drawn 2 and 4 times as far from the middle at 2x and 4x (39, 78 and 156 pixels in a 400-pixel view), and no further at 8x and 16x; the zoom kept between 1x and 64x and the edge levels (0 dB at 1x, -6.02 at 2x, -36.12 at 64x); Option-scrolling zooming vertically while a plain scroll zooms in time; the strip's six buttons in order, each running its command, its readout following the zoom, and the buttons that can't act greyed out. The whole suite passed (888 checks).
- Seen in a capture of the window: the strip under the editor. Not tried by hand: the buttons, the keys and Option-scrolling in the app.

**Milestone 209: Panel menus, and maximising a panel**
- Each group of panels has a menu button (three dots) at the right of its tabs, with what right-clicking a tab offers (close, float, dock at an edge) for the panel in front, and Maximize. Maximize shows that group alone, filling the dock; the dots turn the accent colour, and the same menu then offers Restore All Panels. Double-clicking a tab, or the header beside the tabs, maximises its group or puts the others back. Window > Maximize Editor (Shift-Cmd-F) does the same for the editor, and reads Restore All Panels while one is maximised. Maximising doesn't change the layout kept in the workspace; any change to the layout (showing, closing or moving a panel, or switching workspace) shows every group again.
- Measured by the tests: a maximised group fills the dock with its other tabs, the other groups' panels are taken out and none is let go, and the layout is unchanged; restoring puts every group back; a change to the layout, or switching layouts, restores; a panel that isn't docked can't be maximised; double-clicking a tab maximises and double-clicking again restores, and a double-click inside the panel does nothing. The whole suite passed (881 checks).
- Seen in a capture of the window: the menu button on each group. Not tried by hand: the menu, maximising from it or from the Window menu, and double-clicking a tab (the tests double-click through the same function).

**Milestone 208: A denser layout**
- More room for the audio: the window's margins went from 10 to 6 pixels and the gaps around the panels from 8 to 4; the toolbar is 30 pixels high (34 before), with the transport and zoom buttons 28 square and the time 170 wide (190); panel tabs are 22 high (26) and the file tabs over the editor 24 (30), both in 12-point text with smaller close buttons; the devices and status lines take 46 pixels (52).
- Measured by the tests: the transport bar's ideal width holds its ten buttons and the time with no more than a gap to spare, each button as tall as the bar, and the tab heights. The whole suite passed (874 checks).
- Together the waveform gains 36 pixels of height in a window of the same size (26 from the margins, gaps, toolbar and status lines, 4 from the panel tabs and 6 from the file tabs). Seen in a capture of the window: the Editing workspace with two files.

**Release 0.9.5: docked panels, several files, meters, fade handles and themes**
- `SpectraBlade-0.9.5.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 198 to 207 below on top of 0.9.4. The first release published as a regular release rather than a pre-release, so it shows as the repository's latest. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (872 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's SHA-256 matched its `.sha256`, its app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.9.5, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image (the main window in 2.57 seconds, reopening the last file) and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 207: Colour themes**
- View > Theme switches the whole interface between four themes, Dark (as before), Darker, Light and High Contrast, and four accents, Blue (as before), Teal, Orange and Violet. The switch takes effect at once, with no restart: every panel, control, menu, dialog and floating window, the waveform (redrawn in the new colours), the multitrack lanes and the mixer. The choice is kept for the next launch. The spectrogram's colours, and the track, marker and automation colours, are the same in every theme, since they carry meaning in the audio; the overlays drawn on the audio (grid lines, beat lines, the time selection) are white on the dark themes and black on Light.
- The transport icons and the fade handles' ramps no longer assume a dark background: the icons take the theme's text colour, and the ramps stay white on the accent.
- Measured by the tests: in each of the 16 combinations of theme and accent, body text reads at 7:1 or better on every surface (10.4:1 at least, as measured), secondary text at 4.5:1 or better (5.2:1 at least), and text on a button that is on, on a selected row and in the clock at 4.5:1 or better, with the accent, the cursor, the waveform and the danger colour at 3:1 or better (WCAG ratios); an unknown theme or accent falls back to Dark and Blue; switching sets every colour, the controls' look and the colours given to controls, and switching back restores them; and the waveform view redraws its background in the new theme. The whole suite passed (872 checks).
- Seen in captures of the window: Dark with two files and with the demo session, Light with two files, Darker in the Analysis workspace and High Contrast in the Classic one. Not tried by hand: switching from the menu while the app runs (the tests switch through the same function).

**Milestone 206: Opening several files at once, as tabs**
- File > Open takes several files at once (Shift- or Command-click them), and dropping several files on the editor, or opening several from Finder, opens every one of them; before, only one was opened (the last, or the first dropped). Each opens beside the files already open and the last one shows; each is fitted to the window when first shown. A file already open is switched to, and the files that can't be read are listed in one message.
- The open files show as tabs over the editor: each with its name and a × (a dot in its place while unsaved), the one showing marked with the accent; click a tab to show its file, release on its × (or right-click it) to close it. The tabs are as wide as their names need, up to a limit, and share the width when there are many; they hide in the multitrack view and while the editor is empty.
- The fade handles now always show the accent, each holding a small ramp, rising for the fade in and falling for the fade out, so the two mirror each other.
- Measured by the tests: the tabs (none while the editor is empty, one per file side by side and sized by their names, clicks showing a file, the × closing one only when released over it, and sharing the width when squeezed); dropping several files handing on every audio file in order and leaving out the rest; the two fade handles drawn as mirror images, in the accent. The whole suite passed (872 checks).
- Tried in the app: three files opened together from Finder's route (`open` with three files) came up as three tabs with the last showing (seen in a capture of the window). Not tried by hand: choosing several in the Open dialog and dropping several (they go the same way, through the same function).

**Milestone 205: Fade handles on the waveform**
- Two small handles sit at the top corners of the waveform. Drag the left one to the right to fade the file in, and the right one to the left to fade it out; drag up to bend the curve so it rises sooner, down so it starts slowly (a straight line in between, from -100% to +100%). The fade's curve is drawn over each channel with what it takes away shaded, and a readout beside the handle gives the length and curve while dragging. The waveform follows as you drag (up to 30 times a second), and the fades stay adjustable while they are the last edit: dragging either handle again redoes both from the audio as it was, so they remain one undo step ("Fade In", "Fade Out" or "Fade In and Out"). After another edit, an undo or a redo, a new drag starts a new fade on the audio as it is. Double-click a handle to take its fade away. The two fades never overlap.
- Measured by the tests: the curves (straight at 0, 0.84 halfway at +100%, 0.06 halfway at -100%, from 0 to 1 and never falling); the faded samples at both ends against the curve, and the middle untouched; the fade out shortened rather than overlapping, and no fades making nothing; dragging again redoing both from the audio before them in one step, the limits, an undo taking both away, a new fade on top of another edit, and a fade set to nothing taking its step away; and in the real view, the handles at the corners, a drag setting the fade in's length and curve with the handle following, the fade out's handle, a later drag keeping both in one step without selecting anything, and a double-click taking one fade away. The whole suite passed (867 checks).
- Seen in a render of the view with a test signal, mid-drag: both curves over each channel, the shading and the readout. Not tried by hand in the app.

**Milestone 204: One look of SpectraBlade's own**
- The interface's colours, sizes and corners now come from one theme (`Source/Theme.h`): four surfaces from the darkest well to raised controls, three weights of text, one blue accent, and green, amber and red for what is good, a warning or clipped. A look-and-feel of its own draws the standard controls with it: flat buttons with a thin edge (a button that is on is the deep accent with an accent edge, the same for the Multitrack, Sends and FX buttons), menus and combo boxes with a small chevron, text fields in a well with the accent round the one being typed in, thin scroll bars that widen under the mouse, and tooltips and dialogs on the panels' colours (dialogs and plug-in windows were a mid grey before). About 135 colours written into the views were put onto the theme, where 13 slightly different dark greys for backgrounds, five blues and five reds had grown up. The colours that mean something in the audio (the waveform, the spectrogram's scale, track, marker and automation colours) are unchanged.
- Measured by the tests: body text reads at 10.4:1 or better on every surface, secondary text at 5.2:1 or better on all but the hover colour, and text on a button that is on, on a selected row, the clock, the accent and the cursor at 4.5:1 or better (WCAG ratios); a button draws in the raised colour and, when on, the deep accent with an accent edge; text fields, combo boxes and scroll bars draw in their theme colours; tooltips sit beside the mouse. The whole suite passed (855 checks).
- Seen in captures of the window: the Editing workspace with two files and the Multitrack workspace with the demo session, in the theme.

**Milestone 203: Frequency analysis and a phase meter**
- Window > Frequency Analysis: the spectrum of what goes to the outputs (the last 8,192 frames, both channels mixed, Hann-windowed), on a log scale from 20 Hz to 20 kHz and -90 to 0 dB, falling back 0.6 dB a frame; the frequency and level under the mouse are shown. Window > Phase Meter: a goniometer of the last 2,048 frames (mono straight up, out of phase across) and their correlation from -1 to +1. Both read a copy of the output kept as it plays, after the master meter. A new Analysis workspace has the editor and rack with both panels under them and the levels along the bottom.
- Measured by the tests: a full-scale sine on a bin reads 0.0 dB and one at half the level -6.0 dB, with -100 dB or less 28 bins away; a sine between bins reads within 1.5 dB of its level; silence reads -150 dB; the frequency scale and its inverse; the panel's fall; correlation +1, -1, near 0 for unrelated tones and 0 with a channel silent; the goniometer's directions; the panel's smoothing; and the copy of the output, in order, padded before the first block, a mono block in both channels, and across the end of its ring. The whole suite passed (847 checks).
- Seen in the app: the Analysis workspace laid out as above with the device open, and the panels drawn offscreen with a test signal (peaks at 110 Hz, 880 Hz and 3 kHz; correlation +0.99). Not seen: the panels following audio as it plays in the app.

**Milestone 202: The file's undo history, and a levels panel**
- The Undo History panel lists the open file's steps in the editor (each file keeps its own) and the session's in the multitrack view; clicking a step goes back or forward to it, as before for sessions.
- Window > Levels: a meter of what goes to the outputs as two long bars across the panel, on a dB scale from -60 to 0, with a falling peak hold, the highest peak of each channel since the last reset in numbers, and clip lights; click it to reset them. The scale is left out when the panel is too short for it. The Editing workspace has it along the bottom and the undo history among the rack's tabs.
- Measured by the tests: the file's steps listed from Start, going back and forward through them, the session's steps with the session showing, and each file's steps kept with it when switching; the levels' dB scale, the highest peaks (-6.0 and -12.0 dB for 0.5 and 0.25), a clip at +1.0 dB and the reset. The whole suite passed (837 checks).
- Seen in captures of the window: the Editing workspace with the levels along the bottom and the history tab.

**Milestone 201: Several files open, and the Files panel**
- Files open beside each other instead of in place of the one open: File > Open, a drop, Open Recent or a file from Finder adds it, or switches to it if it is open already. Each keeps its own undo history, selection, zoom, cursor and spectral band, put back when it is shown again. Window > Files lists them with their length, rate, channels and bit depth, marks the one in the editor and dots unsaved ones; click one to show it, and its × or right-click closes it. File > Close File (Cmd-W) closes the one showing, asking about unsaved changes, and Next File and Previous File step through them. Quitting asks about each unsaved file in turn. A new Editing workspace has the files on the left.
- Measured by the tests: opening a second file keeps the first aside with its history; switching back and forth puts each one's audio, history and place back; closing the one showing brings in its neighbour, and closing the last leaves the editor empty; the unsaved ones found; the panel's rows, clicks and close buttons. The whole suite passed (832 checks).
- Seen in captures of the window: the Editing workspace with three files listed.

**Milestone 200: The audio device after a launch without one**
- A device setup saved while no device was open names none, and restoring it opened nothing: once SpectraBlade had quit without a device (its audio service stuck, say), every later launch showed "No audio device" until one was chosen by hand. A saved setup that names no device is now passed over, so the default device opens, as with nothing saved; and the setup is saved only while a device is open, so the one chosen is asked for again next time.
- Measured by the tests: a setup naming no device is passed over and opens the default device. In the app, with such a setup in the settings, the build before showed "No audio device" and this one opened the default interface.

**Milestone 199: Docked panels and workspaces**
- The effects rack, the selection and view times, the mixer, clip properties, the clip list, the marker list, the undo history and the session notes are panels docked around the editor, instead of windows of their own beside it. Panels sit side by side, stacked or as tabs in one group; drag a tab onto the edge of another group to dock it there, onto its middle to join its tabs, or out of the main window to float it in a window of its own (drag that tab back in to dock it again). A tab's menu closes it, floats it, or docks it along an edge of the whole window. The bars between groups share out the room. A panel closed with its × comes back from the Window menu, or its old command, where it goes by default (the mixer under the editor, the lists among the rack's tabs). The keys work with a floating panel in front.
- Window > Workspace keeps layouts: Classic (the editor with the rack and the selection beside it, as before) and Multitrack (the mixer under the editor, clip properties over the rack, markers and clip list), and any saved with Save Workspace As. Each keeps its layout as last left, across launches; Reset Workspace puts the current one back as it was saved; one of your own can be deleted. A rack hidden before this version stays hidden.
- Measured by the tests: docking beside, above and in the middle of a group, at the window's edges, and moving, floating and closing panels, each layout checked by its shape and shares; layouts written and read back, through their text too, leaving out panels that aren't known and putting right a doubled panel, a tab out of range and a weight below zero, and refusing one without the editor; workspaces kept apart from how they were saved, reset, saved as, replaced, restored and deleted; and the dock itself with stand-in panels: groups sized by their weights with a bar between, panels shown where they go and brought to the front, closing, dropping and floating them in a window, and the editor neither closed nor floated. The whole suite passed (817 checks).
- Tried in the app: the Classic and Multitrack workspaces laid out as above, with a file and with a session (seen in captures of the window). Not tried by hand: dragging tabs and bars, floating a panel and the tab menu.

**Milestone 198: Double-click selects the whole file**
- A double-click in a channel's waveform or spectrogram selects the whole file. Double-clicking a marker still renames it, and the ruler, the marker lane and the line between waveform and spectrogram are as before.
- Measured by the tests, through the real view: a double-click in the waveform and one in the spectrogram select every frame; one in the ruler and one in the marker lane leave the selection empty.

**Release 0.9.4: the fifth alpha**
- `SpectraBlade-0.9.4.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestone 197 below (a loading screen, and the audio device opened without holding it up) on top of 0.9.3. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (766 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.9.4, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image (the loading screen in 1.06 seconds and the main window in 3.79 seconds on that first launch; later launches of the built app took about 1.1 seconds) and quit with no crash report, with `afplay` playing normally before and after. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 197: A loading screen, and the audio device opened without holding it up**
- At launch a small window (600 by 380, a quarter of the main window's room) shows the SpectraBlade logo where the main window will be, and under it what is loading: the plug-in list, the audio device (by name when one is saved) and the last file. The main window shows when they are done, after the logo has been up at least 0.7 seconds. If loading takes more than 3 seconds a Continue button comes up, with a note for a step that has one (a device or a volume that isn't answering, and what clears it); Continue shows the main window at once, and what is still loading carries on behind.
- The audio device is first asked for on a thread of its own, with a device object of its own: making the device and asking what it offers, the calls a stuck audio service was seen to wait in. Once that answers, the device opens on the message thread as before. Until it is open, Play, Record and Audio Settings do nothing and are greyed out; while loading, every command but About is.
- Measured by the tests: the loading screen shows the logo and the newest step, stays up its least time, finishes once when the steps end, brings up Continue and the step's note for a long step, and ignores steps after it has finished; the probe, run off the message thread, finds the device the engine opens, a saved one, and the default for a saved one that has gone. The whole suite passed.
- Measured in the app: over three launches the loading screen appeared in 0.51 to 0.65 seconds and the main window in 1.07 to 1.11 seconds (the first launch after a build took 2.68 seconds); the build before showed its window in about 0.6 seconds, so the logo's least time adds about half a second. With the last file on a share that had stopped answering, the loading screen showed "Reopening take.wav", its note and Continue at 4 seconds, and the main window came up with the file once the share answered again (seen in captures of the loading screen). Not tried: an audio service that doesn't answer (it can't be brought about on purpose), and pressing Continue by hand.

**Release 0.9.3: the fourth alpha**
- `SpectraBlade-0.9.3.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestone 196 below (a quick launch with a network volume down) on top of 0.9.2. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (763 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.9.3, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image (its window in 1.1 seconds) and quit with no crash report. A first launch showed no window because the Mac's audio service had stopped answering (`afplay` waited too); after restarting it, the launch above passed. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 196: A quick launch with a network volume down**
- The window no longer waits for a volume that doesn't answer. The last file is reopened on a thread of its own and comes into the editor when read, unless something else was opened, recorded or edited first; one whose volume isn't there is kept for next time, and one that can't be read is forgotten as before. The recent files under the empty editor, and in File > Open Recent, are checked off the message thread too, and a file shows once it is found there; the empty editor used to check every recent file each time it painted, and the menu each time it was built.
- Measured by the tests: a file check that never returns leaves the caller within 50 ms with no answer, runs once per file at a time, reports when it ends, and is made again once its answer is a few seconds old; checks still running when their owner goes end quietly; a file read on its own thread is handed to the message thread, and one that isn't there gives nothing and no error; the empty editor lists the recent files once checked. The whole suite passed.
- Measured on a WebDAV share mounted from a server that was then paused (a `stat` on it waited more than 15 seconds), with the last file and the first recent file on it: 0.9.2 showed no window in 30 seconds, then opened with the file 0.6 seconds after the server came back. This build showed its window in 0.8, 3.9, 0.9 and 1.0 seconds over four launches (0.6 seconds with no share mounted); a sample of the last launch's message thread found no file calls. When the file's read waited on the share, it came into the editor after the server came back (2.9 seconds after, in the one run timed). What held the 3.9-second launch up was not found.

**Release 0.9.2: the third alpha**
- `SpectraBlade-0.9.2.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestone 195 below (the playback crash at a file's end) on top of 0.9.1. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (760 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.9.2, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 195: No crash at a file's end**
- Playback could crash as it reached the end of a file (seen once in 0.9.0). The rate converter in front of the file player sometimes asks for audio that starts past the file's end; the file's read then counted backwards from its last piece and cleared memory in front of the buffer it was writing to, wiping that buffer's own channel table, and the next channel's write landed at an address near zero. The 0.9.0 crash report shows exactly that: the clear at the end of the read, writing 145 frames from 110 frames before the buffer's start. A read past the end now copies nothing and writes silence only where it was asked to.
- Measured by the tests: reads from past the end, across it, and through the playback source leave everything outside the frames asked for untouched and give silence past the end; playing a 1,000-frame file to its end through the rate-converting transport, at 44.1, 48 and 96 kHz with blocks of 64, 100, 480 and 512, made 3 reads that started past the end, all silent and in place. Before the fix the test program crashed (segmentation fault) at these checks. The whole suite passed, and the app launched and quit with no crash report.

**Release 0.9.1: the second alpha**
- `SpectraBlade-0.9.1.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256: Milestones 190 to 194 below, on top of 0.9.0. Universal app, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Download and install](README.md#download-and-install)).
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (758 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.9.1, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 194: Windows stay where you put them**
- The main window, the mixer, the Plug-ins, Keyboard Shortcuts, Marker List, Clip List, Clip Properties, Undo History, Session Notes and Parametric EQ windows, and each plug-in's editor (by plug-in) open where they were last left. A place is saved as the window moves or resizes, so a crash doesn't lose it. A plug-in's editor gets back only its position; its size stays the plug-in's own.
- A window whose top edge no longer shows on any screen (a display unplugged, say) opens centred on the main screen instead, shrunk to fit if need be.
- A bar between the editor and the effects rack drags the rack wider or narrower, from 220 pixels to 60% of the room; the width is remembered. Until it is dragged, the rack keeps its old width.
- Measured by the tests: a window moved to 240, 180 at 420 by 310 comes back there; with its own size kept, only the position comes back; off every screen, or with its top above them, a window is centred on the main screen and shrunk to fit; with its top showing it stays put; the bar reports where it is dragged in its parent. In the running app, a saved place of 300, 200 at 1100 by 650 opened the main window there, and a saved rack width of 500 showed the rack that wide (seen in a capture of the window). Dragging the bar and moving windows by hand were not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 190: Auditioning files before opening them**
- File > Open and Multitrack > Import Audio have a strip under the file list: a Preview checkbox that plays each audio file as you select it, a Play/Stop button for the selected file, and a line describing it (format, rate, channels, depth, length). The checkbox is remembered.
- The file plays once from its start, as it is, converted to the device's rate; it goes out with the metronome's click, past the effects rack, and leaves the open file or session where it was. Closing the panel stops it.
- Measured by the tests: a stereo float file comes out of the player's second output equal to the file (within 0.000000001), left and right apart, nothing on the main output, stopping by itself at its end and at once when stopped; the engine refuses a file that isn't audio; the strip plays nothing unticked, plays each audio file when ticked, stops on a file that isn't audio, and Play and Stop work either way. The strip was not seen in the real open panel (its menus can't be driven by script on the development Mac). The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 191: Rack buttons that respond**
- With plug-ins loaded, the effects rack's Edit, on/off and remove buttons could ignore clicks. Loaded plug-ins can make macOS announce changes to its lists of audio and MIDI devices many times a second; each announcement went on to the engine's listeners, and the rack rebuilt its rows every time, so a row was often replaced between a button's press and its release. Measured in the app with two plug-ins loaded: about 27 rebuilds a second before, 2 in 22 seconds after.
- The engine now passes on a device change only when the device in use, its rate, block size or channels changed, and the rack rebuilds its rows only when its plug-ins or their on/off states change.
- Measured by the tests: twenty device announcements reach none of the engine's listeners, an engine change that leaves the rack alone keeps its rows, and turning a slot off rebuilds them. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 192: Plug-ins by format**
- Adding an effect, to the rack or to a track (its FX button), now offers Audio Units and VST3 as separate submenus, each with a submenu a maker and the plug-ins sorted by name.
- This also fixes adding an effect from a track's FX menu, which had done nothing: the menu took any choice numbered above 1,000,000 for one of the track's own slots, and JUCE 9 numbers plug-in choices far above that. The new menus number them from 1.
- Measured by the tests: each of the 6 effects the test scan found (Audio Units and VST3) appears once, under its format and maker, sorted, Audio Units first, with ids below the track menu's own. The track menu's fault was found by reading its code; adding an effect from it was not tried by hand, before or after. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 193: Sizing the spectrogram**
- In View > Waveform and Spectrogram, drag the line between a channel's waveform and its spectrogram to give either more room (the pointer changes over it). The waveform's share, 35% to start, is kept between 10% and 90%, applies to every channel, and is remembered.
- Measured by the tests: dragging the second channel's line to 60% of its height sets the share to 0.600 and remembers it when the drag ends, without moving the cursor or the selection; the share is kept within 0.1 to 0.9; a drag elsewhere still selects. The line was not dragged by hand in the running app. The whole suite passed.

**Release 0.9.0: the alpha**
- The first build offered for download, as an alpha to test before 1.0: `SpectraBlade-0.9.0.dmg` on the repository's Releases page, with its SHA-256. It holds the universal app (Apple silicon and Intel) and the licence as `LICENSE.txt`, signed ad hoc, not notarized (see [Installing](README.md#download-and-install)). Everything in [Features](README.md#features) through Milestone 189 is in it.
- The version steps back from 1.0.0 to 0.9.0: the 1.0.0 disk image below was built on the development Mac but never published, and 1.0 now waits until the alpha has been tried.
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite (747 checks) passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 0.9.0 with its copyright line, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image and quit with no crash report. The app's Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 189: Audition-style keys**
- The keyboard shortcuts window has Key Sets... with Audition-Style Keys, for people coming from Adobe Audition. After asking (it starts from the defaults, so your own changes go; export them first), it gives these commands Audition's default keys: 1 switches between the file and the multitrack session, = and - zoom in and out, Option-Cmd-Left and Right go to the previous and next marker, Option-comma and Option-period nudge, and Shift-T opens Convert Sample Rate. Space, Home, End, M, Shift-P, Cmd-K, Cmd-T and S already matched. Each command keeps its own keys too, and any key taken from another command is listed afterwards.
- Where the keys come from: Adobe's own list of default shortcuts (helpx.adobe.com, updated June 2026), which Adobe calls partial; and, where it says nothing, keys two published cheat sheets (pie-menu.com and prismmultimedia.com) agree on. Keys only one cheat sheet gives, or that contradict Adobe's list, are left out.
- Not carried over, because SpectraBlade has no such command: going to clip and selection edges, vertical zoom, repeating the last command, editing one channel, the spectral display's scale and resolution, nudging clips between tracks, and Return to Start on Stop. The window says so before applying the set.
- Measured by the tests: a set starts from the defaults, gives each key to its command (keeping the command's own), reports a key taken from another, and passes over a command that doesn't exist. The Audition set itself was applied in the running app through a temporary hook (since removed): all 16 keys reached the intended commands and none was taken from another. Choosing it from the window was not tried by hand. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 188: Listening to what the de-esser takes out**
- The de-esser's dialog has a Listen choice for its preview, "Only to what it takes out", to set the frequency and threshold by hearing exactly what they catch; OK always applies the result. The live effect has it as a Listen switch, for the rack and tracks.
- Measured by the tests: on noise bursts, what it takes out and the result add up to the sound with nothing taken (the split's phase turn only) to 1.8e-7, on the band and on the whole sound, and with Listen off again it gives the offline samples bit for bit. It was not listened to. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 187: De-esser**
- Effects > De-esser: whenever the highs above a frequency (1 to 16 kHz, 6 kHz to start) are louder than the threshold, they are turned down by however far they are over, up to the reduction (0 to 24 dB), within a millisecond, coming back over 60 ms. It turns down the high band alone, leaving the rest of the voice as it was, or the whole sound. Applied to the selection or the whole file, with a preview and presets; it is also a built-in live effect for the rack and tracks, its settings automatable parameters, its window showing by how much it is turning the sound down as it plays.
- The split is a Linkwitz-Riley crossover (24 dB an octave) whose two halves add back up to the sound at every frequency's level; the level is the loudest channel's, so stereo stays put.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz, split at 4 kHz with a -40 dB threshold and 10 dB of reduction: a loud 10 kHz tone came down 9.71 dB while a 500 Hz tone beside it moved 0.0013 dB; turning the whole sound down took the 500 Hz tone down 9.98 dB; a 10 kHz tone under the threshold was left alone (to 2e-8 dB). A frequency above 16 kHz or at half the rate is refused. Live, in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames, it gives the offline samples bit for bit, and the reduction its window shows (as the multiband's does for each band) is exactly what it ended the last block on. The window was rendered to an image to check its layout. It was not listened to on speech. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 186: Multiband compressor**
- Effects > Multiband Compressor: the sound split into lows, middle and highs at two crossovers (200 Hz and 2 kHz to start; the high one stays at least an octave above the low), each band compressed on its own as the Compressor does (threshold, ratio, attack, release, makeup; a 6 dB knee), then the bands added back up, with an output gain. The dialog is one panel: the crossovers and output along the top, a column a band with its frequencies named over it. Preview and presets as the other effects have.
- It is a built-in live effect too, for the rack and tracks, every setting an automatable parameter. Its window is the same panel, with each band's gain reduction shown under its name as it plays.
- The crossovers are Linkwitz-Riley (24 dB an octave), the low band passed through an all-pass matching the high split, so with nothing compressing the three bands add back up to the sound with every frequency at its level (its phase turned, as with any crossover).
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: at a ratio of 1 everywhere, sines from 40 Hz to 15 kHz, the crossovers included, kept their level to 5e-8 dB; with only the low band compressing (-40 dB, 10:1), a loud 100 Hz tone came down 19.8 dB and a 5 kHz tone beside it moved 2e-5 dB, and with only the high band, 5 kHz came down 15.3 dB and 100 Hz moved 3e-6 dB. Crossovers less than an octave apart and an output past 24 dB are refused offline; live, they are moved an octave apart. Live, in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames, it gives the offline samples bit for bit, state and presets restore every setting, and its window and its parameters follow each other. The window was rendered to an image to check its layout; the dialog and hearing it in the running app were not tried, and the reduction display was not watched while playing. The whole suite passed.

**Release 1.0.0: the first release**
- `dist/SpectraBlade-1.0.0.dmg` (10.4 MB) holds the universal app (Apple silicon and Intel) and the licence as `LICENSE.txt`. See [Installing](README.md#download-and-install); it is signed ad hoc, not notarized, so macOS asks once before opening it. Everything in [Features](README.md#features) through Milestone 185 is in it; the multiband compressor and de-esser came after.
- Checked on the development Mac (macOS 27): `Scripts/package.sh` built the app for arm64 and x86_64, signed it, and the whole suite passed with the signed app as the plug-in scan worker; the disk image's app verified (`codesign --verify --deep --strict`), reads 1.0.0 with its copyright line, carries a licence identical to `LICENSE`, and launched from the mounted image and quit with no crash report. The universal test build's Intel half also passed the whole suite under Rosetta (737 checks: the one that an Intel-only plug-in is reported as lacking Apple silicon code applies only natively). The app's own Intel half was not launched, and no Intel Mac was tried.

**Milestone 185: Quiet scroll bars everywhere**
- Every scroll bar in the app (the multitrack's, the lists', the windows') is now the quiet grey Milestone 183 gave the editor's, set once for the whole app instead of view by view.
- Checked in a capture of the window on the development Mac; the whole suite passed.

**Milestone 184: An output meter**
- The main window now meters what goes to the outputs, after the effects rack, beside the output choice: a bar a channel with a falling peak hold and a clip light (click to clear it), as the input meter has.
- The mixer's master meter and this one each read the output's peaks for themselves, so with the mixer open neither takes the other's.
- Checked: the build and the whole suite, and the meter in place in a capture of the window on the development Mac. Neither meter was watched while playing, and the tests don't drive the engine's audio graph, so the two readers are not measured.

**Milestone 183: More room, and recent files at hand**
- View > Effects Rack (Option-Cmd-E) hides the effects rack beside the editor and the multitrack, giving the audio the whole width, and shows it again; the choice is remembered. The rack keeps running while hidden.
- With no file open, the editor lists up to eight recent files that still exist under its hint, each with its folder, and a click opens one (a session opens in the multitrack), asking about unsaved changes first.
- The scroll bar under the waveform is now a quiet grey instead of a bright bar the width of the window.
- Measured by the tests: the empty editor listing the recent files that exist, leaving out a missing one, and a click on one asking to open that file. The window was captured on the development Mac with the list showing. Hiding the rack was not tried in the running app (its menus can't be driven by script there). The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 182: Opening files from Finder**
- SpectraBlade is now an editor for WAV, AIFF (`.aif`, `.aiff`, `.aifc`), FLAC, Ogg Vorbis, MP3, M4A and CAF files and for its own sessions (`.sbsession`), so Finder's Open With lists it, and a file dropped on its Dock icon, double-clicked once it is the default, or named to `open` opens in it. An audio file opens in the editor and a session in the multitrack, each after asking about unsaved changes; a file arriving while recording is refused with a note. Files named on the command line open the same way.
- Checked: launching the app with `open -a` on a WAV file opened it, and sending a second WAV file to the running app opened that one in the same window, with no second copy started. The tests check that every audio type offered has a reader, and that an open request keeps only existing files named by full path (quoted, with spaces), once each. Opening a session this way, the unsaved-changes question and the Dock were not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 181: The transport bar and the selection panel**
- The text buttons along the top are now a transport bar in the middle of the window: Go to Start, Play/Pause, Stop, Record, Loop and Go to End as drawn buttons, the time large between them and four zoom buttons (In, Out, to Selection, to Fit). Each button runs its menu command, so it does exactly what the menu item and its keys do, and dims when that command can't run. Its tooltip names the command with its keys as they are set now, so a remapped key shows at once. Play turns into Pause while playing, Record and Loop light up while on, and the time turns red while recording.
- Transport > Stop is a new command (no key by default; give it one in Keyboard Shortcuts), the one the Stop button runs.
- Along the bottom, a panel shows the Selection's and the View's start, end and duration, in both the editor and the multitrack (where the selection is the time range); clicking it opens Set Selection. The device choices sit above the file's details, and the selected clip's times move down there too.
- Save Rack and Load Rack left the top of the window; they stay in the Window menu.
- Measured by the tests: the tooltips following a remapped key and a removed one, and the panel's cells (with hours past an hour, and dashes without a selection). The window was captured on the development Mac to check the layout; the buttons were not clicked there, and none of it was heard. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 180: The keyboard shortcuts window**
- Window > Keyboard Shortcuts... is now a window of its own instead of JUCE's stock one. A search box finds commands by every word typed, in their names, menus and descriptions or their keys ("transport loop", "cmd l"), and Changed only lists just the commands whose keys differ from the defaults, those keys shown in amber.
- Choosing a command shows its description and its keys. Add Key... asks for the keys to press and says which command has them now; assigning takes them from that command. A key's button changes or removes it, and Reset puts back the command's own defaults, except any another command holds now, which it names.
- Export... writes the changes from the defaults to a `.sbkeys` file, and Import... reads one: the defaults with the file's changes over them, whatever was changed before. A file that isn't a shortcut set is refused and changes nothing. Changes are still saved as they are made.
- Measured by the tests: listing by menu then name with hidden commands left out; search words matched in names, menus, descriptions and keys; a key taken from the command that had it, in place or added; resetting with a key held elsewhere; an exported set imported over a changed fresh start giving the same keys; a file that isn't a set refused; and the window's search narrowing its list. The window was rendered to an image to check its layout; the key entry, the file choosers and using it in the running app were not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 179: Reverse Each Region**
- Effects > Reverse Each Region plays every region backwards in place, as Reverse does to the selection, in one undoable step; the audio between regions is untouched. Like Normalize and Fade Each Region, it goes through the regions in time order and skips one that overlaps a region before it, and it also skips a one-frame region (nothing to turn round), saying how many it left. A region running past the end is reversed up to the end.
- Checked by the tests: two regions turned round sample for sample with everything else unchanged, the overlapping and one-frame regions and a point skipped, and a region past the end; the whole suite passed. The menu command was not tried in the running app.

**Milestone 178: The AGPLv3 licence**
- SpectraBlade is released under the GNU AGPLv3, the licence under which it uses JUCE. `LICENSE` holds the licence's text exactly as the Free Software Foundation publishes it.
- About SpectraBlade, at the top of the application menu, shows the version, the copyright, the licence and its warranty disclaimer, and where the source code is. The app's Info.plist carries the copyright line too.
- `Scripts/package.sh` puts the licence in the disk image as `LICENSE.txt`, beside the app.
- The app stays signed ad hoc: no Developer ID, so no notarization. Installing says how to open it the first time.
- Checked: the build, the copyright line in the built Info.plist, and that the app launches and quits cleanly. The About item and the packaged disk image were not checked by hand (the app menu can't be read by script on the development Mac, and the universal release build wasn't run). The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 177: Graphic EQ**
- Effects > Graphic EQ (10 Bands) and Graphic EQ (31 Bands): a slider a band (double-click for 0 dB) and Flat, each band boosting or cutting up to 12 dB, applied to the selection or the whole file, with a "Preview on the selection" switch and presets. Both are built-in live effects for the rack and tracks too, each band's gain an automatable parameter, their windows the same sliders and presets.
- The 10 bands sit an octave apart from 31.5 Hz to 16 kHz, the 31 a third of an octave apart from 20 Hz to 20 kHz, at the standard centre frequencies. Each is a bell as wide as the spacing (a Q of 1.414 an octave, 4.318 a third), run one after another with the parametric EQ's filters; a band at 0 dB, or at or past half the rate, is left out.
- The bands overlap, so neighbours moved together add up: all ten at +6 dB give +8.8 dB at 1 kHz. The dialog says so.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: a band at +6 dB gives +6.00 dB at its centre, +1.12 dB an octave up with 10 bands, +1.29 dB at the next centre and +0.15 dB an octave up with 31; with ten bands set to a curve, sines from 50 Hz to 12 kHz change by the bands' responses added up, to 1.6e-6 dB; all at 0 dB, a gain past 12 dB and 11 bands are refused; at 32 kHz the 16 and 20 kHz bands are left out. Live, 31 bands in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames give the offline samples bit for bit, and the state and the preset values restore every gain exactly. The live window was rendered to an image to check its layout; the dialog and hearing it in the running app were not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 176: Effect presets**
- Every effect dialog (EQ Band, Compressor, Limiter, Chorus, Flanger, Phaser, Reverb, Convolution Reverb), the Parametric EQ window, and every built-in live effect's window has a presets row: a menu of the effect's saved presets (choosing one fills in its settings, and a preview playing follows it), Save... (asks for a name; an existing name is replaced) and Delete (asks first).
- A dialog and its live effect share their presets: the dialog's settings are named as the live effect's parameters, so a compressor preset saved in Effects > Compressor loads in a track's Compressor and the other way round. Settings a preset doesn't name (the Limiter dialog's look-ahead and true-peak choice, in a preset saved from the live Limiter) are left as they are. The convolution reverb's presets hold the mix, not the impulse.
- Presets are kept in Application Support/SpectraBlade/Presets, one XML file an effect, each value written with 17 significant digits so it reads back exactly. A damaged file reads as no presets.
- The EQ Band, Compressor and Limiter dialogs now run on the same dialog as the newer effects (the Limiter's and EQ Band's choices are menus in it, the Phaser's stages too); their settings, checks and results are as before. The live Limiter previewed from the dialog is made with the dialog's look-ahead.
- The built-in live effects' parameter lists show values to two decimals (one from 100, none from 1,000) instead of a float's digits, so a ratio of 4 reads 4.00, not 3.9999998.
- Measured by the tests: values such as 0.1, 1e-7, 1/3 and a float's double come back exactly from the files, and from a new store reading the same folder; names sort, take any characters, replace by name and are removed; each effect has its own file; a blank name is refused; a built-in effect's values set another to the same settings exactly; names it doesn't have are ignored and settings not named are kept; the Phaser's stages go by index; the Parametric EQ window's bands go to values and back exactly and set the live EQ. The windows were rendered to images to check their layout; the dialogs' presets rows and saving from the running app were not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 175: Convolution reverb**
- Effects > Convolution Reverb asks for an impulse response (a recording of a space's answer to a click: WAV, AIFF or FLAC, mono or stereo, up to 10 s), then for the mix, with a "Preview on the selection" switch. It is a built-in live effect for the rack and tracks too; its window shows the impulse and loads another, and its saved state holds the impulse's samples, so a session doesn't need the file.
- The impulse is resampled to the file's (or the device's) rate and scaled so the energy of its loudest channel is 1, which brings the reverb of noise out about as loud as the noise. Each channel is convolved with the impulse's channel of the same side (a mono impulse serves both).
- The convolution is uniformly partitioned, with the FFT, in blocks of 512 frames, so each frame comes out 512 frames late: the live effect reports that as latency (the rack and tracks line everything up with it), and the offline effect takes it off. Offline, the reverb rings on past the selection into the audio after it, added on top, for the impulse's length (or to the end of the file).
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: all wet, a 3,000-frame stereo impulse on noise gives each channel within 2.5e-7 of direct convolution in double precision (against a peak of 1.28); a click 100 frames into the impulse gives the input 100 frames late, to 2.1e-7; no mix gives the input bit for bit; the tail runs the impulse's length past the range and the audio after it is kept bit for bit; an impulse at 24 kHz becomes twice as many frames at 48 kHz; a silent impulse, three channels or one over 10 s is refused. Live, the effect reports 512 frames of latency and, that late, gives the offline effect's samples bit for bit; its state keeps the impulse's samples exactly; with no impulse the sound passes through, 512 frames late. A minute of stereo through a 5 s impulse took 0.86 s offline on the development Mac (so about 1.4 % of a core live). Hearing it in the running app was not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 174: Reverb**
- Effects > Reverb adds a room's reverberation to the selection or the whole file, with a "Preview on the selection" switch, and it is a built-in live effect for the rack and tracks too.
- It is a feedback delay network of SpectraBlade's own: after the pre-delay, the sound (the channels' average) is smeared by four all-pass filters and fed into eight delay lines (29.7 to 71.9 ms at 100 % size) whose outputs are mixed back into their inputs by a matrix that loses nothing. Each line loses just enough on each pass for the sound to fall 60 dB in the decay time, a low-pass in each takes the highs away faster as damping rises, and two different mixes of the lines make the left and right sides, narrowed toward their middle by the width.
- Settings: pre-delay 0 to 200 ms, decay 0.1 to 20 s, size 50 to 150 %, damping, width and mix 0 to 100 % (20 ms, 2 s, 100 %, 30 %, 100 % and 30 % to start).
- Offline, the reverb rings on past the selection into the audio after it, added on top, for the pre-delay plus the decay (or to the end of the file); the file's length doesn't change. Live, the effect reports the same tail.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: an impulse's reverb, undamped, falls 60 dB in 0.999, 1.994 and 3.976 s with the decay set to 1, 2 and 4 s; with a 100 ms pre-delay nothing comes out until 129.7 ms (the pre-delay plus the shortest line); 80 % damping leaves the tail with under 1 % of the high-frequency share it has undamped (0.012 against 2.0); no mix gives the input bit for bit; no width gives the same reverb on both sides; a 20 s decay on noise peaking at 0.5 peaks at 1.17; a range ending mid-file has its tail added after it for the pre-delay plus the decay, and the audio after that is kept bit for bit. Live, in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames, the reverb gives the offline reverb's samples bit for bit over a whole file. Hearing it in the running app was not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 173: Chorus, flanger and phaser**
- Effects > Chorus, Flanger and Phaser, each applied to the selection or the whole file, each with a "Preview on the selection" switch, and each a built-in live effect for the rack and tracks (category Modulation).
- Chorus and Flanger are one swept delay: a copy of the sound whose delay moves smoothly between the shortest delay and that plus the depth at the rate, fed back by the feedback and mixed in by the mix. The chorus starts at a 15 ms delay, 5 ms depth, 0.8 Hz and no feedback; the flanger at 1 ms, 3 ms, 0.25 Hz and 0.6 feedback. Fractional delays are read by linear interpolation, and the delay is never under one frame.
- The Phaser is a chain of 2 to 12 first-order all-pass filters, their frequency swept evenly in octaves between a low and a high frequency, with feedback around the chain, mixed with the sound: half wet, each two stages make a notch.
- The sweep starts at the top of its cycle (the shortest delay, the lowest frequency); the second channel runs it the stereo spread behind (90 degrees by default), which widens the image. Offline, the effect starts from silence at the start of the range, as the live effect does when it starts, and nothing rings on past the range.
- The offline effects and the live ones run the same per-frame cores, so played over a whole file from its start, the live effect gives the offline effect's samples.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: with no depth, all wet and no feedback, the output is the input exactly 48 frames (1 ms) late, and with no mix the input bit for bit; that delay half wet cancels a 500 Hz sine to 1.4e-13 and passes 1 kHz at its full 0.5; identical channels stay identical with no spread and part with 90 degrees; the delay output differs from the fixed delay at both ends of the sweep; feedback of +0.95 and -0.95 on noise peaking at 0.5 peaks at 1.39 and 1.27. All wet, two all-passes keep a 300 Hz sine at 0.49995 (of 0.5); half wet, two at 1 kHz cancel 1 kHz to 1.8e-8 and leave 100 Hz at 0.49; 12 stages with 0.95 feedback stay bounded. Bad settings (an odd stage count, a high frequency under the low one, feedback of 1) and empty ranges are refused, and frames outside the range are kept bit for bit. Live, in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames, each of the three gives the offline effect's samples bit for bit, the phaser's state restores exactly, and none has latency. Hearing them in the running app was not tried. The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 172: Previewing an effect on the selection**
- The Parametric EQ window, Effects > Compressor and Effects > Limiter have a "Preview on the selection" switch. While it is on, the selection (or the whole file, with none) plays over and over through the live built-in effect, ahead of the rack, and every change to a band or a field is heard as it is made. A field being typed is heard at its last valid setting.
- The preview ends when it is switched off, the dialog closes (OK, which then applies the effect offline, or Cancel) or the EQ window closes, and whenever an edit is applied, a file opened or recording started, or the multitrack view shown. Starting a preview from another window ends the first one and unticks its switch.
- The Limiter's preview holds sample peaks even with true peaks chosen (the switch says so), with the dialog's look-ahead: a new look-ahead swaps in a new limiter as it plays.
- The live Compressor's and Limiter's parameters now cover the dialogs' whole ranges (threshold to -80 dB, ratio to 100, attack from 0.01 ms, release to 5,000 ms, makeup and gain within 48 dB), and a limiter can be made with any look-ahead from 0.1 to 50 ms (5 ms in a rack or on a track).
- The effects run at the device's rate; when that differs from the file's, the preview is heard resampled, as playback always is.
- Measured by the tests: the live Compressor and Limiter take the dialogs' default, extreme and typical settings to within a millionth (the nearest their skewed ranges hold); the Limiter takes each look-ahead as given and reports as many frames of latency as Limiter uses; the engine's preview slot takes an effect, swaps it for the next and clears it, and it never becomes a rack slot or goes into the saved rack. Hearing the previews in the running app was not tried (the app's UI can't be driven here). The whole suite passed.

**Milestone 171: The compressor and limiter as live effects**
- The Compressor and Limiter are now built-in effects too, beside the Parametric EQ in the rack's and tracks' effect menus under SpectraBlade. Their windows are, for now, the generic list of their parameters.
- The live Compressor has the threshold, ratio, knee, attack, release and makeup of Effects > Compressor as automatable parameters, runs with no latency, and carries its gain on through a change of settings. The live Limiter has the gain before it, the ceiling and the release; its look-ahead is fixed at 5 ms, which it reports as latency, so the rack and the track chains line everything else up with it.
- Both now run on a small core shared with the offline effects: the compressor's gain, carried frame to frame, and the limiter's gain, given a look-ahead behind as each frame's need comes in. The offline Compressor and Limiter (sample and true peak) run through the same cores, so the live effects give the same samples: the compressor's exactly, the limiter's exactly, a look-ahead late. Every number the earlier compressor, limiter and Match Loudness tests print came out the same after the change.
- What every built-in effect shares (its description, mono or stereo, hosted parameters, a state of plain values that restores exactly) is now one base class; the Parametric EQ's saved state keeps its format.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: the live Compressor, played in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames over noise stepping between two levels, gives Compressor's samples bit for bit, restores every setting exactly from its state, and reports no latency; the live Limiter reports 240 frames of latency (5 ms), puts out that much silence first, then Limiter's samples bit for bit, none above its ceiling (which the parameter holds as -1 dB to 1e-5); on a track with a compressor after it, the chain reports the 240 frames. Opening them in the running app and hearing them live were not tried. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 170: The parametric EQ in the rack and on tracks**
- The built-in Parametric EQ is now offered wherever effects are: the master rack's add menu and each track's FX menu list it under SpectraBlade, beside the scanned plug-ins. It is in the plug-in list from the first launch, needs no scan, and comes back at once if the Plug-ins window clears the list.
- Its window is the Parametric EQ panel, its five bands over their curve, on the effect's parameters: moving a control or dragging a point sets that band's parameters, as one gesture a control, so writing a track's effect automation records it, and the other bands are left alone. A band changed from outside, by automation or a restored state, shows on the panel within a tenth of a second.
- Measured by the tests: through the window, a band's gain and Q set its parameters (4.5 dB exactly, the Q to 1e-5) with one gesture per move and every other band untouched; a band made a 120 Hz high-pass from outside shows on the panel with its gain greyed out. Opening the menus and the window in the running app, and hearing the EQ live, were not tried. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 169: The parametric EQ as a live effect (first part)**
- SpectraBlade's own effects now have a plug-in format of their own, "SpectraBlade", registered beside VST3 and Audio Unit. Racks, track chains, sessions and offline renders create, save and restore a built-in effect by its description, exactly as they do a scanned plug-in. Nothing needs scanning: the plug-in scan skips the format, and built-in effects are added to the plug-in list directly. This milestone adds the format and its first effect; the menus that offer it come next.
- The first is the Parametric EQ, live: the five bands of Effects > Parametric EQ, each band's on switch, type, frequency, gain and Q an automatable parameter, run block by block with the same Equalizer filters as the offline EQ (the filter code is now shared). A band switched off, flat, or at or past Nyquist at the current rate passes the audio unchanged, and starts afresh when it comes back. It runs mono or stereo with no latency. For now its window is the generic parameter list.
- Its state saves each band's plain values (Hz, dB, Q, type, on), and restoring sets every parameter to exactly the value saved. A float parameter holds the nearest value its range can give (1 kHz is held as 999.99994 Hz and a Q of 1 as 0.99999988), so the starting values are set the same way and every value it holds is one a saved state restores exactly.
- Measured by the tests: created from its description by the app's own format manager, flat at first; three bands played through it in blocks of 1, 7, 512 and 3,000 frames give the offline EQ's samples bit for bit over 100,000 frames of stereo noise; its state, loaded into a new one, gives every band back exactly and plays the same samples; every band off passes the audio bit for bit, and a band switched back on plays as if from the start; it runs mono, and a 15 kHz band at 22.05 kHz passes the audio unchanged; the plug-in list holds it once however often it is added, and its format has nothing to scan; in the master rack it is added by description and saved and restored with its bands. It was not played through the audio device or heard. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 168: True-peak limiting, and Match Loudness through the limiter**
- The Limiter can hold true peaks under its ceiling as well as samples: choose "True peaks (between samples)" in its dialog. Each frame's need then counts the waveform between it and its neighbours, read after 4x oversampling with the sample-rate converter, as Loudness Statistics reads true peak. The gain only approximates the waveform between samples, so the limiter measures what it made and, while the true peak is still more than 0.001 dB over, limits again from the original with the ceiling lowered by the overshoot, up to four passes.
- Match Loudness asks what to do when the true-peak ceiling would stop the gain: stop short of the target, as before, or limit to reach it. Limiting puts the full gain in front of a true-peak limiter at the ceiling (50 ms release, 5 ms look-ahead), measures the result, and raises the gain by any shortfall, up to four passes, until it is within 0.05 LU of the target; it says so if it isn't. The choice is remembered. It is one undo step.
- The limiter now keeps one float a frame while it works instead of two doubles, a quarter of the memory.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: a quarter-rate sine whose samples sit at -3 dBFS reads 0.10 dBTP (its abrupt ends ring a little above its 0 dBTP crests); sample-peak limiting at -1 dBFS leaves it bit for bit, and true-peak limiting brings it to -1.00004 dBTP as Loudness measures it (the limiter's own reading agrees to 1e-6 dB). Ten seconds of stereo noise with a click every half second, at -24.56 LUFS and -3.15 dBTP, which plain gain could raise only 2.15 dB toward -14 LUFS under a -1 dBTP ceiling, comes out at -14.02 LUFS and -1.00 dBTP with 11.14 dB of gain into the limiter, in 0.09 s. The earlier limiter and compressor checks still pass on the rewritten limiter. How either sounds was not listened to, and the dialogs were not tried by hand. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 167: Compressor and Limiter**
- Effects > Compressor asks for a threshold, a ratio, a knee, an attack, a release and a makeup gain (-20 dB, 4:1, 6 dB, 10 ms, 100 ms and 0 dB to start, all remembered while the app runs) and compresses the selection, or the whole file. Above the threshold, each ratio dB in comes out as 1 dB, eased in over the knee's width centred on the threshold. At each frame the level is the loudest channel's sample magnitude, and the gain in dB moves toward the curve's gain for it, falling with the attack time and rising with the release time (each the time to go 63 % of the way), the same on every channel so a stereo image stays put. It is one undo step.
- Effects > Limiter asks for a gain before it, a ceiling, a release and a look-ahead (0 dB, -1 dBFS, 50 ms and 5 ms to start, remembered while the app runs) and keeps every sample of the selection, or the whole file, at or below the ceiling. The gain each frame needs is the least needed anywhere in the look-ahead after it, averaged over the look-ahead, so the gain eases down before a peak and is already down when it arrives; it recovers with the release time. The ceiling holds for samples, not for the waveform between them. With no gain before it, audio more than the look-ahead before anything that needs limiting is kept bit for bit. It is one undo step.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: the compressor's curve is 0 below the knee, -3/4 of each dB over above it (4:1), and -0.9375 dB at the middle of a 10 dB knee. On steps between -40 and -6 dB, the gain is unchanged below the knee, settles at the curve's -10.5 dB to within 1e-4 dB, is 63 % of the way down after the attack time and 63 % of the way back after the release time (to 0.01 dB), and is the same on both channels; makeup adds exactly its gain, frames outside the range are bit for bit, and a ratio under 1, no attack or an empty range is refused. The limiter keeps every sample of a +6 dBFS sine and of random peaks up to +12 dBFS at or below a -1 dBFS ceiling, holds the steady sine's peaks at -1 dBFS (to 0.1 dB), and leaves the quiet audio before them bit for bit; with 30 dB of gain before it, a -40 dB stretch comes up exactly 30 dB and a -6 dB stretch sits at the -3 dBFS ceiling (to 1e-4 dB), falling only in the look-ahead before it; a ceiling above 0 or no release is refused. How either sounds was not listened to, and the dialogs were not tried by hand. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 166: Dragging the EQ's points**
- In the Parametric EQ window, each band switched on has a point on the curve at its frequency. Drag it across to set the frequency and up or down to set the gain: the gain is chosen so the curve passes under the mouse there, allowing for the other bands (a bell makes all its gain at its frequency, a shelf half), within the -24 to +24 dB the sliders allow. A high-pass or low-pass point moves in frequency only. Turning the wheel over a point raises or lowers the band's Q by a tenth a step. The pointer shows a hand over a point. The sliders follow the point as it moves.
- Fixed from Milestone 165: after any change to a band's row, a band at 0 dB read back about 1e-15 dB (the gain slider steps from its bottom end), so Apply counted it as a band that changes something. The gain is now read to the slider's 0.1 dB step, so it is exactly 0.
- Measured by the tests, with synthesised mouse events on the panel: a bell dragged to 2 kHz and +6 dB lands there (to 0.5 Hz and 0.1 dB); with another band cut 6 dB, a bell dragged to 500 Hz and +3 dB puts the curve at 3 dB there to within 0.1 dB, its own gain higher; a high shelf dragged to 6 kHz and +4 dB puts the curve at 4 dB there; a high-pass moves to 60 Hz with its gain still exactly 0; a press away from every point, or on a band switched off, moves nothing; the wheel multiplies the Q by 1.1 and back. A bell at 0 dB whose row was changed reads exactly 0 dB and is left out of Apply. Dragging was not tried by hand in the running app. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 165: Parametric EQ**
- Effects > Parametric EQ opens a window of five EQ bands over the response they make together, drawn from 20 Hz to 20 kHz on a log scale and from -24 to +24 dB, with each band's number on the curve at its frequency. Each band has an on switch, a type (bell, low shelf, high shelf, high-pass or low-pass), a frequency, a gain and a Q; a pass's gain is greyed out, since it has none. It starts as a low shelf at 100 Hz, bells at 400 Hz, 1 kHz and 3 kHz and a high shelf at 8 kHz, all at 0 dB, and keeps its settings while the app runs. Apply filters the selection, or the whole file, with the bands that change anything, in order, as one undo step, with the EQ Band filters (Milestone 164), and the window stays open; Flat sets every gain back to 0 dB. Frequencies stop below 45 % of the file's rate, so a band never reaches Nyquist.
- Measured by the tests, driving the panel's own controls: it starts flat with no bands to apply; a bell's gain and Q, a band changed to a high-pass and moved to 80 Hz, and a band switched off are each read back, the curve's response is the sum of the active bands' (to 1e-6 dB), and every change is reported once; a Q of 0.7071 stays 0.7071 when its row changes. The drawing's scales put 20 Hz and 20 kHz at the edges and map back to within 0.01 Hz, with 0 dB in the middle and anything past +24 dB held at the top; at 22.05 kHz no band can be set at or past Nyquist; a band that opens as a pass has its gain greyed out. The panel was rendered to an image and looked at, which found the curve not drawn at first (fixed). The window was not opened in the running app, and how it sounds was not listened to. The whole suite passed, the default-device test included.

**Milestone 164: EQ Band**
- Effects > EQ Band asks for a type (bell, low shelf, high shelf, high-pass or low-pass), a frequency, a gain and a Q (a 6 dB bell at 1 kHz with a Q of 1 to start, all remembered while the app runs) and filters the selection, or the whole file, on every channel. A bell boosts or cuts around the frequency, a shelf boosts or cuts everything below or above it with half the gain at the frequency, and a pass is 3 dB down at the frequency with a Q of 0.71. It is SpectraBlade's first built-in equalizer: each band is a second-order filter made from its analogue prototype by the bilinear transform and run in double precision. Before the range the filter runs over up to a second of the audio before it, so a selection starts as it would inside a longer pass; nothing rings past the range's end. Bands stack by applying it again, and each is one undo step.
- Measured by the tests at 48 kHz: the designed responses are exactly 6 dB at a 6 dB bell's frequency, 3 and -4.5 dB at the frequencies of 6 and -9 dB shelves, -3.0103 dB at a pass's corner, within 0.03 dB of the analogue -12.30 dB an octave below a high-pass's corner, and within 0.1 dB of flat far from each band.
